Abstract
. We introduce the symplectic group Sp 2 ⁡ ( A , σ ) \Sp_{2}(A,\sigma) over a noncommutative algebra A A with an anti-involution σ \sigma . We realize several classical Lie groups as Sp 2 \Sp_{2} over various noncommutative algebras, which provides new insights into their structure theory. We construct several geometric spaces, on which the groups Sp 2 ⁡ ( A , σ ) \Sp_{2}(A,\sigma) act. We introduce the space of isotropic A A -lines, which generalizes the projective line. We describe the action of Sp 2 ⁡ ( A , σ ) \Sp_{2}(A,\sigma) on isotropic A A -lines, generalize the Kashiwara-Maslov index of triples and the cross ratio of quadruples of isotropic A A -lines as invariants of this action. When the algebra A A is Hermitian or the complexification of a Hermitian algebra, we introduce the symmetric space X Sp 2 ⁡ ( A , σ ) X_{\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)} , and construct different models of this space. Applying this to classical Hermitian Lie groups of tube type (realized as Sp 2 ⁡ ( A , σ ) \Sp_{2}(A,\sigma) ) and their complexifications, we obtain different models of the symmetric space as noncommutative generalizations of models of the hyperbolic plane and of the three-dimensional hyperbolic space. We also provide a partial classification of Hermitian algebras in Appendix A.



1.
Introduction

The special linear groups
\SL_{2}(R)
, when
R
is a commutative ring, are among the most important and best studied groups in mathematics. They arise in many different contexts: for example in number theory and arithmetic geometry and in the theory of finite simple groups. In the special case when
R=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
, they are ubiquitous in Lie theory and in representation theory. Via their geometric actions, they are fundamental objects in projective, conformal and hyperbolic geometry.

The main aim of this paper is to generalize these groups to the case when
R
is a noncommutative ring. Unfortunately, for a noncommutative ring, the definition of
\SL_{2}(R)
is tricky, see
[
5
,
4
]
and the comments below.

We will thus slightly change our point of view, and notice that, for a commutative ring
R
, the special linear group
\SL_{2}(R)
is isomorphic to the symplectic group
\Sp_{2}(R)
. In this article we develop the theory of symplectic groups
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
over possibly noncommutative algebras
A
with an anti-involution
\sigma
.
We show that many aspects of the classical
\SL_{2}
theory can be developed over such noncommutative algebras
(A,\sigma)
. By realizing several classical Lie groups as
\Sp_{2}
over a noncommutative algebra, this also provides new insights into the theory of some higher dimensional classical Lie groups.

(1)

We define the group
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
and describe its action on a generalization of the projective line: the space of isotropic lines. We generalize the Kashiwara-Maslov index of triples and the cross ratio of
4
-tuples of isotropic lines.

(2)

We introduce Hermitian algebras
(A,\sigma)
(see Definition
2.13
), a special class of
\mathbb{R}
-algebras exhibiting positivity properties. In this case, we construct different models of the symmetric space associated to
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
and its complexification
\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
. We obtain generalizations of several models of the hyperbolic plane and the three-dimensional hyperbolic space, which are the symmetric spaces associated to
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
and
\SL(2,\mathbb{C})
respectively.

(3)

We show that classical Hermitian Lie groups of tube type, such as the standard real symplectic group
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})
can be naturally realized as
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
. This explains many aspects of the structure theory of Hermitian Lie groups of tube type.

(4)

As an application we describe new explicit models of the symmetric spaces associated to the complex Lie groups
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C}),\GL(2n,\mathbb{C})
, and
\OO(4n,\mathbb{C})
.

(5)

A large part of the theory outlined in (2) is completely algebraic, and works even when
\mathbb{R}
is replaced by any real closed field. This may be of interest for some applications.

In
[
5
,
4
]
two of the authors started to develop the general theory of Lie groups and Lie algebras over noncommutative rings. This is a highly non-trivial theory. Some of the difficulties can be already be seen when trying to define the group
\SL_{n}
over a noncommutative ring. It is immediate to define the group
\GL_{n}
over a noncommutative ring
R
as linear invertible maps from
R^{n}
to
R^{n}
. But there is no appropriate definition for the group
\SL_{n}
as a subgroup of
\GL_{n}
because there is no canonical choice of a determinant, even though there is rich theory of quasi-determinants
[
10
]
.

Our approach is motivated by the notion of
\Theta
-positivity, a generalization of Lusztig’s total positivity in the context of real Lie groups which are not necessarily split, introduced in
[
12
,
11
,
22
]
. Hermitian Lie groups of tube type admit a
\Theta
-positive structure.
1
1

1

In fact, for Hermitian Lie groups the
\Theta
-positive structure is related to the theory of Lie semigroups.

The combinatorics of this
\Theta
-positive structure is governed by the Weyl group of a root system of type
A_{1}
, making Hermitian Lie groups of tube type “look like”
\SL_{2}
theories over noncommutative algebras. Here we make this precise.

Our description of the different models of the symmetric spaces for most of the Hermitian groups of tube type shows that the geometry of these symmetric space is a noncommutative version of the classical planar hyperbolic geometry. This is not surprising: for example a well-known model of the symmetric space of
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})
is the Siegel upper half space, and the formulae describing this model look like the formulae of planar hyperbolic geometry, with matrices replacing numbers. The theory developed here puts this observation into a more general theoretical framework.

Even more interesting is our description of different models of the symmetric spaces of the complexifications of the groups discussed above, such as
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C})
,
\GL(2n,\mathbb{C})
,
\OO(4n,\mathbb{C})
. Here we show that the geometry of their symmetric spaces is a noncommutative version of the classical
3
-dimensional hyperbolic geometry. This fact is more surprising.

We believe that this new point of view can be helpful and leads to new applications. An immediate application is that the construction of noncommutative coordinates for symplectic representations of fundamental groups of punctured surfaces developed in
[
2
]
and its relation to noncommutative cluster algebras introduced in
[
6
]
generalizes to representations into classical Hermitian Lie groups of tube type. This will appear in a forthcoming work.

We now describe the results of the paper in more detail.

1.1.
Symplectic groups over involutive algebras

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a field and consider an associative, possibly noncommutative unital finite-dimensional
\mathbb{K}
-algebra
A
. Let
\sigma:A\to A
be an anti-involution, i.e. a
\mathbb{K}
-linear map with
\sigma(ab)=\sigma(b)\sigma(a)
for all
a,b\in A
and
\sigma^{2}=\Id
. We denote the set of fixed points of
\sigma
by
A^{\sigma}
.
Then we introduce the non-degenerate sesquilinear form
\omega:A^{2}\times A^{2}\to A
, defined by

\omega(x,y):=\sigma(x)^{T}\Omega y\,,

for all
x,y\in A^{2}
and
\Omega=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}
.
The symplectic group
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
is defined as

\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)=\Aut(\omega)=\{f\in\Aut(V)\mid\forall x,y\in V:\omega(f(x),f(y))=\omega(x,y)\}\,.

Similar to the classical case, we look at natural spaces, on which the group
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts. The first such space is our noncommutative analog of the projective line. The group does not act transitively on the space of all lines in
A^{2}
, hence we restrict our attention to the lines that are isotropic for
\omega
:

\mathbb{P}(\Is(\omega)):=\{xA\mid\omega(x,x)=0,\,x\in A^{2}\text{ regular}\}\,,

where an element
x\in A^{2}
is called regular if there exists
y\in A^{2}
such that
x,y
form a basis. Two isotropic lines
xA
and
yA
are said to be transverse if
x,y
form a basis.

The action of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
on
\mathbb{P}(\Is(\omega))
has some of the features we know from projective or Möbius geometry, e.g. the action on quadruples of points preserve an invariant that generalizes the cross-ratio:

Theorem 1.1
.

(1)

The group
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts transitively on
\mathbb{P}(\Is(\omega))

(2)

The group
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts transitively on the set of pairs of transverse isotropic lines.

(3)

The
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
-orbits in the space of pairwise transverse triples of isotropic lines are in one-to-one correspondence with the orbits of the action of the group of invertible elements
A^{\times}
on the set of invertible fixed points
(A^{\sigma})^{\times}
by

\begin{matrix}A^{\times}\times(A^{\sigma})^{\times}&\mapsto&(A^{\sigma})^{\times}\\
(a,b)&\mapsto&ab\sigma(a).\end{matrix}

This gives rise to a generalization of the Kashiwara-Maslov index.

(4)

The

\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
-orbits in the space of pairwise transverse 4-tuples of isotropic lines are in one-to-one correspondence with the orbits of the action of the group of invertible elements
A^{\times}
on the set
A_{0}
of products of invertible fixed points
(A^{\sigma})^{\times}
,
A_{0}=\{bb^{\prime}\,\mid\,b,b^{\prime}\in(A^{\sigma})^{\times}\}
:

\begin{matrix}A^{\times}\times A_{0}&\mapsto&A_{0}\\
(a,b)&\mapsto&aba^{-1}.\end{matrix}

The conjugacy class in
A_{0}
is thus a noncommutative cross-ratio of a four-tuple of pairwise transverse isotropic lines.

Of particular interest to us is a special class of involutive algebras that we call Hermitian algebras. We describe the geometry of the symmetric spaces for
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
, when
(A,\sigma)
is Hermitian or the complexification of an Hermitian algebra.

Hermitian algebras are algebras
(A,\sigma)
over a real closed field
\mathbb{K}
where the following holds: if
a,b\in A^{\sigma}
, then
a^{2}+b^{2}=0
if and only if
a=b=0
. Readers not familiar with real closed fields can simply think about the case where
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
, since this is the most interesting case for our applications.
Involutive algebras are closely related to Jordan algebras, and Hermitian algebras give rise to formally real Jordan algebras see Section
2.3
. A key feature of Hermitian algebras is the existence of a proper convex cone
A^{\sigma}_{+}\subset A^{\sigma}
.

In the main body of the paper, we focus on semisimple Hermitian algebras. So, for the rest of the introduction, whenever we say Hermitian algebra, we mean semisimple Hermitian algebra. In Appendix
A
we consider a more general class of rings with anti-involution, which we call pre-Hermitian, investigate the theory of general Hermitian algebras, and classify semisimple Hermitian algebras.

There are different ways in which we can construct new Hermitian algebras out of old ones: Matrix algebras over Hermitian algebras are Hermitian, and complexifications (and quaternionifications) of Hermitian algebras provide new Hermitian algebras.
Given a Hermitian algebra
(A,\sigma)
we consider the complexification
A_{\mathbb{C}}
of
A
and extend the involution complex linearly to an involution
\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}
and complex anti-linearly to an involution
\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Then
(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
is an Hermitian algebra, but
(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
is not, see Section
2
.

For Hermitian algebras and their complexifications we can refine Theorem
1.1
.
For this we make the following definition: we say that a triple of pairwise transverse isotropic lines is positive, if its
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
-orbits corresponds to an element in
A^{\sigma}_{+}
by the identification given in Theorem
1.1
.

Theorem 1.2
.

If
(A,\sigma)
is Hermitian, then
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts transitively on the space of positive triples of pairwise transverse isotropic lines.

Theorem 1.3
.

Assume
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
and let
(A,\sigma)
be a Hermitian algebra or its complexification. Then the space of isotropic lines is compact.

1.2.
Symmetric spaces associated to
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)

Recall that the symmetric space associated to
\Sp_{2}(\mathbb{R})
is
X_{\Sp_{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\Sp_{2}(\mathbb{R})/\UU_{1}(\mathbb{C})
, where
\UU_{1}(\mathbb{C})
is a maximal compact subgroup. It admits many explicit models. It can be realized as the space of compatible complex structures on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, this is the space

\mathfrak{C}:=\left\{J\text{ complex structure on $\mathbb{R}^{2}$}\mid\omega(J\cdot,\cdot)\text{ is an inner product}\right\}.
We call this the complex structure model.
To obtain the upper hemisphere model, we complexify
\mathbb{R}^{2}
to
\mathbb{C}^{2}
and extend the symplectic form
\omega
complex linearly to a symplectic from
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
. Similarly we extend a complex structure
J
complex linearly to
\mathbb{C}^{2}
. Over
\mathbb{C}
now the complex structure is a diagonalizable endomorphism and we can associate to every compatible complex structure its
+i
-eigenspace in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
. This provides an embedding of
\mathfrak{C}
into the space of isotropic lines
\mathbb{P}(\Is(\omega_{\mathbb{C}}))=\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
, whose image we denote by
\mathfrak{P}
and call the projective model.

The Poincaré disk model
D=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid\overline{z}z<1\}
and the upper half plane model
\mathfrak{U}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid\Imm(z)>0\}
arise from projective model
\mathfrak{P}
naturally by picking specific affine charts in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
. We call the disk model the precompact model.
Taking its closure in
\mathbb{C}
, or the closure of
\mathfrak{P}
in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
, we obtain a compactification of the symmetric space, in which the space of (isotropic) lines in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
arises as the boundary.

We show that these constructions can be appropriately generalized to symplectic groups
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
over real Hermitian algebras
(A,\sigma)
.
In order to introduce the maximal compact subgroup we observe that
given
(A,\sigma)
the algebra
\Mat_{n}(A)
of
n\times n
-matrices over
A
can be endowed with the anti-involution
\sigma^{T}
, which applies
\sigma
to each entry and then the transpose. When
(A,\sigma)
is a Hermitian algebra, then
(\Mat_{n}(A),\sigma^{T})
is Hermitian as well. In that case the subgroup
\UU_{n}(A,\sigma)=\{M\in\Mat_{n}(A)\,\mid\,\sigma(M)^{T}M=\Id_{n}\}
is compact.
This allows us to define the maximal compact subgroups

\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)=\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)\cap\UU_{2}(A,\sigma)\subset\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
, and
\KSp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})=\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})\cap\UU_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})\subset\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
.
We can thus consider the symmetric space associated to
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
,

X_{\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)}=\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)/\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)
, and the symmetric space

X_{\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})}=\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})/\KSp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
.
We develop different explicit models for the symmetric spaces

X_{\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)}
and
X_{\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})}
.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
(A,\sigma)
be a real Hermitian algebra and
(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
its complexification. Then the symmetric space
X_{\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)}=\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)/\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)
admits

(1)

a complex structure model

\mathfrak{C}:=\left\{J\text{ complex structure on $A^{2}$}\mid\omega(J\cdot,\cdot)\text{ is a $\sigma$-inner product}\right\}.

(2)

a projective model
\mathfrak{P}:=\{vA_{\mathbb{C}}\mid v\in\Is(\omega_{\mathbb{C}}),\;i\omega_{\mathbb{C}}(\overline{v},v)\in(A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\bar{\sigma}})_{+}\},
where
(A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\bar{\sigma}})_{+}
is the proper convex cone in
(A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\bar{\sigma}})
.

(3)

a precompact model
\mathring{D}(A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}):=\{c\in A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}}\mid 1-\bar{c}c\in(A^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}})_{+}\}
.

(4)

an upper half-space model
\mathfrak{U}:=\{z\in A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}\mid\Imm(z)\in A^{\sigma}_{+}\}
, where
A^{\sigma}_{+}
is the proper convex cone in
A^{\sigma}
.

Furthermore, there are natural maps between these different models.
The closure of the precompact model gives rise to a compactification of
X_{\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)}
in which the space of isotropic lines
\mathbb{P}(\Is(\omega))
appears as the closed
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
-orbit.

For the group
\Sp_{2}(\mathbb{C})
there is a similar construction of explicit models of the symmetric space, which is less well known.
The symplectic group
\Sp_{2}(\mathbb{C})
acts on
(\mathbb{C}^{2},\omega)
, which we view as the complexification of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
A quaternionic structure on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
is an additive map
J:\mathbb{C}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}^{2}
such that
J^{2}=-\Id
and such that
J(xa)=J(x)\bar{a}
for all
x\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
,
a\in\mathbb{C}
.
Then
\Sp_{2}(\mathbb{C})
acts naturally on the space of compatible quaternionic structures on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, which is

\mathfrak{C}:=\{J\text{ quaternionic structure on $\mathbb{C}^{2}$}\mid\omega(J\cdot,\cdot)\text{ is a Hermitian inner product}\}.
We call
\mathfrak{C}
the quaternionic structure model of
X_{\Sp_{2}(\mathbb{C})}
.
Analogously to the above construction, where we complexified
\mathbb{R}^{2}
we can now quaternionify
\mathbb{C}^{2}
and extend the symplectic form
\omega
as well as the quaternionic structure. One has to be a bit more cautious working with the quaternions
\mathbb{H}
, but
one gets a projective model
\mathfrak{P}\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
, a precompact model
D=\{x+yi+zj\in\mathbb{H}\mid 1-(x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2})>0\}\subset\mathbb{H}
and a upper half space model
\mathfrak{U}=\{x+iy+kj\in\mathbb{H}\mid x,y,z\in\mathbb{R},\;z>0\}\subset\mathbb{H}
.
These quaternionic models for the three-dimensional hyperbolic space are for example described in
[
1
,
19
]
.

We prove that an analogous construction can be made for the complexified symplectic groups
\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
, using quaternionic extensions
A_{\mathbb{H}}
of the Hermitian algebra
A
with two appropriate quaternionic extensions
\sigma_{0}
and
\sigma_{1}
of the anti-involution
\sigma
. In particular, we obtain

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
be the complexification of a real Hermitian algebra
(A,\sigma)
. Then the symmetric space
X_{\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})}=\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})/\KSp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
admits

(1)

a quaternionic structure model

\mathfrak{C}:=\{J\text{ quaternionic structure on $A_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}$}\mid\omega(J\cdot,\cdot)\text{ is a
4
$\bar{\sigma}$-inner product}\}

(2)

a projective model
\mathfrak{P}\subset\Is(\omega_{\mathbb{H}})
,

(3)

a precompact model
\mathring{D}(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}},\sigma_{1}):=\{c\in A^{\sigma_{0}}_{\mathbb{H}}\mid 1-\sigma_{1}(c)c\in(A^{\sigma_{1}}_{\mathbb{H}})_{+}\}
, where
(A^{\sigma_{1}}_{\mathbb{H}})_{+}
is the proper convex cone in the quaternionification of
A
.

(4)

an upper half-space model
\mathfrak{U}:=\{z_{0}+z_{1}j\in A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}}\mid z_{0}\in A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}},\;z_{1}\in(A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\bar{\sigma}})_{+}\}
.

Furthermore, there are natural maps between these different models.
The closure of the precompact model gives rise to a compactification of
X_{\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})}
in which the space of isotropic lines
\mathbb{P}(\Is(\omega_{\mathbb{C}}))
appears as the closed
\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
-orbit.

1.3.
Hermitian Lie groups of tube type

As we mentioned before, Hermitian Lie groups of tube type look a lot like groups of type
\Sp_{2}
over real involutive noncommutative algebras. We illustrate it on the following example:

We consider the
\mathbb{R}
-algebra
A=\Mat(n,\mathbb{R})
of real
n\times n
-matrices. A natural anti-involution on
A
is the transposition that we denote by
\sigma
. Then by definition,
M\in\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
if and only if
\sigma(M)\Omega M=M^{T}\Omega M-\Omega
where
\Omega=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}
. Here we used the standard identification of
\Mat(2n,\mathbb{R})
and
\Mat_{2}(\Mat(n,\mathbb{R}))
. That means,
M
is a symplectic
2n\times 2n
-matrix and the group
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
agrees with
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})
.

With the theory of symplectic groups over noncommutative involutive algebras, we can make the correspondence between Hermitian Lie groups of tube type and symplectic groups over semisimple Hermitian algebras very precise, at least for classical Hermitian Lie group of tube type.

Theorem 1.6
.

The following classical Hermitian Lie groups of tube type can be realized as symplectic groups over Hermitian algebras:

(1)

\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})=\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
, where
A=\Mat(n,\mathbb{R})
is the algebra of
n\times n
matrices over
\mathbb{R}
with involution
\sigma:A\to A
given by
\sigma(r)=r^{T}
.

(2)

\UU(n,n)=\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
, where
A=\Mat(n,\mathbb{C})
is the algebra of
n\times n
matrices over
\mathbb{C}
with involution
\sigma:A\to A
given by
\sigma(r)=\bar{r}^{T}
.

(3)

\SO^{*}(4n)=\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
, where
A=\Mat(n,\mathbb{H})
is the algebra of
n\times n
matrices over
\mathbb{H}
with involution
\sigma:A\to A
given by
\sigma:A\to A
is
\sigma(r)=\bar{r}^{T}=\bar{r_{1}}^{T}-\bar{r_{2}}^{T}j
for
r=r_{1}+r_{2}j
and
r_{1},r_{2}\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{C})
.

Remark 1.7
.

The other classical Hermitian Lie group of tube type,
\SO_{0}(2,n)
, cannot be realized in the same way as
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
. However its double cover
\Spin_{0}(2,n)
) can be realized as
\Sp_{2}
over a slightly more complicated object
B_{n}
,
which is a Jordan subalgebra of an appropriate Clifford algebra
(A,\sigma)
. We will discuss this in a forthcoming article.

The exceptional Hermitian Lie group of tube type cannot be realized as
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
, see Remark
3.27
.

There are other Lie groups that can be realized as
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
over involutive algebras
(A,\sigma)
that are not Hermitian, see Section
3.4.1
.

For these Hermitian Lie groups of tube type, the above models for the symmetric space
X_{\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)}
give the well known explicit models of Hermitian symmetric spaces. The precompact model is the bounded symmetric domain model, and the space of isotropic lines identifies with the Shilov boundary of the bounded symmetric domain model.

In view of Theorem
1.6
and Theorem
1.4
classical Hermitian symmetric spaces of tube type can be thought of as hyperbolic planes over noncommutative involutive algebra
(A,\sigma)
.

As a Corollary of Theorem
1.6
we can realize the complexifications of Hermitian Lie groups of tube type
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
as symplectic groups over complexifications of Hermitian algebras.

Theorem 1.8
.

The following complex Lie groups can be realized as symplectic groups over involutive algebras:

(1)

\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C})=\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
, where
A=\Mat(n,\mathbb{R})
is the algebra of
n\times n
matrices over
\mathbb{R}
with involution
\sigma:A\to A
given by
\sigma(r)=r^{T}
.

(2)

\GL(2n,\mathbb{C})=\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
, where
A=\Mat(n,\mathbb{C})
is the algebra of
n\times n
matrices over
\mathbb{C}
with involution
\sigma:A\to A
given by
\sigma(r)=\bar{r}^{T}
.

(3)

\OO(4n,\mathbb{C})=\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
, where
A=\Mat(n,\mathbb{H})
is the algebra of
n\times n
matrices over
\mathbb{H}
with involution
\sigma:A\to A
given by
\sigma:A\to A
is
\sigma(r)=\bar{r}^{T}=\bar{r}_{1}^{T}-r_{2}^{T}j
for
r=r_{1}+r_{2}j
and
r_{1},r_{2}\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{C})
.

In particular, Theorem
1.5
applies and we obtain explicit new realizations of models for the symmetric spaces associated to
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C})
,
\GL(2n,\mathbb{C})
, and
\OO(4n,\mathbb{C})
.
We illustrate here the upper half-space model for
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C})
and refer the reader to Section
7
for an explicit description of all the models for
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C})
,
\GL(2n,\mathbb{C})
, and
\OO(4n,\mathbb{C})
.

For
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C})
, we consider again
A=\Mat(n,\mathbb{R})
with the anti-involution
\sigma
given by transposition. Then
A_{\mathbb{C}}=\Mat(n,\mathbb{C})
,
\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}
is the transposition and
\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})=\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C})
. The upper half-space model of the symmetric space of
\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
is then (see Theorem
1.5
(4))

\mathfrak{U}=\{z_{1}+z_{2}j\mid z_{1}\in A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}},\;z_{2}\in(A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}})_{+}\}=\{z_{1}+z_{2}j\mid z_{1}\in\Sym(n,\mathbb{C}),\;z_{2}\in\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{C})\}.

Structure of the paper:
In Section
2
we discuss algebras
A
with anti-involution, and introduce the notion of Hermitian algebra. A more general notion of pre-Hermitian algebras and a classification of Hermitian algebras is given in Appendix
A
.
In Section
3
we introduce the symplectic groups
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
over noncommutative rings and give examples of classical Lie groups that are realized as
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
. In Section
4
we investigate the action of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
on the space of isotropic lines.
We construct the various models of the symmetric space
X_{\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)}
in Section
5
, and of the symmetric space
X_{\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})}
in Section
6
.
In Section
7
we spell this construction out for the complexifications of the Hermitian Lie groups of tube type, giving explicit models for the symmetric spaces of
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C})
,
\GL(2n,\mathbb{C})
, and
\OO(4n,\mathbb{C})
.

2.
Algebras with anti-involution

In this section we consider algebras with anti-involutions and introduce basic notions which play an important role throughout the paper.

2.1.
Main definitions

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a field and
A
a unital associative possibly noncommutative finite-dimensional
\mathbb{K}
-algebra. If
\mathbb{K}
is a topological field,
A
has a well defined topology.

Definition 2.1
.

An
anti-involution
on
A
is a
\mathbb{K}
-linear map
\sigma\colon A\to A
such that

•

\sigma(ab)=\sigma(b)\sigma(a)
;

•

\sigma^{2}=\Id
.

An
involutive
\mathbb{K}
-algebra
is a pair
(A,\sigma)
, where
A
is a
\mathbb{K}
-algebra and
\sigma
is an anti-involution on
A
.

Remark 2.2
.

Sometimes in the literature the maps that satisfy Definition
2.1
are called just involutions. We add the prefix “anti” in order to emphasise that they exchange the factors.

Remark 2.3
.

Notice, since the algebra
A
is unital, we always have the canonical copy of
\mathbb{K}
in
A
, namely
\mathbb{K}\cdot 1
where
1
is the unit of
A
. We will always identify
\mathbb{K}\cdot 1
with
\mathbb{K}
. Moreover, since
\sigma
is linear, for all
k\in\mathbb{K}
,
\sigma(k\cdot 1)=k\sigma(1)=k\cdot 1
, i.e.
\sigma
preserves
\mathbb{K}\cdot 1
.

Definition 2.4
.

An element
a\in A
is called
\sigma
-normal
if
\sigma(a)a=a\sigma(a)
. An element
a\in A
is called
\sigma
-symmetric
if
\sigma(a)=a
. An element
a\in A
is called
\sigma
-anti-symmetric
if
\sigma(a)=-a
. We denote

A^{\sigma}:=\Fix_{A}(\sigma)=\{a\in A\mid\sigma(a)=a\},

A^{-\sigma}:=\Fix_{A}(\sigma)=\{a\in A\mid\sigma(a)=-a\}.

Example 2.5
.

Typical examples of involutive algebras are given by matrix algebras: If
\mathbb{K}
is a field, then the space of
\mathbb{K}
-valued
n\times n
-matrices
\Mat(n,\mathbb{K})
with anti-involution given by the transposition is an involutive algebra.

If additionally
\mathbb{K}
admits an involution
\delta\colon\mathbb{K}\to\mathbb{K}
, then
\Mat(n,\mathbb{K})
with the anti-involution
\delta\circ\sigma
is again an example of an involutive
\mathbb{K}
-algebra.

Semisimple involutive finite-dimensional algebras over perfect fields with the additional assumption
A^{\sigma}=\mathbb{K}
can be classified. To state the classification, we need the following well-known definitions:

Definition 2.6
.

A field
\mathbb{K}
is called
perfect
if every irreducible polynomial over
\mathbb{K}
has distinct roots in its splitting field.

Remark 2.7
.

Notice that every field of characteristic zero is perfect.

Definition 2.8
.

A non-zero algebra is called
simple
if it has no two-sided ideal besides the zero ideal and itself. A finite-dimensional algebra is called
semisimple
if it is isomorphic to a product of simple algebras.

Theorem 2.9
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a perfect field with
\ch(\mathbb{K})\neq 2
and
(A,\sigma)
be a semisimple finite-dimensional involutive algebra over
\mathbb{K}
such that
A^{\sigma}=\mathbb{K}
. Then either
A
is a division algebra over
\mathbb{K}
of dimension
1,2
or
4
, or
A=\mathbb{K}\oplus\mathbb{K}
and the anti-involution exchanges the two summands.

A proof of this statement will be given in Proposition
A.31
and Theorem
A.32
.

We denote by
A^{\times}
the group of all invertible elements of
A
. If
V\subset A
is a vector subspace, we denote

V^{\times}=A^{\times}\cap V\,,

the set of invertible elements in
V
. We consider the following map
\theta
, that will play the role of a norm on
A

\begin{matrix}\theta\colon&A&\to&A^{\sigma}\\
&a&\mapsto&\sigma(a)a\end{matrix}

Definition 2.10
.

The closed subgroup

U_{(A,\sigma)}=\{a\in A^{\times}\mid\theta(a)=1\}

of
A^{\times}
is called the
unitary group
of
A
. The Lie algebra of
U_{(A,\sigma)}
agrees with
A^{-\sigma}
.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
(A,\sigma)
be an algebra with an anti-involution. We define the set of
\sigma
-positive elements by

A^{\sigma}_{+}:=\left\{\ \sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}^{2}\ \,\middle|\,\ a_{i}\in(A^{\sigma})^{\times},\;k\in\mathbb{N}\ \right\},

and the set of
\sigma
-non-negative elements by

A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}:=\left\{\ \sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}^{2}\ \,\middle|\,\ a_{i}\in A^{\sigma},\;k\in\mathbb{N}\ \right\}.

Remark 2.12
.

If
(A,\sigma)
is an algebra over an ordered field
\mathbb{K}
, then
A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}
is the topological closure of
A^{\sigma}_{+}
. This follows from Corollary
2.30
.

We will now give the definition of a Hermitian algebra that will be a key notion in this paper. Before this, we remind the well-known definition of a real closed field. A
real closed
field is an ordered field
\mathbb{K}
which is of index two in its algebraic closures. Equivalently, it is an ordered field
\mathbb{K}
in which every positive element of
\mathbb{K}
is a square and every odd degree polynomial has at least one zero.

Definition 2.13
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a real closed field. A unital associative
\mathbb{K}
-algebra with an anti-involution
(A,\sigma)
is called
Hermitian
if for all
x,y\in A^{\sigma}
,
x^{2}+y^{2}=0
implies
x=y=0
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a real closed field. Slightly abusing our notation, we denote the algebraic closure of
\mathbb{K}
by
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Since
\mathbb{K}
is real closed,
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathbb{K}[i]
where
i
is a square root of
-1
called also the
imaginary unit
of
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
. The field
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
is called the
complexification of
\mathbb{K}
. The involution
\bar{\cdot}\colon\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
defined as
\bar{1}=1
,
\bar{i}=-i
is called the
complex conjugation
. With this (anti-)involution,
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
becomes a Hermitian algebra over
\mathbb{K}
of dimension
2
. If
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
then as usual we denote
\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

For a real closed field
\mathbb{K}
there exist a generalized quaternion skew-field
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{H}}
defined by the following presentation:

\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{H}}=\{x_{0}+x_{1}i+x_{2}j+x_{3}k\mid i^{2}=j^{2}=-1,~ij=-ji=k\}.

If
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
, then
\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{H}}
is the classical quaternion skew-field that we will denote as usual by
\mathbb{H}
.
We call
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{H}}
the
quaternionic extension
of
\mathbb{K}
. With the anti-involution
\bar{\cdot}
defined as
\bar{i}=-i
,
\bar{j}=-j
and called the
quaternionic conjugation
,
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{H}}
becomes a Hermitian algebra over
\mathbb{K}
of dimension
4
. The elements
i,~j,~k
are called imaginary units of
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{H}}
.

Also the generalized octonionic algebra
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{O}}
can be defined over any real closed field
\mathbb{K}
. This is the 8-dimendional non-associative noncommutative algebra that is generated (as a
\mathbb{K}
-vector space) by the unit
1\in\mathbb{K}
and seven imaginary units
i,~j,~k,~E,~I,~J,~K
. The multiplication rule of the imaginary units is the same as in the classical octonionic
\mathbb{O}
algebra over
\mathbb{R}

Remark 2.14
.

Although in the notation of complexification and quaternionification we use symbols
\mathbb{C}
,
\mathbb{H}
and
\mathbb{O}
, we do not assume that
\mathbb{C}
is a subfield of
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
,
\mathbb{H}
is a subalgebra of
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{H}}
and
\mathbb{O}
is a subalgebra of
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{O}}
. In the Appendix
A
, we consider other division algebras over fields (not necessarily over real closed fields). In the notation of Appendix
A
,
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{H}}=\mathbb{H}_{-1,-1}
and
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{O}}=\mathbb{O}_{-1,-1}
.

Example 2.15
.

The main examples of real closed fields are
\mathbb{R}
and the subfield of algebraic reals. An example of real closed field not contained into
\mathbb{R}
is given by the field of Puiseux series with real coefficients. Another example is the field of hyperreals. The field
\mathbb{Q}
is not real closed, since for example
2
is not a square.

Remark 2.16
.

Every real closed field has characteristic zero and contains the field of algebraic reals as a subfield.

For real closed fields the following theorem holds:

Theorem 2.17

(Generalized Frobenius Theorem)
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a real closed field. Every associative division algebra over
\mathbb{K}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{K}
,
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
or
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{H}}
. The only non-associative noncommutative division algebra over
\mathbb{K}
is
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{O}}
.

In case
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
this theorem is the classical Frobenius theorem. The general version of this theorem follows from the classical one using the Tarski–Seidenberg transfer principle (see
[
7
, Proposition 5.2.3]
).

Proposition 2.18
.

A subalgebra of a Hermitian algebra that is closed under the anti-involution is Hermitian.

Example 2.19
.

The following matrix algebras provide examples of Hermitian
\mathbb{R}
-algebras:
\Mat(n,\mathbb{R})
with the transposition and
\Mat(n,\mathbb{C})
with the transposition composed with the complex conjugation.

Semisimple finite-dimensional algebras over real closed fields with the additional property
A^{\sigma}=\mathbb{K}
are all Hermitian and can be classified as follows:

Corollary 2.20
.

Let
(A,\sigma)
be a semisimple finite-dimensional algebra over
\mathbb{K}
with
A^{\sigma}=\mathbb{K}
. Then one of the following cases holds:

(1)

A=\mathbb{K}
, the anti-involution
\sigma
acts trivially;

(2)

A=\mathbb{K}\oplus\mathbb{K}
, the anti-involution
\sigma
permutes copies of
\mathbb{K}
, i.e.
\sigma(x,y)=(y,x)
;

(3)

A=\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
, the anti-involution
\sigma
acts by complex conjugation;

(4)

A=\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{H}}
, the anti-involution
\sigma
acts by quaternionic conjugation.

In every case
(A,\sigma)
is Hermitian. The bilinear form

\begin{matrix}\beta\colon&A^{-\sigma}\times A^{-\sigma}&\to&\mathbb{K}\\
&(a,a^{\prime})&\mapsto&-aa^{\prime}-a^{\prime}a\end{matrix}

is an inner product on
A^{-\sigma}
.

This follows from the Theorems
2.9
and
2.17
.

Remark 2.21
.

The property to be Hermitian can also be defined in the same way for algebras with an anti-involution over any field. In this paper, we are discussing only Hermitian algebras over real closed fields. In the Appendix
A
, a larger class of Hermitian and pre-Hermitian rings is considered and classified.

Remark 2.22
.

Notice, if
\mathbb{K}
is a real closed field and
(A,\sigma)
is an involutive algebra over
\mathbb{K}
, then the addition and the multiplication in
A
as well as the anti-involution
\sigma
are semi-algebraic maps and the spaces
A
,
A^{\sigma}
,
A^{-\sigma}
,
A^{\sigma}_{+}
,
A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}
,
U_{(A,\sigma)}
are semi-algebraic sets.

Remark 2.23
.

If
\mathbb{K}
is a real closed field and
(A,\sigma)
is a Hermitian algebra over
\mathbb{K}
, then
A^{\sigma}_{+}=\{a^{2}\mid a\in(A^{\sigma})^{\times}\}
and
A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}=\{a^{2}\mid a\in A^{\sigma}\}
are proper convex cones in
A^{\sigma}
. For a proof in case
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
see
[
9
, Theorem III.2.1]
. In the general case, the proof is identical.

2.2.
Some properties

Proposition 2.24
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a field and
A
be a unital associative
\mathbb{K}
-algebra of finite dimension
n
over
\mathbb{K}
. Then
A
is isomorphic to a subalgebra of
\Mat(n,\mathbb{K})
.

Proof.

For every
x\in A
, consider the linear map
L_{x}:A\rightarrow A
defined by

L_{x}(y)=xy\,.

Consider the map

A\ni x\rightarrow L_{x}\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{K})\,.

This is an injective
\mathbb{K}
-algebra homomorphism (there is no kernel because
A
is unital).
∎

Definition 2.25
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a field,
A
be a unital associative finite dimensional
\mathbb{K}
-algebra and
a\in A
. An element
\lambda\in\mathbb{K}
is called
eigenvalue
of
a
if
a-\lambda\cdot 1
is not invertible.

Remark 2.26
.

Notice that this definition of an eigenvalue works for non-associative algebras as well.

Proposition 2.27
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a field and
A
be a unital associative finite dimensional
\mathbb{K}
-algebra. Then
a\in A
is not invertible if and only if
a
is a zero divisor in
\mathbb{K}[a]
.

Proof.

Since
A
is of finite dimension over
\mathbb{K}
, for every
a\in A
there exists
k\geq 0
such that set
\{1,a,a^{2},\dots,a^{k-1}\}
is linearly independent over
\mathbb{K}
and
\{1,a,a^{2},\dots,a^{k}\}
is linearly dependent over
\mathbb{K}
. This means that there exists a non-trivial polynomial
p\in\mathbb{K}[X]
such that
\deg(p)=k
and
p(a)=0
. Without lost of generality, assume
p=\sum_{i=0}^{k}c_{i}X^{i}
,
c_{i}\in\mathbb{K}
and
c_{k}=1
.

First we note that if
a
is non-invertible, then
p(0)=c_{0}=0
. Indeed, assume
p(0)=c_{0}\in\mathbb{K}^{\times}
. Then

p(a)=\sum_{i=0}^{k}c_{i}a^{i}=(\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}c_{i}a^{i-1})a+c_{0}=0.

This means that
a
is invertible and
a^{-1}=-c_{0}^{-1}(\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}c_{i}a^{i-1})
.

Now, since
c_{0}=0
, we write
p(X)=q(X)X
where
q\in\mathbb{K}[X]
is a non-trivial polynomial of degree
k-1
. Hence,
q(a)\neq 0
and we obtain
q(a)a=0
. That means,
a
is a zero divisor in
\mathbb{K}[a]

∎

Proposition 2.28
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a field and
A
be a unital associative finite dimensional
\mathbb{K}
-algebra. Let
a\in A
and
p\in\mathbb{K}[X]
such that
p(a)=0
, then
p(\lambda)=0
for all eigenvalues
\lambda
of
a
. In particular, there are only finitely many eigenvalues of
a
.

Proof.

Let
\lambda
be an eigenvalue of
a
. Therefore, by previous Proposition, there exists
0\neq b\in\mathbb{K}[a]
with
(a-\lambda\cdot 1)b=0
. In particular,
ab=\lambda b
and by induction
a^{i}b=a^{i-1}(ab)=a^{i-1}(\lambda b)=\lambda^{i}b
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

If
p\in\mathbb{K}[X]
be such that
p(a)=0
, then
p(a)b=0
. Since
a^{i}b=\lambda^{i}b
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we obtain
0=p(a)b=p(\lambda)b
. But
p(\lambda)\in\mathbb{K}
and
b\neq 0
, therefore,
p(\lambda)=0
.
∎

Definition 2.29
.

We will say that a topological field has a
non-trivial topology
if its topology is not discrete nor indiscrete.

The condition that the topology on a field is non-trivial is remarkably strong. Such topologies are automatically
T_{0}
, because the intersection of all the neighborhoods of
0
must be an ideal, hence it is
\{0\}
. This implies that the topology is Hausdorff, from the properties of topological groups. Fields with non-trivial topologies must be infinite: in fact, for finite
\mathbb{K}
, every one-point set is closed means that every one-point set is open as complement of finite-point (closed) set, i.e. the topology is discrete. An important example is an ordered field endowed with the order topology.

Proposition 2.30
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a topological field with a non-trivial topology. Let
A
be a unital associative finite-dimensional
\mathbb{K}
-algebra, and let
V
be a vector subspace of
A
that contains at least one invertible element. Then
V^{\times}
is an open dense subset of
V
.

Proof.

Notice that
x\in A
is invertible in
A
if and only if the linear map
L_{x}
is surjective, which holds if and only if
L_{x}
is invertible in
\Mat(n,\mathbb{K})
. Since
0\in\mathbb{K}
is closed, the set of all invertible elements
\GL(n,\mathbb{K})=\Mat(n,\mathbb{K})\setminus\det^{-1}(0)\subset\Mat(n,\mathbb{K})
is open. Hence, the intersection of
\GL(n,\mathbb{K})
with
V
is open in
V
as well.

To see the density, consider an invertible element
u\in V
. The set
u^{-1}\cdot V
is again a vector subspace of
A
and contains the unit
1
. It suffices to show that density holds for
u^{-1}\cdot V
. If
x\in u^{-1}\cdot V
is not invertible, consider
y_{\epsilon}=x+\epsilon\cdot 1
. By the previous proposition, there are only finitely many values of
\epsilon
such that
y_{\epsilon}
is not invertible, namely the opposites of eigenvalues of
x
.

Since
\mathbb{K}
is infinite and non-discrete with closed points, there exists a (maybe punctured) neighborhood
U
of
0\in\mathbb{K}
that does not contain eigenvalues of
x
. Now every open neighborhood of
x
intersects non-trivially the set
\{x+\epsilon\cdot 1\mid\epsilon\in U\}
. To see this, we choose a basis
(e_{1},\dots,e_{n})
of
u^{-1}\cdot V
containing
1=e_{1}
. Then by definition of the product topology for every neighborhood
W
of
x
there exist a neighborhood
W^{\prime}
of
0\in\mathbb{K}
such that
W
contains the following open neighborhood
\{x+\lambda_{1}e_{1}+\dots+\lambda_{n}e_{n}\mid\lambda_{i}\in W^{\prime}\}
. Since
U\cap W^{\prime}\neq\varnothing
,
W\cap\{x+\epsilon\cdot 1\mid\epsilon\in U\}\neq\varnothing
. This shows that
V^{\times}
is dense in
V
.
∎

Corollary 2.31
.

For algebras
(A,\sigma)
satisfying Corollary
2.30
,

A^{\times}
is open and dense in
A
,
(A^{\sigma})^{\times}
is open and dense in
A^{\sigma}
.

When
\mathbb{K}
is an ordered field, we will assume that it is endowed with the order topology, which is always non-trivial. Recall that a subset
C\subset V
of a
\mathbb{K}
-vector space is a
cone
if it is stable under multiplication by a strictly positive scalar. A cone is
convex
if it is stable by sums of its elements.
If
C
is a convex cone, its closure
\overline{C}
and its interior
\mathring{C}
are still convex cones. The set of the opposites of the elements of
C
, denoted by
-C
, is still a convex cone.
A convex cone
C
is
proper
if

\overline{C}\cap-\overline{C}=\{0\}\,.

If
C
is a cone in some algebra over
\mathbb{K}
, then we denote by
C^{\times}
the subset of all invertible elements of
C
.

Similarly to Proposition
2.30
can be proven:

Proposition 2.32
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be an ordered field. Let
A
be a unital associative finite-dimensional
\mathbb{K}
-algebra, and let
C
be a convex cone of
A
that contains at least one invertible element. Then
C^{\times}
is an open dense subcone of
C
.

Remark 2.33
.

As we have seen in Remark
2.23
, for Hermitian algebras,
A^{\sigma}_{+}
and
A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}
are proper convex cones in
A^{\sigma}
. Proposition
2.32
implies that
A^{\sigma}_{+}
is open and dense in
A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}
.

2.3.
Jordan algebras and spectral theorem

In this section we recall the definitions of a Jordan algebra and formally real Jordan algebra and prove some properties of formally real Jordan algebra that we will need later. We also show how Jordan algebras arise from associative algebras.

Definition 2.34
.

Let
(V,\circ)
be an possibly non-associative unital algebra over some field
\mathbb{K}
.
(V,\circ)
said to be a
Jordan algebra
if for all
x,y\in V

(1)

x\circ y=y\circ x
;

(2)

(x\circ y)\circ(x\circ x)=x\circ(y\circ(x\circ x))
(Jordan identity).

A Jordan algebra
(V,\circ)
over a real closed field is called
formally real
if for all
x,y\in V
,
x^{2}+y^{2}=0
implies
x,y=0
.

The following is immediate:

Proposition 2.35
.

Let
(A,\sigma)
be a finite dimensional algebra with anti-involution, then the following hold:

•

The algebra
(A^{\sigma},\circ)
is a Jordan algebra where

x\circ y=\frac{xy+yx}{2}.

•

If
(A,\sigma)
is Hermitian, the Jordan algebra
(A^{\sigma},\circ)
is formally real.

Jordan algebras that arise form associative algebras as in
2.35
are called
special Jordan algebras
. All other Jordan algebras are called
exceptional Jordan algebras
.

In this section we establish some properties of formally real Jordan algebras. We do not assume that they are necessarily special.

Let
(V,\circ)
be a formally real Jordan algebra over a real closed field
\mathbb{K}
. We use the following notation:

V_{+}=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{2}\,\middle|\,a_{i}\in V^{\times},\;n\in\mathbb{N}\right\},

V_{\geq 0}=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{2}\,\middle|\,a_{i}\in V,\;n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}.

Definition 2.36
.

An element
c\in V
is called an
idempotent
if
c^{2}=c
.

•

Two idempotents
c,c^{\prime}\in V
are called
orthogonal
if
c\circ c^{\prime}=0
.

•

A tuple
(c_{1},\dots,c_{k})
of pairwise orthogonal idempotents is called a
complete orthogonal system of idempotents
if
c_{1}+\dots+c_{k}=1
.

Remark 2.37
.

Note that every idempotent
c
satisfies
c\in V_{\geq 0}
. Moreover, if
c\neq 1
, then
c\in V_{\geq 0}\setminus V_{+}

Theorem 2.38

(Spectral theorem, first version)
.

Let
V
be a formally real Jordan algebra over a real closed field
\mathbb{K}
. For every
b\in V
, there exist a unique
k\in\mathbb{N}
, unique elements
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{k}\in\mathbb{K}
, all distinct, and a unique complete system of orthogonal idempotents
c_{1},\dots,c_{k}\in\mathbb{K}[b]\subseteq V
such that

b=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}c_{i}.

We call this the
spectral decomposition
of
b
.

For a proof of this theorem for real Jordan algebras see
[
9
, Theorem III.1.1]
. A proof for general real closed fields is identical.

We collect some direct consequences of Theorem
2.38

Corollary 2.39
.

Let
(V,\circ)
be a formally real Jordan algebra and
c_{1},c_{2}\in V
be two orthogonal idempotents. There exists an
a\in V
such that
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{K}[a]
. If
V=A^{\sigma}
for an associative Hermitian algebra
(A,\sigma)
, then
c_{1}c_{2}=0
.

Proof.

We consider
a:=c_{1}-c_{2}
. This is the spectral decomposition of
a
. By the spectral theorem,
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{K}[a]
. Let
V=A^{\sigma}
for an Hermitian algebra. Since
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{K}[a]
, they commute with respect to the product of
A
. Therefore,
0=c_{1}\circ c_{2}=c_{1}c_{2}
.
∎

Corollary 2.40
.

For
b\in V_{\geq 0}
, we have
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{k}\geq 0
. If
b\in V_{+}
, we have moreover,
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{k}>0
.

Corollary 2.41
.

The set of all invertible elements
V^{\times}
of
V
consists of elements such that in the spectral decomposition we have
\lambda_{i}\neq 0
for all
i
. If all
\lambda_{i}\neq 0
, then

\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}c_{i}\right)^{-1}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}^{-1}c_{i}.

Corollary 2.42
.

For every (continuous/smooth) function
f\colon\mathbb{K}\to\mathbb{K}
, the (continuous/smooth) map

\hat{f}\colon V\to V

can be defined: if

b=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}c_{i},

then

\hat{f}(b):=\sum_{i=1}^{k}f(\lambda_{i})c_{i}.

This map is well defined because the spectral decomposition is unique. Analogously, for any function
f\colon\mathbb{K}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{K}
or
f\colon\mathbb{K}_{+}\to\mathbb{K}
,
\hat{f}\colon V_{\geq 0}\to V
resp.
\hat{f}\colon V_{+}\to V
can be defined.

In particular, for every
b\in V_{\geq 0}
, the element
b^{t}\in V_{\geq 0}
for
t\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}
is well-defined (If
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
it is even well-defined for all
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
). This definition is compatible with integer powers of elements.

Proposition 2.43
.

•

The space
V_{+}
is
semi-algebraically contractible. The set
\{1\}
is a semi-algebraic deformation retract of
V_{+}
. In particular,
V_{+}
is semi-algebraically connected.

•

The space
V_{\geq 0}
is
semi-algebraically contractible. The set
\{1\}
is a semi-algebraic deformation retract of
V_{\geq 0}
. In particular,
V_{\geq 0}
is semi-algebraically connected.

Proof.

We consider the following continuous semi-algebraic map:
H(t,a)=t\cdot a+(1-t)\cdot 1
where
t\in[0,1]=\{s\in\mathbb{K}\mid 0\leq s\leq 1\}\subset\mathbb{K}
,
a\in V_{+}
. Since,
H(t,a)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\lambda_{i}t+(1-t))c_{i}
where
(c_{i})
is a complete system of orthogonal idempotents in the spectral decomposition of
a
. Since all
\lambda_{i}
are positive, the convex combination
\lambda_{i}t+(1-t)
is positive for all
t\in[0,1]
. Therefore,
H(t,a)\in V_{+}
for all
t\in[0,1]
,
a\in V_{+}
. Moreover,
H(1,a)=a
and
H(0,a)=1
, i.e.
H
is a contraction and
\{1\}
is a semi-algebraic deformation retract of
V_{+}
.

The same contraction works also for
V_{\geq 0}
.
∎

Corollary 2.44
.

Let
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
.
The space
V_{+}
is (non-semi-algebraically) homeomorphic to
V
. In particular,
V_{+}
is open in
V
and contractible, and
\{1\}\subset V_{+}
is a deformation retract of
V_{+}
.

Proof.

The map
f(t)=\log(t)
gives a homeomorphism between
V_{+}
and
V
. The rest follows from
2.43
.
∎

We now state the second version of the spectral theorem. For this we need to give some additional definitions:

Definition 2.45
.

An idempotent
0\neq c\in V
is called
primitive
if it cannot be written as a sum of two orthogonal non-zero idempotents.

•

A complete orthogonal system of primitive idempotents
(c_{1},\dots,c_{k})
is called a
Jordan frame
.

•

The maximal number of elements in a Jordan frame is called the
rank
of a Jordan algebra.

Theorem 2.46

(Spectral theorem, second version)
.

Let
V
be a formally real Jordan algebra over a real closed field
\mathbb{K}
. Suppose that
V
has rank
n
. For every
b\in V
there exist a Jordan frame
(e_{1},\dots,e_{n})
and a unique
n
-tuple of elements
(\lambda_{1}(b),\dots,\lambda_{n}(b))\in\mathbb{K}^{n}
such that
\lambda_{1}(b)\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{n}(b)
and

b=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}(b)e_{i}.

The elements
\lambda_{1}(b),\dots,\lambda_{n}(b)\in\mathbb{K}
(with their multiplicities) are called the
eigenvalues
of
b
are uniquely determined by
b
. In particular, they do not depend (up to permutations) on the Jordan frame
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\in V
.

For a proof of this theorem for real Jordan algebras see
[
9
, Theorem III.1.2]
. The proof for a general real closed field is identical.

Remark 2.47
.

A Jordan frame
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\in V
associated to the element
b\in V
by Theorem
2.46
is in general not unique and elements
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\notin\mathbb{K}[b]
, in contrast to the complete system of orthogonal idempotents from Theorem
2.38
.

Remark 2.48
.

In Definition
2.25
we already defined eigenvalues for elements of an associative algebras. This definition works also for non-associative algebras (see Remark
2.26
). If
V
is a formally real Jordan algebra, then the eigenvalues defined by the spectral theorem agree with the eigenvalues defined in Definition
2.25
.

Definition 2.49
.

The
signature
of an element
b\in V
is a pair
(p,q)
with
p,q\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
such that
b
has
p
positive eigenvalues and
q
negative eigenvalues in the spectral decomposition defined in
2.46
.

Remark 2.50
.

Notice that for an element
b
of signature
(p,q)
,
p+q\leq n
, moreover,
p+q=n
if and only if
b
is invertible.

Definition 2.51
.

Let
b\in V
and
\lambda_{1}(b),\dots,\lambda_{n}(b)
are all its eigenvalues with multiplicities. We define
the
eigenvalue map
:

\begin{matrix}\lambda\colon&V&\mapsto&\mathbb{K}^{n}\\
&b&\to&(\lambda_{1}(b),\dots,\lambda_{n}(b)),\end{matrix}

and the
trace
and the
determinant
maps:

\tr(b):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}(b),\;\det(b):=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}(b).

Remark 2.52
.

Since the eigenvalues defined by the spectral theorem and eigenvalues defined in
2.25
agree, the eigenvalue map is semi-algebraic. The eigenvalue map is continuous. For a proof when
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
see
[
3
, Corollary 24]
. For a general real closed field the proof is identical.

Corollary 2.53
.

The function

\begin{matrix}\beta\colon&V\times V&\to&\mathbb{K}\\
&(b_{1},b_{2})&\mapsto&\tr\left(b_{1}\circ b_{2}\right)\end{matrix}

is an inner product on
V
, where
V
is intended as a
\mathbb{K}
-vector space.

Corollary 2.54
.

•

The set
V_{+}
is an open and closed proper convex cone in
V^{\times}
. In particular,
V_{+}
is a semi-algebraic connected component of
V^{\times}
.

•

The set
V_{\geq 0}
is a closed proper convex cone in
V
.

Proof.

From
2.23
we already know that
V_{+}
and
V_{\geq 0}
are proper convex cones in
V^{\times}
and in
V
respectively.

The set
V_{+}=\lambda^{-1}(\mathbb{K}_{+}^{n})
is open and closed in
V^{\times}=\lambda^{-1}((\mathbb{K}\setminus\{0\})^{n})
. In particular,
V_{+}
is a semi-algebraic connected component of
V
because by
2.43

V_{+}
is semi-algebraically connected.

The set
V_{+}=\lambda^{-1}(\mathbb{K}^{n}_{\geq 0})
is closed in
V
.
∎

2.4.
Classification of simple formally real Jordan algebras

In this section, we recall the well-known classification of simple formally real Jordan algebras over
\mathbb{R}
and generalize it to all real closed fields.

Theorem 2.55
.

Every simple formally real Jordan algebra over a real closed field
\mathbb{K}
is isomorphic to one of the following Jordan algebras:

(1)

Symmetric real matrices
(\Sym(n,\mathbb{K}),\circ)
where
a\circ b=\frac{ab+ba}{2}
for
a,b\in\Sym(n,\mathbb{K})
;

(2)

Hermitian complex matrices
(\Herm(n,\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}),\circ)
where
a\circ b=\frac{ab+ba}{2}
for
a,b\in\Herm(n,\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}})
;

(3)

Hermitian quaternionic matrices
(\Herm(n,\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{H}}),\circ)
where
a\circ b=\frac{ab+ba}{2}
for
a,b\in\Herm(n,\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{H}})
;

(4)

(B_{n},\circ)
where
B_{n}=\Span_{\mathbb{K}}(1,x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
with
1\circ x_{i}=x_{i}
,
x_{i}\circ x_{j}=0
for
i\neq j
,
x_{i}\circ x_{i}=1
for all
i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
;

(5)

Hermitian octonionic
3\times 3
matrices
(\Herm(3,\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{O}}),\circ)
where
a\circ b=\frac{ab+ba}{2}
for
a,b\in\Herm(3,\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{O}})
.

In case
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
this theorem agrees with the classification of formally real Jordan algebras over
\mathbb{R}
that is proven in
[
13
,
9
]
. The general version of this classification follows from the classification in case
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
using the Tarski–Seidenberg transfer principle (see
[
7
, Proposition 5.2.3]
).

The Jordan algebras (1)–(3) from
2.55
can be seen as
A^{\sigma}
for certain simple algebras
(A,\sigma)
. Let
\mathbb{K}
be a real closed field.

(1)

Symmetric real matrices:
A=\Mat(n,\mathbb{K})
,
\sigma(r):=r^{T}
is an algebra with an anti-involution. Then
A^{\sigma}=\Sym(n,\mathbb{K})
space of all symmetric matrices. The algebra
(A,\sigma)
is Hermitian with
A^{\sigma}_{+}=\Sym^{+}(n,\mathbb{K})
real symmetric positive definite matrices.

(2)

Hermitian complex matrices:
A=\Mat(n,\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}})
,
\bar{\sigma}(r):=\bar{r}^{T}
is a Hermitian algebra with
A^{\bar{\sigma}}=\Herm(n,\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}})
complex Hermitian matrices and
A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{+}=\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}})
complex Hermitian positive definite matrices.

(3)

Hermitian quaternionic matrices:
A=\Mat(n,\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{H}})
,
\bar{\sigma}(r):=\bar{r}^{T}
, is a Hermitian algebra with
A^{\bar{\sigma}}=\Herm(n,\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{H}})
quaternionic Hermitian matrices and
A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{+}=\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{H}})
quaternionic Hermitian positive definite matrices.

There is another anti-involution on
A=\Mat(n,\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{H}})
, namely
\sigma_{1}(r):=\sigma(r_{1})+\bar{\sigma}(r_{2})j
where
r_{1},r_{2}\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}})
. This algebra
(A,\sigma_{1})
is not Hermitian.

Fact 2.56

(
[
13
, Corollary 2.8.5]
)
.

The Jordan algebra
(\Herm(3,\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{O}}),\circ)
is exceptional. This means that there is no associative real algebra
A
that contains
\Herm(3,\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{O}})
as a Jordan subalgebra.

Remark 2.57
.

The Jordan algebra
(B_{n},\circ)
can be embedded as a Jordan subalgebra into the even Clifford algebra
\Cl_{even}(1,n)
for some appropriate anti-involution
\sigma
, but
\Cl(1,n)^{\sigma}
is strictly bigger than
B_{n}
for
n>2
. In the case
n=2
,
\Cl(1,2)
is isomorphic to
\Mat(2,\mathbb{K})
as an associative algebra, and
B_{2}
is isomorphic to
\Sym(2,n)
as a Jordan algebra.

2.5.
Positivity of the norm

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a real closed field and
(A,\sigma)
be a Hermitian semisimple algebra over
\mathbb{K}
. The goal of this subsection is to show that
\theta(a)=\sigma(a)a\in A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}
for all
a\in A
. To do it, first, we prove some technical propositions.

Proposition 2.58
.

Let
(A,\sigma)
be a Hermitian semisimple algebra, then
-a^{2}\in A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}
for every
a\in A^{-\sigma}
.

Proof.

We prove it by induction on the rank of
A
. If its rank is one, then the statement follows from the Corollary
2.20
.

Let now rank of
A
be equal
n>1
and we assume that for every Hermitian semisimple algebra
A^{\prime}
of rank smaller then
n
, the Proposition holds. Take
a\in A^{-\sigma}
, then
a^{2}\in A^{\sigma}
. We consider a spectral decomposition:

a^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}e_{i}

where
(e_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
is a Jordan frame. We denote

E:=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}e_{i},\;\;e:=e_{n}.

Then
E+e=1
,
eE=Ee=0
, and algebras
(EAE,\sigma)
and
(eAe,\sigma)
are Hermitian semisimple subalgebras of
A
of rank strictly smaller then rank of
A
. Therefore,

\displaystyle a^{2}=

\displaystyle(EaE+Eae+eaE+eae)^{2}=EaEaE+eaeae+(eaEae+EaeaE)

\displaystyle+(Eaeae+EaEae)+(eaeaE+eaEaE).

Since
EaEaE=(EaE)^{2}
and
EaE\in(EAE)^{-\sigma}
, we have
-EaEaE\in(EAE)^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}\subseteq A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}
. The same holds for
-eaeae
. Further,

-(eaEae+EaeaE)=(exE-Exe)^{2}\in A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}

because
exE-Exe\in A^{\sigma}
.
Finally,

Eaeae+EaEae=Ea(E+e)ae=Ea^{2}e=0

beacsue of the spectral decomposition of
a^{2}
and
eE=Ee=0
. The same holds for
eaeaE+eaEaE
.
∎

Proposition 2.59
.

Let
(A,\sigma)
be a Hermitian semisimple algebra and
x\in A
satisfy the property
\sigma(x)x=0
, then
x=0
.

Proof.

We denote:
A_{0}:=\{x\in A\mid\sigma(x)x=0\}
.
First, note that
\sigma(x)x=0
if and only if
x\sigma(x)=0
. Indeed, let
\sigma(x)x=0
, then
x\sigma(x)\in A^{\sigma}
and
(x\sigma(x))^{2}=x\sigma(x)x\sigma(x)=0
. Therefore, since
A
is Hermitian,
x\sigma(x)=0
.

Further, write
x=x^{s}+x^{a}
where
x^{s}\in A^{\sigma}
,
x^{a}\in A^{-\sigma}
. Then

\sigma(x)x=(x^{s})^{2}-(x^{a})^{2}+x^{s}x^{a}-x^{a}x^{s}=0=x\sigma(x)=(x^{s})^{2}-(x^{a})^{2}-x^{s}x^{a}+x^{a}x^{s}.

Therefore,
x^{s}x^{a}-x^{a}x^{s}=0
and
(x^{s})^{2}-(x^{a})^{2}=0
. Since
(x^{s})^{2},-(x^{a})^{2}\in A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}
,
x^{s}=0
and
(x^{a})^{2}=0
. Therefore
1+x^{a}=1+x
is invertible and
(1+x)^{-1}=1-x
.

Let
r\in A
, consider
y=xr
. Then
\sigma(y)y=\sigma(r)\sigma(x)xr=0
, i.e.
y\in A_{0}
. Further, take
r^{\prime}\in A
and consider
z=r^{\prime}y
, then
z\sigma(z)=0
. Therefore, as we have seen,
\sigma(z)z=0
and
z\in A_{0}
. That means, for every
r,r^{\prime}\in A
,
1+r^{\prime}xr\in A^{\times}
, i.e.
x\in J(A)
where
J(A)
is the Jacobson radical of
A
(see Definition
A.1
). Because
A
is semisimple,
J(A)=\{0\}
.
∎

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a real closed field. As before we denote by
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
the algebraic closure of
\mathbb{K}
.

Proposition 2.60
.

Let
(A,\sigma)
be a Hermitian semisimple algebra, then the complexified algebra
(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
, where
A_{\mathbb{C}}=A\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}
the complex anti-linear extension of
\sigma
(i.e.
\bar{\sigma}(x+iy)=\sigma(x)-i\sigma(y)
for
x,y\in A
), is Hermitian as an involutive algebra over
\mathbb{K}
ans semisimple.

Proof.

The algebra
(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
is clearly unital and associative. We check now that, if
x^{2}+y^{2}=0
, then
x=y=0\in A^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

For
x\in A^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}}
we write
x=x_{1}+ix_{2}
,
x_{1},x_{2}\in A
. Since

\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}(x)=\sigma(x_{1})-i\sigma(x_{2})
,

x_{1}\in A^{\sigma}
,
x_{2}\in A^{-\sigma}
. Further,

x^{2}+y^{2}=x_{1}^{2}+y_{1}^{2}-x_{2}^{2}-y_{2}^{2}+i(x_{1}y_{2}+y_{1}x_{2}+x_{2}y_{1}+y_{2}x_{1})=0.

Since
x_{1}^{2},y_{1}^{2},-x_{2}^{2},-y_{2}^{2}\in A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}
, we have
x_{1}^{2}=y_{1}^{2}=-x_{2}^{2}=-y_{2}^{2}=0
. Therefore, by Proposition
2.59
,
x_{1}=x_{2}=y_{1}=y_{2}=0
, i.e.
x=y=0
.

Assume now that
A_{\mathbb{C}}
is not semisimple, i.e. the Jacobson radical
J(A_{\mathbb{C}})
of
A_{\mathbb{C}}
is non-trivial. Let
0\neq x\in J(A_{\mathbb{C}})
, then by
A.9
,
\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}(x)=-x
and
x^{2}=0
. Therefore,
\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}(ix)=ix
, i.e.
ix\in A^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
(ix)^{2}=0
. This contradicts to the property to be Hermitian for
(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
.
∎

Proposition 2.61
.

Let
Y
be a finite dimensional
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
-algebra,
V\subseteq Y
be a
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
-vector subspace. Then
V^{\times}
is semi-algebraically connected.

Proof.

If
V^{\times}=\varnothing
then
V^{\times}
is semi-algebraically connected.

Assume now that
1\in V^{\times}\neq\varnothing
. As we have seen in the Proposition
2.24
,
Y
can be embedded as a subalgebra into
\Mat(r,\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}})
for some
r\in\mathbb{N}
. We identify
Y
as a subalgebra of
\Mat(r,\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}})
.

Let
a\in V^{\times}\subseteq\GL(n,\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}})
. We consider

S^{1}_{\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}}:=\{x+iy\in\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}\mid x,y\in\mathbb{K},x^{2}+y^{2}=1\}.

Notice, that
S^{1}_{\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}}
is a semi-algebraically connected set. In particular, it has infinitely many points.
Since
a
has only finitely many eigenvalues, and
0
is not one of them, there is a point
z\in S^{1}_{\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}}\subset\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
such that the
\mathbb{K}
-line
\{tz\mid t\in\mathbb{K}\}
in
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
through the origin containing
z
does not intersect any of the eigenvalues of
a
. Now, consider the
\mathbb{K}
-path
f(t)=at+z(1-t)\Id
,
t\in[0,1]=\{s\in\mathbb{K}\mid 0\leq s\leq 1\}\subset\mathbb{K}
. It lies completely in
V
because it is a
\mathbb{C}
-vector space. This has determinant
0
if and only if
z(t-1)
is an eigenvalue of
at
, which happens if and only if
z(1-t)/t
is an eigenvalue of
a
(this does not work when
t=0
, but then it is clear that the determinant is non-zero). By construction, it is not the case for any
t\in[0,1]\subset\mathbb{K}
, so this defines a path form
a
to
z\Id
.
Now, there is a path in
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
not passing through
0
from
z
to
1
, and, since
\{z\Id\mid z\in S^{1}_{\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}}\}\subset V^{\times}
, this gives rise to a semi-algebraic path in
A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
from
z\Id
to
\Id
, and so concatenating these two paths, we get a path from
a
to
\Id
that lies in
V^{\times}
, showing that
V^{\times}
is semi-algebraically connected.

Finally, if
1\notin V^{\times}
but there exists
v\in V^{\times}
, then
V^{\times}
is semi-algebraically connected if and only if
(v^{-1}V)^{\times}=v^{-1}(V^{\times})
is semi-algebraically connected. Moreover,
v^{-1}V
is also a
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
-vector space and
1\in(v^{-1}V)^{\times}
. So
(v^{-1}V)^{\times}
is semi-algebraically connected and, therefore,
V^{\times}
is semi-algebraically connected as well.
∎

Now we are ready to prove the following

Theorem 2.62
.

Let
(A,\sigma)
be a Hermitian semisimple algebra, then
\sigma(a)a\in A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}
for all
a\in A
. In particular,
A^{\sigma}_{+}=\theta(A^{\times})
and
A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}=\theta(A)
.

Proof.

First, we embed
(A,\sigma)
into its complexification
(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
that is Hermitian as well. Let
a\in A^{\times}\subset A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
. Since
A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
is semi-algebraically connected, we take a semi-algebraic path
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to A^{\times}_{\mathbb{C}}
such that
\gamma(0)=a
,
\gamma(1)=1\in A
. The eigenvalue map:

\lambda(\sigma(\gamma(t))\gamma(t))
takes values in the semi-algebraic connected component of
(1,\dots,1)\in(\mathbb{K}\setminus\{0\})^{n}
, i.e. in
\mathbb{K}_{+}^{n}
. In particular,
\lambda(\sigma(a)a)=\lambda(\sigma(\gamma(0))\gamma(0))
is positive meaning that
a\in A^{\sigma}_{+}\subset(A^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}})_{+}
.

Since
A
is the closure of
A^{\times}
, the eigenvalue map takes values in the closure of the connected component of
(1,\dots,1)\in(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})^{n}
, i.e. all eigenvalues of
\sigma(a)a
are non-negative for
a\in A
.
∎

Remark 2.63
.

We conjecture that the Theorem
2.62
holds also for non-semisimple Hermitian algebras.

Definition 2.64
.

Let
V
be a formally real Jordan algebra over a real closed field
\mathbb{K}
and
V_{+}
be the cone of positive elements of
V
. The group of all linear transformations of
V
(as
\mathbb{K}
-vector space) which preserve
V_{+}
is called
structure group of
V
and denoted by
G(V)
.

Corollary 2.65
.

The group
A^{\times}
acts on
A^{\sigma}
preserving
A^{\sigma}_{+}
in the following way:

\begin{matrix}\psi\colon&A^{\times}\times A^{\sigma}&\mapsto&A^{\sigma}\\
&(a,b)&\to&\sigma(a)ba.\end{matrix}

In other words,
\psi(A^{\times})
is a subgroup of
G(A^{\sigma})
. Moreover, the restriction of this action to
A^{\sigma}_{+}
is transitive.

2.6.
Polar decomposition

Let
(A,\sigma)
to be a Hermitian algebra. From now on, we always assume the algebra
A
to be semisimple.

Theorem 2.66

(Polar decomposition, first version)
.

The map

\begin{matrix}\pol\colon&U_{(A,\sigma)}\times A^{\sigma}_{+}&\to&A^{\times}\\
&(u,b)&\mapsto&ub\end{matrix}

is a semi-algebraic homeomorphism. In particular, for every
g\in A^{\times}
there exist unique
b\in A^{\sigma}_{+}
and
u\in U_{(A,\sigma)}
such that
g=ub
.

Proof.

The map
\pol
is well-defined because
A^{\sigma}_{+}\subseteq A^{\times}
. First, we prove the surjectivity. Take
g\in A^{\times}
, then
\sigma(g)g\in A^{\sigma}_{+}
by Proposition
2.62
. Take
b:=(\sigma(g)g)^{\frac{1}{2}}
, then
u:=g(\sigma(g)g)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\in U_{(A,\sigma)}
. Indeed,

\sigma(u)u=(\sigma(g)g)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sigma(g)g(\sigma(g)g)^{-\frac{1}{2}}=1.

Now, we prove the injectivity. Let
g=ub=u^{\prime}b^{\prime}
where
u,u^{\prime}\in U_{(A,\sigma)}
,
b,b^{\prime}\in A^{\sigma}_{+}
. Then
\sigma(g)g=(b^{\prime})^{2}=b^{2}\in A^{\sigma}_{+}
. We take the spectral decompositions of
b
and
b^{\prime}
:

b=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}c_{i},\;b^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}\lambda_{i}^{\prime}c_{i}^{\prime}

where all
k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\lambda_{i},\lambda_{i}^{\prime}>0
and
\{c_{i}\}
,
\{c^{\prime}_{i}\}
are complete orthogonal systems of idempotents of
A^{\sigma}
. Then

b^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}^{2}c_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}(\lambda_{i}^{\prime})^{2}c_{i}^{\prime}=(b^{\prime})^{2}.

Because of the uniqueness of the spectral decomposition,
k=k^{\prime}
and, up to reordering, all
\lambda_{i}^{2}=(\lambda_{i}^{\prime})^{2}
,
c_{i}=c_{i}^{\prime}
. But all
\lambda_{i}>0
, therefore,
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{i}^{\prime}
, i.e.
b=b^{\prime}
and
u=gb^{-1}=g(b^{\prime})^{-1}=u^{\prime}
.

Finally, by definition,
\pol
is continuous. Moreover,

\pol^{-1}(g)=(g(\sigma(g)g))^{-\frac{1}{2}},(\sigma(g)g))^{\frac{1}{2}})

is continuous as well and contains only semi-algebraic operations. Therefore,
\pol
is a semi-algebraic homeomorphism.
∎

Corollary 2.67
.

The map

\begin{matrix}A^{\sigma}_{+}\times U_{(A,\sigma)}&\to&A^{\times}\\
(b,u)&\mapsto&bu\end{matrix}

is a semi-algebraic homeomorphism. In particular, for every
g\in A^{\times}
there exist unique
b\in A^{\sigma}_{+}
and
u\in U_{(A,\sigma)}
such that
g=bu
.

Corollary 2.68
.

The group
U_{(A,\sigma)}<A^{\times}
is a semi-algebraic deformation retract of
A^{\times}
.

Proof.

This follows from the polar decomposition
2.66
and from
2.43
.
∎

Proposition 2.69
.

The following
\mathbb{K}
-bilinear form
\beta\colon A\times A\to\mathbb{K}
:

\beta(a_{1},a_{2}):=\tr\left(\frac{\sigma(a_{1})a_{2}+\sigma(a_{2})a_{1}}{2}\right)

on
A
is positive definite. The group
U_{(A,\sigma)}
acts on
A
by the left and right multiplication preserving
\beta
.

Proof.

The map
\beta
is
\mathbb{K}
-bilinear on
A
. Moreover,
\beta(a,a)=\tr(\sigma(a)a)\geq 0
for all
a\in A
because
\sigma(a)a\in A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}
.

If
\beta(a,a)=0
, then all eigenvalues of
\sigma(a)a
are zero, i.e.
\sigma(a)a=0
and, therefore, because of semisimplicity of
A
we have
a=0
(see Proposition
2.59
).

The form
\beta
is invariant under the left and right action by multiplication of
U_{(A,\sigma)}
because the trace is invariant under conjugation.
∎

Corollary 2.70
.

The adjoint action of
U_{(A,\sigma)}
on
A
preserves
\beta
. In particular, the Lie algebra
A^{-\sigma}
of
U_{(A,\sigma)}
is compact.

Corollary 2.71
.

With respect to the norm induced by the inner product
\beta
on
A
, the following subsets of
A
are closed, bounded and semi-algebraic:

•

the group
U_{(A,\sigma)}
;

•

D(A,\sigma):=\{a\in A\mid 1-\sigma(a)a\in A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}\}
.

In particular, for every vector subspace
V
of
A
, the set

D(V,\sigma):=\{a\in V\mid 1-\sigma(a)a\in A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}\}=D(A,\sigma)\cap V

closed, bounded and semi-algebraic.

We remind the definition of a derivation of a Jordan algebra:

Definition 2.72
.

Let
V
be a Jordan algebra over some field
\mathbb{K}
. A
\mathbb{K}
-linear map
D\colon V\to V
is called a
derivation
if
D(xy)=D(x)y+xD(y)
for all
x,y\in V
. The space of all derivations of
V
is denoted by
\Der(V)
.

Remark 2.73
.

The space
\Der(V)
is a subspace of the space of all
\mathbb{K}
-vector space endomorphisms
\End(V)
of
V
. Moreover,
\Der(V)
is a Lie algebra with respect to the commutator and it agrees with the Lie algebra of the group of all Jordan algebra automorphisms
\Aut(V)
of
V
.

Proposition 2.74
.

Let
(A,\sigma)
be a semisimple associative algebra over some field
\mathbb{K}
. The map
\ad\colon A^{-\sigma}\to\Der(A^{\sigma})
,
\ad(u)a:=ua-au
for
u\in A^{-\sigma}
,
a\in A^{\sigma}
is a surjective homomorphism of Lie algebras.

Proof.

As we have seen before, for all
u\in A^{-\sigma}
and for all
a\in A^{\sigma}
,
\ad(u)a=ua-au\in A^{\sigma}
. Moreover, it is easy to see that
\ad(u)
is a derivation for all
u\in A^{-\sigma}
and that
\ad
is a Lie algebra homomorphism. Therefore,
\ad(A^{-\sigma})\subseteq\Der(A^{\sigma})
.

Since
A
is semisimple,
A^{\sigma}
is a semisimple Jordan algebra. Every derivation of a semisimple Jordan algebra is inner (see
[
14
, Theorem 2]
), i.e. for every derivation
D\in\Der(A^{\sigma})
there exist
a_{1},a_{2}\in A^{\sigma}
such that for all
a\in A^{\sigma}
,
D(a)=\ad([a_{1},a_{2}])a=[a_{1},a_{2}]a-a[a_{1},a_{2}]
, where
[\cdot,\cdot]
is the commutator on
A
. Finally, notice that
[a_{1},a_{2}]\in A^{-\sigma}
for
a_{1},a_{2}\in A^{\sigma}
. That means that
\ad(A^{-\sigma})=\Der(A,\sigma)
.
∎

Corollary 2.75
.

Let
(A,\sigma)
be a semisimple associative algebra over a real closed field
\mathbb{K}
.
The semi-algebraic connected component of the identity
\Aut_{0}(A^{\sigma})
of the automorphism group
\Aut(A^{\sigma})
of the Jordan algebra
A^{\sigma}
agrees with the semi-algebraic connected component of the identity of the group
\Ad(U_{(A,\sigma)})
where
\Ad
is the adjoint Lie group action on its Lie algebra.

Proof.

This statement follows from the fact that
\Lie(U_{(A,\sigma)})=A^{-\sigma}
,
\Lie(\Aut(A^{\sigma}))=\Der(A^{\sigma})
and the derivative of the adjoint action
\Ad
is
\ad
.
∎

Corollary 2.76
.

Let
(A,\sigma)
be a semisimple Hermitian algebra over a real closed field
\mathbb{K}
such that the Jordan algebra
A^{\sigma}
is simple. Then
U_{(A,\sigma)}
acts transitively on the set of all Jordan frames of
A^{\sigma}
.

Proof.

This statement follows from the fact that
\Aut_{0}(A^{\sigma})
acts transitively on the set of all Jordan frames of
A^{\sigma}
(see
[
9
, Corollary IV.2.6]
for a proof over
\mathbb{R}
, general case follows identically).
∎

Corollary 2.77

(Sylvester’s law of inertia)
.

Let
(A,\sigma)
be a semisimple Hermitian algebra over a real closed field
\mathbb{K}
such that the Jordan algebra
A^{\sigma}
is simple. The action

\begin{matrix}\psi\colon&A^{\times}\times A^{\sigma}&\mapsto&A^{\sigma}\\
&(a,b)&\to&\sigma(a)ba.\end{matrix}

of the group
A^{\times}
on
A^{\sigma}
preserves the signature of elements. The orbits of this action are precisely the sets of all elements of fixed signature in
A^{\sigma}
.

Proof.

Let
k
be the rang of
A^{\sigma}
. We fix a Jordan frame
(e_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
and denote:
o_{p,q}:=\sum_{i=1}^{p}e_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{q}e_{k-i+1}
for
p+q\leq k
.
First notice that for every element
b\in A^{\sigma}
of signature
(p,q)
there is an element
g\in A^{\times}
such that
o_{p,q}=\sigma(g)b_{1}g
. To see this, first, we take a spectral decomposition
b=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}c_{i}
for a Jordan frame
(c_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
and
\lambda_{1}\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{k}
. Let
u\in U_{(A,\sigma)}
such that
u
maps the Jordan frame
(c_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
to
(e_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
. Therefore:
\sigma(u)bu=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}e_{i}
. Finally, we take
a:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mu_{i}e_{i}
where
\mu_{i}=\lambda_{i}^{-1}
if
\lambda_{i}\neq 0
and
\mu_{i}=1
otherwise. Then
\sigma(ua)b(ua)=o_{p,q}
.

The structure group
G(A^{\sigma})
acts on
A^{\sigma}
preserving the signature (see
[
15
, Theorem 1]
). Therefore,
A^{\times}
does it as well.
∎

2.7.
Matrix algebra over a Hermitian algebra

One way to construct new semisimple Hermitian algebras is to consider a matrix algebra over a semisimple Hermitian algebra. For this, we assume
(A,\sigma)
to be a semisimple Hermitian algebra. We consider the following anti-involution on the algebra
\Mat_{n}(A)
of
n\times n
-matrices over
A
:

\begin{matrix}\sigma^{T}\colon&\Mat_{n}(A)&\to&\Mat_{n}(A)\\
&M&\mapsto&\sigma(M)^{T}\end{matrix}

where
\sigma(M)
means applying
\sigma
componentwise to elements of the matrix
M\in\Mat_{n}(A)
. We denote

\Sym_{n}(A,\sigma):=\Fix_{\Mat_{n}(A)}(\sigma^{T});

\Sym_{n}^{\geq 0}(A,\sigma):=\{M\in\Sym_{n}(A,\sigma)\mid\sigma(x)^{T}Mx\in A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}\text{ for all }x\in A^{n}\};

\Sym_{n}^{+}(A,\sigma):=\{M\in\Sym_{n}(A,\sigma)\mid\sigma(x)^{T}Mx\in A^{\sigma}_{+}\text{ for all regular }x\in A^{n}\};

\UU_{n}(A,\sigma):=U_{(\Mat_{n}^{\times}(A),\sigma^{T})}=\{M\in\Mat_{n}(A)\mid\sigma(M)^{T}M=\Id_{n}\}.

Proposition 2.78
.

For a Hermitian semisimple algebra
(A,\sigma)
, the algebra
(\Mat_{n}(A),\sigma^{T})
is Hermitian and semisimple.

Proof.

First, we have to check that for every two
M,N\in\Sym_{n}(A,\sigma)
, if
M^{2}+N^{2}=0
, then
M=N=0
.

Note that for every
x\in A^{n}
, if
\sigma(x)^{T}x=0
, then
x=0
. Indeed, take
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})^{T}
such that
\sigma(x)^{T}x=\sigma(x_{1})x_{1}+\dots+\sigma(x_{n})x_{n}=0
. Since all
\sigma(x_{i})x_{i}\in A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}
, all
\sigma(x_{i})x_{i}=0
. Because of semisimplicity of
A
, all
x_{i}=0
.

Now, take
x\in A^{n}
and consider
Mx,Nx\in A^{n}
. Assume
M^{2}+N^{2}=0
, then

0=\sigma(x)^{T}(M^{2}+N^{2})x=\sigma(x)^{T}M^{2}x+\sigma(x)^{T}N^{2}x=\sigma(Mx)^{T}Mx+\sigma(Nx)^{T}Nx.

Since for every
y\in A^{2}
,
\sigma(y)^{T}y\in A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}
,
\sigma(Mx)^{T}Mx,\sigma(Nx)^{T}Nx=0
, By semisimplicity of
A
,
Mx=Nx=0
for all
x\in A^{n}
. Therefore,
M=N=0
.

Finally, we have to check that
\Mat_{n}(A)
is semisimple. We do it by induction. Assume
\Mat_{k}(A)
is semisimple for all
k<n
.
Take
X\in J:=J(\Mat_{n}(A))
in the Jacobson radical of
\Mat_{n}(A)
(see Definition
A.1
), we write
X
as a block matrix:

X=\begin{pmatrix}x&a\\
b&y\end{pmatrix}

where
x\in\Mat_{n-1}(A)
,
a,b^{T}\in A^{n-1}
,
y\in A
. Since
X\in J
by Proposition
A.9
,
\sigma^{T}(X)=-X
and
X^{2}=0
, i.e.
\sigma(x)^{T}=x
,
b=-\sigma(a)^{T}
,
y\in A^{-\sigma}
,

X^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}x^{2}-a\sigma(a)^{T}&xa+ay\\
-\sigma(a)^{T}x-y\sigma(a)^{T}&-\sigma(a)^{T}a+y^{2}\end{pmatrix}=0.

Since
0=\sigma(a)^{T}a-y^{2}=\sigma(a)^{T}a+\sigma(y)y
and
\sigma(a)^{T}a,\sigma(y)y\in A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}
,
\sigma(a)^{T}a=\sigma(y)y=0
. Because of semisimplicity of
A
,
a=0
,
y=0
. Moreover, since
x^{2}-a\sigma(a)^{T}=x^{2}=0
and
\Mat_{n-1}(A)
is semisimple,
x=0
, i.e.
X=0
. Therefore,
J=\{0\}
and
\Mat_{n}(A)
is semisimple.
∎

2.8.
Complex extensions of algebras

Let
A
be an algebra over a real closed field
\mathbb{K}
. We denote
A_{\mathbb{C}}:=A\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
and call
A_{\mathbb{C}}
the complexification of
A
. We extend
\sigma
“complex anti-linearly” to an anti-involution
\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}
on
A_{\mathbb{C}}
:

\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}(x+iy):=\sigma(x)-\sigma(y)i.

We embed
A_{\mathbb{C}}
into
\Mat_{2}(A)
in the following way:

(2.1)

\begin{matrix}\Upsilon\colon&A_{\mathbb{C}}&\to&\Mat_{2}(A)\\
&x+yi&\mapsto&\begin{pmatrix}x&y\\
-y&x\end{pmatrix}.\end{matrix}

This map is an injective homomorphism of
\mathbb{K}
-algebras. Moreover, the anti-involution
\bar{\sigma}
corresponds to
\sigma^{T}
under this embedding. From Corollary
2.18
we obtain:

Corollary 2.79
.

Let
(A,\sigma)
be a Hermitian algebra. The algebra
(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
is Hermitian if and only if
A
is semisimple. If
A
is semisimple then
A_{\mathbb{C}}
is semisimple.

Proof.

If
(A,\sigma)
is Hermitian and semisimple, then
(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma)
is Hermitian and semisimple by
2.60
.

If
A
is not semisimple, i.e. the Jacobson radical
J(A)
of
A
is not trivial, then by
A.9
, for every
0\neq x\in J(A)
,
x^{2}=0
and
\sigma(x)=-x
. Therefore,
\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}(ix)=ix\neq 0
, i.e.
ix\in A^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
(ix)^{2}=0
. This contradicts to the property to be Hermitian for
(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
.
∎

Corollary 2.80
.

Let
(A,\sigma)
be Hermitian and semisimple.

•

The group
U_{(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})}=\{z\in A_{\mathbb{C}}\mid\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}(z)z=1\}
is semi-algebraically connected (as a semi-algebraic deformation retract of a semi-algebraically connected space
A^{\times}_{\mathbb{C}}
).

•

If
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
, then the group
U_{(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})}
is a maximal compact subgroup of
A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
.

Remark 2.81
.

There is another anti-involution on
A_{\mathbb{C}}
, namely the complex linear extension
\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}
of
\sigma
. Together with this anti-involution
A_{\mathbb{C}}
is never Hermitian because

0=1-1=\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}(1)\cdot 1+\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}(i)\cdot i.

Similarly to spectral theorems for
\sigma
-symmetric elements of a semisimple, Hermitian algebra
(A,\sigma)
, the spectral theorem for
\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}
-normal elements of
(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
can be proven.

Theorem 2.82

(Spectral theorem for normal elements, first version)
.

Let
(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
be Hermitian. For every
\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}
-normal element
b\in A_{\mathbb{C}}
, there exist a unique
k\in\mathbb{N}
, unique elements
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{k}\in\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
, all distinct, and a unique complete system of orthogonal idempotents
c_{1},\dots,c_{k}\in\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}[b]\cap A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}
such that

b=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}c_{i}.

We call this the
spectral decomposition
of
b
.

Theorem 2.83

(Spectral theorem for normal elements, second version)
.

Let
(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
be Hermitian. Suppose that the Jordan algebra
A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}
has rank
n
. For every
\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}
-normal element
b\in A_{\mathbb{C}}
there exist a Jordan frame
(e_{1},\dots,e_{n})
of
A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}
and an
n
-tuple of elements
(\lambda_{1}(b),\dots,\lambda_{n}(b))\in\mathbb{K}^{n}_{\mathbb{C}}
such that

b=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}(b)e_{i}.

The tuple
(\lambda_{1}(b),\dots,\lambda_{n}(b))
is uniquely defined by
b
up to permutations. The elements
\lambda_{1}(b),\dots,\lambda_{n}(b)\in\mathbb{K}
(with their multiplicities) are called the
eigenvalues
of
b
