. In particular, they do not depend on the Jordan frame
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\in A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}
.

Remark 2.84
.

A Jordan frame
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\in A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}
associated to a
\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}
-normal element
b\in A_{\mathbb{C}}
by Theorem
2.83
is in general not unique and elements
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\notin\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}[b]
, in contrast to the complete system of orthogonal idempotents from Theorem
2.82
.

Corollary 2.85
.

Let
(A,\sigma)
be semisimple and Hermitian. Let
a_{1},a_{2}\in A^{\sigma}
that commute. Then there exist a complete orthogonal system of idempotents
c_{1},\dots c_{k}\in A^{\sigma}
and elements
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{k},\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{k}\in\mathbb{K}
such that

a_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}c_{i},\;\;a_{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mu_{i}c_{i}.

Moreover, there exist an
a\in A^{\sigma}
such that
c_{1},\dots,c_{k}\in\mathbb{K}[a]
. In particular,
a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathbb{K}[a]
.

Corollary 2.86
.

If
a\in A_{\mathbb{C}}
is
\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}
-normal, then
\sigma(a)\in\mathbb{K}[a]
.

Corollary 2.87
.

Let
(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
be Hermitian. If
a\in A^{-\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}
, then all the eigenvalues of
a
are purely imagine. If
a\in U_{(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})}
, then all the eigenvalues are in
S^{1}_{\mathbb{K}}=\{z\in\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}\mid|z|=1\}
.

Corollary 2.88

(Spectral theorem for
U_{(A,\sigma)}
)
.

For every
u\in U_{(A,\sigma)}
, there exist unique
r\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
,
s\in\{0,1,2\}
with
r+s>0
, unique systems of idempotents
c_{1},\dots,c_{r}\in A^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
c_{1}^{\prime},\dots,c_{s}^{\prime}\in A^{\sigma}
such that
c_{1},\dots,c_{r},\bar{c}_{1},\dots,\bar{c}_{r},c_{1}^{\prime},\dots,c_{s}^{\prime}
is a complete orthogonal system of idempotents of
A^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}}
and unique elements
\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{r}\in S^{1}_{\mathbb{K}}
with
\Imm(\zeta_{i})>0
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,r\}
all distinct and
\varepsilon_{1},\dots\varepsilon_{s}\in\{1,-1\}
all distinct such that

u=\sum_{j=1}^{r}(\zeta_{j}c_{j}+\bar{\zeta}_{j}\bar{c}_{j})+\sum_{j=1}^{s}\varepsilon_{j}c_{j}^{\prime}.

Proof.

For an element
u\in U(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
,
u\in U(A,\sigma)
if and only if
u=\bar{u}
. We take the spectral decompositions of
u
and
\bar{u}
:

u=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\zeta_{j}c_{j},\;\;\bar{u}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\bar{\zeta}_{j}\bar{c}_{j}

where all
\zeta_{j}\in S_{1}
,
(c_{j})
is a complete orthogonal system of idempotents of
A^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}}
.
Notice, all
\bar{c}_{1},\dots,\bar{c}_{k}
is a complete orthogonal system of idempotents of
A^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}}
because
\bar{c}_{i}\bar{c}_{j}=\overline{c_{i}c_{j}}
.
If
u=\bar{u}
, then, because of uniqueness of the spectral decomposition, for every
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
there exists
j^{\prime}\in\{1,\dots,k\}
such that
\zeta_{j}c_{j}=\bar{\zeta}_{j}\bar{c}_{j^{\prime}}
. There can be to cases:

(1)

j=j^{\prime}
then
\zeta_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
i.e.
\zeta_{j}\in\{1,-1\}
.

(2)

j\neq j^{\prime}
then
c_{j^{\prime}}=\bar{c}_{j}
and
\zeta_{j}\notin\mathbb{R}
, i.e.
\Imm(\zeta_{j})\neq 0
.

Because all
\zeta_{j}
are distinct, there can be at most one
j
such that
\zeta_{j}=1
and at most one
j
with
\zeta_{j}=-1
. For such
j
,
c_{j}\in A^{\sigma}
. So we obtain

u=\sum_{j=1}^{r}(\zeta_{j}c_{j}+\bar{\zeta}_{j}\bar{c}_{j})+\sum_{j=1}^{s}\varepsilon_{j}c_{j}^{\prime}.

for appropriate
r,s\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
,
s\leq 2
. Finally, we can rename the idempotents so that
\Imm(\zeta_{j})>0
for all
j\in\{1,\dots r\}
.
∎

Definition 2.89
.

The
determinant map
on
U_{(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})}
is given by:

\det(u)=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\zeta_{j}\in S^{1}_{\mathbb{K}}\subset\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}

where
u=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\zeta_{j}e_{j}
for some Jordan frame
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\in A^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proposition 2.90
.

Let
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
. The fundamental group of
U_{(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})}
contains a copy of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
.

Proof.

The determinant map

\det\colon U_{(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})}\to S^{1}

is continuous and surjective. It induces the homomorphism of fundamental groups:

(\det)_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(U_{(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})},1)\to\pi_{1}(S^{1},1).

This homomorphism is surjective because the curve
u(t)=e^{it}e_{1}+\sum_{j=2}^{n}e_{j}
for
t\in[0,2\pi]
for some Jordan frame
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}
maps to the curve
\det_{*}u(t)=e^{it}
which generates
\pi_{1}(S^{1},1)
. Therefore
u(t)
generates in
\pi_{1}(U_{(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})},1)
a subgroup that is isomorphic to
(\mathbb{Z},+)
.
∎

Theorem 2.91
.

Let
(A,\sigma)
be a semisimple Hermitian algebra and
(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
be its complexification. Assume that the Jordan algebra
A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}
is simple.
The following action of
A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
on
(A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}})^{\times}

\begin{array}[]{cccl}\psi_{\mathbb{C}}\colon&A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}\times(A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}})^{\times}&\to&(A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}})^{\times}\\
&(a,b)&\mapsto&\sigma(a)ba\end{array}

is transitive.

Proof.

We will show that for every
b\in(A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}})^{\times}
there exists an
a\in A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
such that
\psi_{\mathbb{C}}(a,1)=b
. We take
b\in(A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}})^{\times}
and consider its polar decomposition of
b=ub^{\prime}
where
u\in U_{(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})}
,
b^{\prime}\in(A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}})^{\times}
. Using the spectral theorem we obtain:

b^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}c_{i}

where
(c_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
is a Jordan frame of
A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}
and
\lambda_{i}>0
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
.

We fix a Jordan frame
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
of
A^{\sigma}
. This is also a Jordan frame of
A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}
. By
2.76
, the group
U_{(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})}
acts transitively on Jordan frames of
A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}
. Therefore, there exists
u^{\prime}\in U_{(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})}
such that
x_{i}=\bar{\sigma}(u^{\prime})c_{i}u^{\prime}
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
. Further

\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}(u^{\prime})bu^{\prime}=\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}(u^{\prime})u\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}(u^{\prime})^{-1}\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}(u^{\prime})b^{\prime}u^{\prime}=\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}(u^{\prime})u\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}(u^{\prime})^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}x_{i}=:u^{\prime\prime}b^{\prime\prime}

where
u^{\prime\prime}=\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}(u^{\prime})u\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}(u^{\prime})^{-1}\in U_{(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})}
,
b^{\prime\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}x_{i}\in A^{\sigma}
. Since
u^{\prime\prime}b^{\prime\prime}=\sigma(u^{\prime})bu^{\prime}\in A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}
,

u^{\prime\prime}b^{\prime\prime}=\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}(u^{\prime\prime}b^{\prime\prime})=b^{\prime\prime}\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}(u^{\prime\prime})=b^{\prime\prime}(\bar{u}^{\prime\prime})^{-1}.

Therefore,
b^{\prime\prime}=u^{\prime\prime}b^{\prime\prime}\bar{u}^{\prime\prime}
. By induction, we obtain
b^{\prime\prime}=(u^{\prime\prime})^{n}b^{\prime\prime}(\bar{u}^{\prime\prime})^{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, or equivalently
(u^{\prime\prime})^{n}b^{\prime\prime}=b^{\prime\prime}(\bar{u}^{\prime\prime})^{-n}
.

Now assume
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
. Since
(u^{\prime\prime})^{n}b^{\prime\prime}=b^{\prime\prime}(\bar{u}^{\prime\prime})^{-n}
holds for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the following equality
f(u^{\prime\prime})b^{\prime\prime}=b^{\prime\prime}f((\bar{u}^{\prime\prime})^{-1})
holds for every function
f
that can be expressed as a convergent Taylor series at
u^{\prime\prime}
. Since
u^{\prime\prime}\in U_{(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})}
,
u^{\prime\prime}\neq 0
. By
2.87
, there exists
w\in U_{(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})}
such that
(u^{\prime\prime})=w^{2}
. So we can take as
f
the branch of the square root such that
f(u^{\prime\prime})=w
. Then we obtain
wb^{\prime\prime}=b^{\prime\prime}(\bar{w})^{-1}
. Since any branch of the square root is a semi-algebraic function, using the Tarski–Seidenberg transfer principle (see
[
7
, Proposition 5.2.3]
), this holds for every real closed field
\mathbb{K}
.

This implies:

u^{\prime\prime}b^{\prime\prime}=wb^{\prime\prime}\bar{w}^{-1}=(wb^{\frac{1}{2}})b^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{w}^{-1}=\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}(b^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{w}^{-1})b^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{w}^{-1}=\psi_{\mathbb{C}}(b^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{w}^{-1},1).

Finally,

b=\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}((u^{\prime})^{-1})u^{\prime\prime}b^{\prime\prime}(u^{\prime})^{-1}=\psi_{\mathbb{C}}(b^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{w}^{-1}(u^{\prime})^{-1},1)
.
∎

2.9.
Quaternionic extensions of algebras

If
A
is an algebra over a real closed field
\mathbb{K}
, then we call
A_{\mathbb{H}}=A\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{H}}
the
quaternionification
of
A
.

Sometimes, to make a construction more precise, we will
write
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{H}}\{i,j,k\}
to emphasize the imaginary units. The multiplication rule is then
ij=-ji=k
. Sometimes, we will also write
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}\{\kappa\}
to emphasize the imaginary unit
\kappa
of the complexified field
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

If
A_{\mathbb{C}}=A\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the complexification of some real algebra
A
, then it can be
embedded into
A\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{H}}
in many different ways. If we write
A_{\mathbb{C}}:=A\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}\{i\}
,

A_{\mathbb{H}}:=A\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{H}}\{i,J,K\}
, it means that
A_{\mathbb{C}}
is embedded into
A_{\mathbb{H}}
by the map induced by
the identification
A_{\mathbb{C}}\ni i\mapsto i\in A_{\mathbb{H}}
.

Let
A
be a
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}
-algebra,
A_{0}\subset A
be
\mathbb{K}
-subalgebra of
A
with the property that there is a central element
I\in Z(A)
such that

I^{2}=-1
and
A=A_{0}\oplus A_{0}I
. Then we say that
A_{0}
is a
real locus
of
A
with respect to the
imaginary unit
I
. In this case,
A
is isomorphic to
A_{0}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}\{I\}
as
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{C}}\{I\}
-algebra. We
take the following
\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{H}}
-algebra:

\mathbb{H}[A,A_{0},I,J]:=A_{0}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{K}_{\mathbb{H}}\{I,J,K\}.

The algebra
A
sits inside
\mathbb{H}[A,A_{0},I,J]
as described above.

Definition 2.92
.

We call
\mathbb{H}[A,A_{0},I,J]
the
quaternionification
of
A
with respect to the real locus

A_{0}
and the imaginary unit
I
.

The algebra
A_{\mathbb{H}}
can be embedded into
\Mat_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}})
in the following way:

(2.2)

\begin{matrix}\Upsilon_{\mathbb{H}}\colon&A_{\mathbb{H}}&\to&\Mat_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}})\\
&x+yj&\mapsto&\begin{pmatrix}x&y\\
-\bar{y}&\bar{x}\end{pmatrix}.\end{matrix}

This map is an injective homomorphism of
\mathbb{C}\{i\}
-algebras. Moreover, the anti-involution
\sigma_{1}
on
A_{\mathbb{H}}
defined as follows:

\sigma_{1}(x+yj)=\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}(x)-\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}(y)j

for
x,y\in A_{\mathbb{C}}
, corresponds under this embedding to
\bar{\sigma}^{T}
. By Corollary
2.18
, we obtain:

Corollary 2.93
.

Let
(A,\sigma)
be Hermitian and semisimple.

•

The algebra
(A_{\mathbb{H}},\sigma_{1})
is Hermitian and semisimple.

•

If
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
, then the group
U_{(A_{\mathbb{H}},\sigma_{1})}=\{z\in A_{\mathbb{C}}\mid\sigma_{1}(z)z=1\}
is a maximal compact subgroup of
A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\times}
.

Remark 2.94
.

There is another anti-involution
\sigma_{0}
on
A_{\mathbb{H}}
defined as follows:

\sigma_{0}(x+yj)=\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}(x)+\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}(y)j

for
x,y\in A_{\mathbb{C}}
. The algebra
(A_{\mathbb{H}},\sigma_{0})
is never Hermitian.

2.10.
Some properties of real Hermitian algebras

In this section, we assume that
(A,\sigma)
is a semisimple real Hermitian algebra. We will state some properties that depend on the fact that
\mathbb{R}
is locally compact, contrary to the other real closed fields.

Proposition 2.95
.

The group
U_{(A,\sigma)}
is a maximal compact subgroup of
A^{\times}
.

Proof.

The group
U_{(A,\sigma)}\subset\Isom(\beta)
is compact by
2.71
. By
2.68
,
U_{(A,\sigma)}
is a deformation retract of
A^{\times}
. Hence, it is a maximal compact subgroup of
A^{\times}
.
∎

Corollary 2.96
.

\UU_{n}(A,\sigma)
is a maximal compact subgroup of
\Mat_{n}^{\times}(A)

Corollary 2.97

(Polar decomposition, second version)
.

The following map:

\begin{matrix}\bpol\colon&U_{(A,\sigma)}\times A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}&\to&A\\
&(u,b)&\mapsto&ub.\end{matrix}

is proper, surjective.

Proof.

Since the group
U_{(A,\sigma)}
is compact, we can take a closure in the polar decomposition and the map stays surjective. Let
K\subset A
be a compact subset, then

\bpol^{-1}(K)\subseteq\{(\sigma(a)a)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mid a\in K\}\times U_{(A,\sigma)}

is compact.
∎

Proposition 2.98
.

If
A
is Hermitian, the map
\theta\colon A\to A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}
is proper.

Proof.

Let
K\subset A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}
be a compact subset. Then

\theta^{-1}(K)=\{ub_{+}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mid u\in U_{(A,\sigma)},b_{+}\in K\}=\bpol(U_{(A,\sigma)}\times K).

Since
U_{(A,\sigma)}\times K
is compact in
U_{(A,\sigma)}\times A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}
and
\bpol
is continuous,
\theta^{-1}(K)
is compact.
∎

Proposition 2.99
.

Let
(A,\sigma)
be Hermitian. The domain

D(A,\sigma):=\{a\in A\mid 1-\sigma(a)a\in A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}\}

is compact.

In particular, for every vector subspace
V
of
A
, the domain

D(V,\sigma):=\{a\in V\mid 1-\sigma(a)a\in A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}\}=D\cap V

is compact.

This follows directly from
2.71

3.
Symplectic groups over noncommutative algebras

In this section we introduce symplectic groups over unital rings with anti-involution.

3.1.
Sesquilinear forms on
A
-modules and their groups of symmetries

Let
A
be a unital associative ring with an anti-involution
\sigma
.

Definition 3.1
.

A
\sigma
-sesquilinear form

\omega
on a right
A
-module
V
is a map

\omega\colon V\times V\to A

such that

\omega(x+y,z)=\omega(x,z)+\omega(y,z)

\omega(x,y+z)=\omega(x,y)+\omega(x,z)

\omega(x_{1}r_{1},x_{2}r_{2})=\sigma(r_{1})\omega(x_{1},x_{2})r_{2}

We denote by

\Aut(\omega):=\{f\in\Aut(V)\mid\forall x,y\in V:\omega(f(x),f(y))=\omega(x,y)\}

the group of symmetries of
\omega
. We also define the corresponding Lie algebra:

\End(\omega):=\{f\in\End(V)\mid\forall x,y\in V:\omega(f(x),y)+\omega(x,f(y))=0\}

with the usual Lie bracket
[f,g]=fg-gf
.

We now set
V=A^{2}
. We view
V
as the set of columns and endow it with the structure of a right
A
-module.

Definition 3.2
.

We make the following definitions:

(1)

A pair
(x,y)
for
x,y\in A^{2}
is called
basis
of
A^{2}
if for every
z\in A^{2}
there exist
a,b\in A
such that
z=xa+yb
.

(2)

The element
x\in A^{2}
is called
regular
if there exists
y\in A^{2}
such that
(x,y)
is a basis of
A^{2}
.

(3)

l\subseteq A^{2}
is called a
line
if
l=xA
for a regular
x\in A^{2}
. We denote the space of lines of
A^{2}
by
\mathbb{P}(A^{2})
.

(4)

Two regular elements
x,y\in A^{2}
are called
linearly independent
if
(x,y)
is a basis of
A^{2}
.

(5)

Two lines
l,m
are called
transverse
if
l=xA
,
m=yA
for linearly independent
x,y\in A^{2}
.

(6)

An element
x\in A^{2}
is called
isotropic
with respect to
\omega
if
\omega(x,x)=0
. The set of all isotropic regular elements of
(A^{2},\omega)
is denoted by
\Is(\omega)
.

(7)

A line
l
is called isotropic if
l=xA
for an regular isotropic
x\in A^{2}
. The set of all isotropic lines of
(A^{2},\omega)
is denoted by
\mathbb{P}(\Is(\omega))
.

Definition 3.3
.

We consider a form
\omega
and say

(1)

The form

\omega
is
non-degenerate
if for every regular
x\in A^{2}
there exists a
y\in A^{2}
such that
\omega(x,y)\in A^{\times}
.

(2)

The form is
\sigma
-symmetric
if
\omega(x_{2},x_{1})=\sigma(\omega(x_{1},x_{2}))
for all
x_{1},x_{2}\in A^{2}
.

(3)

The form is
\sigma
-skew-symmetric
if
\omega(x_{2},x_{1})=-\sigma(\omega(x_{1},x_{2}))
for all
x_{1},x_{2}\in A^{2}
.

(4)

When
A
is Hermitian, a
\sigma
-symmetric form is called
\sigma
-inner product
if
\omega(x,x)\in A^{\sigma}_{+}
for all regular
x\in A^{2}
.

We can now introduce the symplectic group
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
over
(A,\sigma)
.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
(A,\sigma)
be a unital ring with anti-involution.
Let
\omega(x,y):=\sigma(x)^{T}\Omega y
with
\Omega=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}
. The form
\omega
is called the
standard symplectic form
on
A^{2}
.
The group
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma):=\Aut(\omega)
is the
symplectic group

\Sp_{2}
over
(A,\sigma)
. Its Lie algebra is
\spp_{2}(A,\sigma):=\End(\omega)
.

We have

\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\,\middle|\,\sigma(a)c,\,\sigma(b)d\in A^{\sigma},\,\sigma(a)d-\sigma(c)b=1\right\}\subseteq\Mat_{2}^{\times}(A)

\spp_{2}(A,\sigma)=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}x&z\\
y&-\sigma(x)\end{pmatrix}\,\middle|\,x\in A,\;y,z\in A^{\sigma}\right\}\subseteq\Mat_{2}(A).

From now on, we assume
\omega(x,y):=\sigma(x)^{T}\Omega y
on
A^{2}
.

Definition 3.5
.

A basis
(x,y)
of
A^{2}
is called
symplectic
if
x,y
are isotropic and
\omega(x,y)=1
.

Proposition 3.6
.

For every basis
(x,y)
of
A^{2}
and for every
z\in A^{2}
there exist unique
a,b\in A
such that
z=xa+yb
. Moreover, for every regular
x\in A^{2}
, the map

\begin{matrix}A&\to&xA\\
a&\mapsto&xa\end{matrix}

is an isomorphism of right
A
-modules.

Proof.

Take a basis
(x,y)
of
A^{2}
.
Consider the following
A
-homomorphism of right
A
-modules:

\begin{matrix}A^{2}&\to&A^{2}\\
(a,b)&\mapsto&z=xa+yb.\end{matrix}

This is also a surjective
\mathbb{R}
-homomorphism of vector spaces of the same dimension. Therefore, it is injective, i.e.
(a,b)
is uniquely defines by
z
. The restriction of this homomorphism to
A\times\{0\}
is an isomorphism
A\to xA
of right
A
-modules.
∎

Proposition 3.7
.

The form
\omega
is non-degenerate.

Proof.

Let
x=(x_{1},x_{2})^{T}\in A^{2}
regular. We want to find
y\in A^{2}
such that
\omega(y,x)=1
. Since
x
is regular, there exists
x^{\prime}=(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})^{T}\in A^{2}
such that
(x,x^{\prime})
is a basis. That means that the matrix

X:=\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&x_{1}^{\prime}\\
x_{2}&x_{2}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}
is invertible, i.e. there exists the inverse matrix

X^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}&a_{2}\\
a_{1}^{\prime}&a_{2}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}
. Therefore,
a_{1}x_{1}+a_{2}x_{2}=1
. We take
y:=(\sigma(a_{2}),-\sigma(a_{1}))^{T}
, then

\omega(y,x)=(a_{2},-a_{1})\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\end{pmatrix}=(a_{1},a_{2})\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\end{pmatrix}=1.

So
\omega
is non-degenerate.
∎

Proposition 3.8
.

An element
x=(x_{1},x_{2})^{T}\in A^{2}
is isotropic if and only if
\sigma(x_{1})x_{2}\in A^{\sigma}
.

Proof.

The proof follows by direct computation.
∎

Proposition 3.9
.

If
x,y\in A^{2}
are isotropic and
\omega(x,y)=1
, then
(x,y)
is a basis.

Proof.

Let
x,y\in A^{2}
are isotropic and
\omega(x,y)=1
. Consider the map

\begin{matrix}A^{2}&\to&A^{2}\\
(a,b)&\mapsto&xa+yb.\end{matrix}

To see that this map is an isomorphism, it is enough to check that it is injective. Assume
xa+yb=0
for some
a,b\in A
, then

0=\omega(x,xa+yb)=\omega(x,y)b=b,

0=\omega(y,xa+yb)=-\omega(x,y)a=-a.

So
a=b=0
.
∎

Corollary 3.10
.

\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\,\middle|\,\left(\begin{pmatrix}a\\
c\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}b\\
d\end{pmatrix}\right)\text{ is a symplectic basis}\right\}

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
x\in A^{2}
regular isotropic,
y\in A^{2}
and
\omega(x,y)\in A^{\times}
. Then
(x,y)
is a basis of
A^{2}
. In particular,
y
is regular.

Proof.

To see that
(x,y)
is a basis, it is enough to check that the map

\begin{matrix}A^{2}&\to&A^{2}\\
(a,b)&\mapsto&xa+yb\end{matrix}

is injective. Assume
xa+yb=0
for some
a,b\in A
, then

0=\omega(x,xa+yb)=\omega(x,y)b.

Since
\omega(x,y)\in A^{\times}
,
b=0
.

The element
x\in A^{2}
is regular, therefore, by Proposition
3.6
, if
xa=0
, then
a=0
. So, we obtain
(a,b)=(0,0)
, i.e. the map above is an isomorphism.
∎

Proposition 3.12
.

For every regular isotropic
x\in A^{2}
, there exists an isotropic
y\in A^{2}
such that
(x,y)
is a symplectic basis.

Proof.

Since
\omega
is non-degenerate, there exists
y^{\prime}\in A^{2}
such that
\omega(x,y^{\prime})\in A^{\times}
and
(x,y^{\prime})
is a basis. We take
y^{\prime\prime}:=y^{\prime}-\frac{x}{2}\omega(y^{\prime},x)^{-1}\omega(y,y)
, then

\omega(y,y)=\omega(y^{\prime},y^{\prime})-\frac{1}{2}\omega(y^{\prime},x)\omega(y^{\prime},x)^{-1}\omega(y,y)-

-\frac{1}{2}\sigma(\omega(y^{\prime},x)^{-1}\omega(y,y))\omega(x,y^{\prime})=0.

Since
\omega(x,y^{\prime})=\omega(x,y^{\prime\prime})
, if we take
y:=y^{\prime\prime}\omega(x,y^{\prime})^{-1}
, we obtain
\omega(x,y)=1
and
x,y
are isotropic, so
(x,y)
is a symplectic basis.
∎

Corollary 3.13
.

The group
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts transitively on regular isotropic elements of
(A^{2},\omega)
.

Proof.

If
x=(x_{1},x_{2})^{T}\in A^{2}
is regular isotropic, then there exists
y=(y_{1},y_{2})^{T}\in A^{2}
regular isotropic such that
(x,y)
is a symplectic basis. Then

g:=\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&y_{1}\\
x_{2}&y_{2}\end{pmatrix}\in\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)

and
g(1,0)^{T}=x
.
∎

Let now
\mathbb{K}
be a real closed field and
(A,\sigma)
be a Hermitian
\mathbb{K}
-algebra. We consider the following sesquilinear form
b\colon A^{2}\times A^{2}\to A
,
b(x,y):=\sigma(x)^{T}y
for
x,y\in A^{2}
.

Proposition 3.14
.

b(x,x)=0
for an element
x\in A^{2}
if and only if
x=0
.

Proof.

For
x=(x_{1},x_{2})^{T}\in A^{2}
,
b(x,x)=\sigma(x_{1})x_{1}+\sigma(x_{2})x_{2}
with
\sigma(x_{1})x_{1},\sigma(x_{2})x_{2}\in A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}
. If
b(x,x)=0
, then
\sigma(x_{1})x_{1}=\sigma(x_{2})x_{2}=0
.
∎

Proposition 3.15
.

If
x\in A^{2}
is regular then
b(x,x)\in A^{\sigma}_{+}
.

Proof.

Let
x=(x_{1},x_{2})^{T}
and assume
b(x,x)=\sigma(x)x=\sigma(x_{1})x_{1}+\sigma(x_{2})x_{2}\in A^{\sigma}_{\geq 0}
is not invertible. By the Lemma
2.27
, there exists
c\in\mathbb{R}[a]\subseteq A^{\sigma}
such that
0=cb(x,x)c=b(xc,xc)
. Therefore,
xc=0
and so by Proposition
3.6
, the map
A\to xA
,
a\mapsto xa
is not invertible. In particular,
x
is not regular.
∎

Proposition 3.16

(Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization)
.

Let
x\in A^{2}
be regular, then there exists
a\in A
and
y\in A^{2}
such that
(xa,y)
is an
orthonormal with respect to
b
basis
of
A^{2}
, i.e.
b(xa,xa)=b(y,y)=1
,
b(xa,y)=0
.

Proof.

Since
x
is regular,
b(x,x)\in A^{\sigma}_{+}
. Take
a=b(x,x)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, then
b(xa,xa)=1
. Moreover, there exists
z\in A^{2}
such that
(x,z)
is a basis of
A^{2}
. Consider
y^{\prime}:=z-xab(xa,z)
, obviously
(xa,y^{\prime})
is a basis as well. Then
b(xa,y^{\prime})=b(xa,z-xab(xa,z))=b(xa,z)-b(xa,xa)b(xa,z)=0
. Since
y^{\prime}
is regular and so
b(y^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in A^{\sigma}_{+}
, we can take
y:=y^{\prime}b(y^{\prime},y^{\prime})^{-1}
.
∎

3.2.
Maximal compact subgroup of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
over Hermitian algebras

We now assume
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
and
(A,\sigma)
to be Hermitian, semisimple
\mathbb{R}
-algebra. We describe a maximal compact subgroup of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

Definition 3.17
.

We denote:

\UU_{2}(A,\sigma):=\{M\in\Mat_{2}(A)\mid\sigma(M)^{T}M=\Id\};

\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma):=\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)\cap\UU_{2}(A,\sigma).

Proposition 3.18
.

\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
-b&a\end{pmatrix}\left|\,\begin{matrix}\sigma(a)a+\sigma(b)b=1\\
\sigma(a)b-\sigma(b)a=0\end{matrix}\right.,\,a,b\in A\right\}.

Proof.

Take

M:=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)
. On one hand,
M\in\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
, therefore,

M^{-1}=-\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\sigma(M)^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\sigma(d)&-\sigma(b)\\
-\sigma(c)&\sigma(a)\end{pmatrix}.

On the other hand,
M\in\UU_{2}(A,\sigma)
, therefore,

M^{-1}=\sigma(M)^{T}=\begin{pmatrix}\sigma(a)&\sigma(c)\\
\sigma(b)&\sigma(d)\end{pmatrix}.

So we obtain,
a=d
and
b=-c
.
∎

Theorem 3.19
.

The group

\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)
is a maximal compact subgroup of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

Proof.

The group
\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)
is a closed subgroup of the compact group
\UU_{2}(A,\sigma)
, so it is compact.

Now, we show that
\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)
is a maximal compact subgroup of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
. For this let
K
be a compact subgroup containing
\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)
as a proper subgroup. We consider the following decomposition of
\spp_{2}(A,\sigma)
:

\spp_{2}(A,\sigma)=\ksp_{2}(A,\sigma)\oplus S

where

\ksp_{2}(A,\sigma)=\Lie(\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma))=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
-b&a\end{pmatrix}\,\middle|\,\sigma(a)=-a\in A,b\in A^{\sigma}\right\},

S=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}c&d\\
d&-c\end{pmatrix}\,\middle|\,c,d\in A^{\sigma}\right\}.

By our assumption,
\Lie(K)
contains
\ksp_{2}(A,\sigma)
and has nontrivial intersection with
S
. Take some matrix

\begin{pmatrix}c&d\\
d&-c\end{pmatrix}\in\Lie(K)\cap S,\;c,d\in A^{\sigma}.

The matrix

\begin{pmatrix}0&d\\
-d&0\end{pmatrix}\in\ksp_{2}(A,\sigma)\subset\Lie(K),

therefore,

\begin{pmatrix}c&2d\\
0&-c\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}c&d\\
d&-c\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0&d\\
-d&0\end{pmatrix}\in\Lie(K)\setminus\ksp_{2}(A,\sigma).

Using the exponential map of
\spp_{2}(A,\sigma)
restricted to
\Lie(K)
, we obtain that there exits a matrix
M:=\begin{pmatrix}g&gx\\
0&g^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\in K\setminus\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)
where
g=\exp(c)\in A^{\sigma}
,
x\in A^{\sigma}
. We consider the spectral decomposition of
g=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}c_{i}
with
\lambda_{i}>0
and
(c_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
a complete orthogonal system of idempotents. Take a sequence
\{M^{r}\}\subseteq K
. Then

M^{r}_{11}=g^{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}^{r}c_{i},\;M^{r}_{22}=g^{-k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}^{-r}c_{i}.

Assume that there exists
s\in\{1,\dots,k\}
such that
\lambda_{s}\neq\pm 1
. Then either
0<|\lambda_{s}|<1
or
0<|\lambda_{s}^{-1}|<1
. Without loss of generality, we may assume
0<|\lambda_{s}|<1
. Since
K
is compact,
\{M^{r}\}\subseteq K
has a convergent subsequence
\{M^{r_{j}}\}\subseteq K
:

\lim M^{r_{j}}_{11}=\lim\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}^{r_{j}}c_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\hat{\lambda}_{i}c_{i}

where
\hat{\lambda}_{i}=\lim\lambda_{i}^{r_{j}}
. But
\hat{\lambda}_{s}=\lim\lambda_{s}^{r_{j}}=0
for any subsequence
\{r_{j}\}
. Therefore
\lim M^{r_{j}}_{11}
is not invertible and so
\lim M^{r_{j}}
is not invertible as well. Therefore, all
\lambda_{i}=\pm 1
and
g^{2}=1
. The element
L:=\begin{pmatrix}g&0\\
0&g^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\in\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)\subset K
. Then
ML=\begin{pmatrix}1&x\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\in K
. Take
(ML)^{r}=\begin{pmatrix}1&rx\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\in K
. This sequence does not have any convergent subsequence unless
x=0
. So we get
M=L\in\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)
. This contradicts the assumption
M\notin\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)
and we obtain that
\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)
is a maximal compact subgroup of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.
∎

Corollary 3.20
.

The embedding

\Upsilon\colon A_{\mathbb{C}}\to\Mat_{2}(A)

(see equation
2.1
) maps
U_{(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})}
isomorphically to
\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)
. In particular, the fundamental group of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
is infinite.

3.3.
Maximal compact subgroups of
\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
over complexified algebras

Let
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
and
(A,\sigma)
be a Hermitian, semisimple
\mathbb{R}
-algebra. In Section
2.8
we have seen that
(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
is also Hermitian and semisimple and
(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
is never Hermitian.

Definition 3.21
.

We set

\KSp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}):=\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})\cap\UU_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}).

Proposition 3.22
.

The group
\KSp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
is given by

\KSp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
-\bar{b}&\bar{a}\end{pmatrix}\left|\;\begin{matrix}\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}(a)a+\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}(b)\bar{b}=1\\
\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}(a)b-\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}(b)\bar{a}=0\end{matrix}\right.,\;a,b\in A_{\mathbb{C}}\right\}.

Proof.

Take

M:=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\KSp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
. On one hand,
M\in\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
, therefore,

M^{-1}=-\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}(M)^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}(d)&-\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}(b)\\
-\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}(c)&\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}(a)\end{pmatrix}.

On the other hand,
M\in\UU_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
, therefore,

M^{-1}=\bar{\sigma}(M)_{\mathbb{C}}^{T}=\begin{pmatrix}\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}(a)&\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}(c)\\
\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}(b)&\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}(d)\end{pmatrix}.

So we obtain,
d=\bar{a}
and
c=-\bar{b}
.
∎

Theorem 3.23
.

The group

\KSp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
is a maximal compact subgroup of
\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
.

Proof.

The proof follows the same strategy as the proof of Theorem
3.19
.

By definition,
\KSp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
is closed subgroup of
\UU_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
which is compact, so
\KSp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
is compact as well.

To show that
\KSp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
is a maximal compact subgroup of
\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
, we assume
K
to be a compact subgroup of
\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
containing
\KSp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
as a proper subgroup. We consider the following decomposition of
\spp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
:

\spp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})=\ksp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})\oplus S

where

\ksp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})=\Lie(\KSp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}))=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
-\bar{b}&\bar{a}\end{pmatrix}\,\middle|\,\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}(a)=-a\in A_{\mathbb{C}},b\in A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{\mathbb{C}}\right\},

S=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}c&d\\
\bar{d}&-\bar{c}\end{pmatrix}\,\middle|\,c,d\in A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{\mathbb{C}}\right\}.

By our assumption,
\Lie(K)
contains
\ksp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
and has nontrivial intersection with
S
. So we can take a matrix

\begin{pmatrix}c&d\\
\bar{d}&-\bar{c}\end{pmatrix}\in\Lie(K)\cap S,\;c,d\in A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{\mathbb{C}}.

Then

\begin{pmatrix}0&d\\
-\bar{d}&0\end{pmatrix}\in\ksp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})\subset\Lie(K),

therefore,

\begin{pmatrix}c&2d\\
0&-\bar{c}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}c&d\\
\bar{d}&-\bar{c}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0&d\\
-\bar{d}&0\end{pmatrix}\in\Lie(K)\setminus\ksp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}).

Using the exponential map of
\spp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
restricted to
\Lie(K)
, we obtain that there exits a matrix
M:=\begin{pmatrix}g&gx\\
0&\bar{g}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\in K\setminus\KSp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
where
g=\exp(c)\in A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{\mathbb{C}}
,
x\in A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{\mathbb{C}}
. We now consider the spectral decomposition of
g=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}c_{i}
with
\lambda_{i}>0
and
(c_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
a complete orthogonal system of idempotents. Take a sequence
\{M^{r}\}\subseteq K
. Then

M^{r}_{11}=g^{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}^{r}c_{i},\;M^{r}_{22}=\bar{g}^{-k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}^{-r}c_{i}.

We assume, there exists
s\in\{1,\dots,k\}
such that
\lambda_{s}\neq\pm 1
. Then either
0<|\lambda_{s}|<1
or
0<|\lambda_{s}^{-1}|<1
. Without loss of generality, we assume
0<|\lambda_{s}|<1
. Since
K
is compact,
\{M^{r}\}\subseteq K
has a convergent subsequence
\{M^{r_{j}}\}\subseteq K
:

\lim M^{r_{j}}_{11}=\lim\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}^{r_{j}}c_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\hat{\lambda}_{i}c_{i}

where
\hat{\lambda}_{i}=\lim\lambda_{i}^{r_{j}}
. But
\hat{\lambda}_{s}=\lim\lambda_{s}^{r_{j}}=0
for any subsequence
\{r_{j}\}
. Therefore
\lim M^{r_{j}}_{11}
is not invertible and so
\lim M^{r_{j}}
is not invertible as well. Therefore, all
\lambda_{i}=\pm 1
and
g^{2}=1
. The element
L:=\begin{pmatrix}g&0\\
0&g^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\in\KSp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})\subset K
. Then
ML=\begin{pmatrix}1&x\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\in K
. Take
(ML)^{r}=\begin{pmatrix}1&rx\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\in K
. This sequence does not have any convergent subsequence unless
x=0
. So we get
M=L\in\KSp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
. This contradicts the assumption
M\notin\KSp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
and we obtain that
\KSp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
is a maximal compact subgroup of
\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
.
∎

Corollary 3.24
.

Let
(A,\sigma)
be a real Hermitian algebra,
A_{\mathbb{C}}
be the complexification of
A
, and
\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}
be the complex linear extension of
\sigma
. The embedding

\Upsilon_{\mathbb{H}}\colon A_{\mathbb{H}}\to\Mat_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}})

from
2.2
maps
U_{(A_{\mathbb{H}},\sigma_{1})}
isomorphically to
\KSp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
.

Remark 3.25
.

Notice, that the group
\KSp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
is never compact because it is a complexification of the real group
\KSp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
.

3.4.
Realization of classical Lie groups as
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)

In the case when
(A,\sigma)
is a Hermitian algebra so that
(A^{\sigma},\circ)
is a Jordan algebra, the symplectic groups
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
are isomorphic to classical Hermitian Lie groups of tube type.
There are also other groups that can be realized as
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
for algebras with anti-involution which are not Hermitian.

(1)

Real symplectic group
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})

In order to realize the real symplectic group
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})
, we take

A=\Mat(n,\mathbb{R})
to the be algebra of
n\times n
matrices over
\mathbb{R}
and consider the involution
\sigma:A\to A
given by
\sigma(r)=r^{T}
,
r\in A
.

Then
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
is isomorphic to
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})
.
The maximal compact subgroup is

\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)=\Upsilon(\UU(n))\cong\UU(n).

(2)

Indefinite unitary group
\UU(n,n)

To realize the unitary group
\UU(n,n)
of an indefinite Hermitian form of signature
(n,n)
we consider
A=\Mat(n,\mathbb{C})
, and the involution
\bar{\sigma}:A\to A
given by
\bar{\sigma}(r)=\bar{r}^{T}
.
Then
\Sp_{2}(A,\bar{\sigma})
is isomorphic to
\UU(n,n)
. To see this, we notice that the standard Hermitian form
h
of signature
(n,n)
on
\mathbb{C}^{2n}
is given by
h(x,y):=i\omega(xT,yT)
where
T=\diag(\Id_{n},-i\Id_{n})
). The complexification
A_{\mathbb{C}}
is isomorphic to
\Mat(n,\mathbb{C})\times\Mat(n,\mathbb{C})
(see Section
B.1.1
). Therefore,

\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)\cong\UU(n)\times\UU(n).

Note that we cannot realize the special unitary group
\mathrm{SU}(n,n)
as
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

(3)

The group
\SO^{*}(4n)

The groups
\SO^{*}(2n)
are Hermitian Lie groups, but they are of tube type only if
n
is even.
In order to realize
\SO^{*}(4n)
as
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
we consider
A=\Mat(n,\mathbb{H})
and the involution
\sigma_{1}:A\to A
, given by
\sigma_{1}(r)=\bar{r}^{T}=\bar{\sigma}(r_{1})-\sigma(r_{2})j
for
r=r_{1}+r_{2}j
and
r_{1},r_{2}\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{C})
.
Then
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma_{1})
is isomorphic
\SO^{*}(4n)
(some authors also use the notation
\OO(2n,\mathbb{H})
) considered as the group of isometries of the quaternionic form
\beta
on
\mathbb{H}^{2n}
defined by

\beta(x,y)=\sum_{i=1}^{2n}\bar{x}_{i}jy_{i}=\bar{x}^{T}(\Id_{2n}j)y.

To see this, we notice that

\Id_{2n}j=\sigma_{1}(T)\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}T

for

T=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\Id_{n}&-\Id_{n}j\\
-\Id_{n}j&\Id_{n}\end{pmatrix}.

In this case
A_{\mathbb{C}}
is isomorphic to
\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})
(see Section
B.1.2
). Therefore.

\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)\cong\UU(2n).

Remark 3.26
.

The Hermitian Lie group of tube type
\SO_{0}(2,n)
cannot be realized in the same way as
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
. The reason is that the Jordan algebra
B_{n}
(see the classification
2.55
) cannot be seen as
A^{\sigma}
for an appropriate Hermitian algebra
(A,\sigma)
. Nevertheless, since
B_{n}
can be seen as Jordan subalgebra of an appropriate Clifford algebra, the group
\SO_{0}(2,n)
(or more precisely, its double cover
\Spin_{0}(2,n)
) can be realized as
\Sp_{2}
over some more complicated object which we do not discuss in this paper.

Remark 3.27
.

The exceptional Hermitian Lie group of tube type
E_{7}
cannot be realized as
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
. The reason is that the Jordan algebra
\Herm(3,\mathbb{O})
(see the classification
2.55
) cannot be embedded into any associative algebra, in particular it cannot be realized as
A^{\sigma}
for a Hermitian algebra
(A,\sigma)
.

Some other Lie groups can also be realized as
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
for algebras with anti-involution
(A,\sigma)
that is not Hermitian.

3.4.1.
Other examples

There are other interesting cases of classical groups that can be realized as
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
where
(A,\sigma)
is not Hermitian.

(1)

To realize the
symplectic group over any field
\mathbb{K}
we consider
A=\Mat(n,\mathbb{K})
with the anti-involution
\sigma(r):=r^{T}
. Then
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
is isomorphic to
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{K})
.

If
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{C}
,
A
is the complexification of
\Mat(n,\mathbb{R})
, and the maximal compact subgroup is

\KSp^{c}_{2}(A,\sigma)\cong\Sp(n).

(2)

The
indefinite symplectic group
can be realized as
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
. For this consider
A=\Mat(n,\mathbb{H})
with involution
\sigma_{0}(r)=\sigma(r_{1})+\bar{\sigma}(r_{2})j
for
r=r_{1}+r_{2}j
and
r_{1},r_{2}\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{C})
. Note that the algebra
(A,\sigma_{0})
is not Hermitian and also does not appear as a complexification of a Hermitian algebra.

Then
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma_{0})
is isomorphic
\Sp(n,n)
considered as the group of isometries of the quaternionic form
\omega
on
\mathbb{H}^{2n}
given by

\omega(x,y)=\sum_{i=1}^{2n}\bar{x}_{i}y_{i}=\bar{x}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}-\Id_{n}&0\\
0&\Id_{n}\end{pmatrix}y=

=k\left(\sigma_{0}(x)\begin{pmatrix}\Id_{n}k&0\\
0&-\Id_{n}k\end{pmatrix}y\right).

To see this, we notice that

\begin{pmatrix}\Id_{n}k&0\\
0&-\Id_{n}k\end{pmatrix}=\sigma_{0}(T)\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}T

for

T=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\Id_{n}&\Id_{n}k\\
\Id_{n}k&\Id_{n}\end{pmatrix}.

The maximal compact subgroup of
\Sp(n,n)
is
\Sp(n)\times\Sp(n)
. One can see this using the machinery developed in the Section
5.3
.

The subgroup
\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma_{0})
is isomorphic to
\GL_{n}(\mathbb{H})
which is not compact because
(A,\sigma_{0})
is not Hermitian.

4.
Space of isotropic lines

We assume
(A,\sigma)
to be a unital ring with an anti-involution. We denote by
\mathbb{P}(A^{2})
the space of lines in
A^{2}
, i.e.

\mathbb{P}(A^{2}):=\{xA\mid x\in A^{2}\text{ regular}\}.

The group
\Mat_{2}^{\times}(A)
acts on
\mathbb{P}(A^{2})
.

If
A^{2}
is equipped with the standard symplectic form
\omega
, we denote by
\mathbb{P}(\Is(\omega))
the space of all isotropic (with respect to
\omega
) lines:

\mathbb{P}(\Is(\omega))=\{xA\subset A^{2}\mid\omega(x,x)=0,\,x\text{ regular}\}.

This space is a closed subspace of
\mathbb{P}(A^{2})
. The group
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts on the space of isotropic lines.

4.1.
Space of lines as a homogeneous space

Now assume
\mathbb{K}
to be real closed field and
(A,\sigma)
is an algebra with an anti-involution over
\mathbb{K}
. We show that
\mathbb{P}(A^{2})
and
\mathbb{P}(\Is(\omega))
can be seen as homogeneous (even symmetric) spaces that is compact in case
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
(A,\sigma)
be Hermitian. The group
\UU_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts continuously and transitively on
\mathbb{P}(A^{2})
with

\Stab_{\UU_{2}(A,\sigma)}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}A=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}&0\\
0&u_{2}\end{pmatrix}\,\middle|\,u_{1},u_{2}\in U_{(A,\sigma)}\right\}\cong U_{(A,\sigma)}\times U_{(A,\sigma)}.

Proof.

Let
xA
be a line. Since
x\in A^{2}
is regular, by Proposition
3.15
, we can assume
b(x,x):=\sigma(x)^{T}x=1
. By Proposition
3.16
, there exists
y
such that
(x,y)
is an orthonormal basis, i.e. the matrix

\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&y_{1}\\
x_{2}&y_{2}\end{pmatrix}\in\UU_{2}(A,\sigma),

where
x=(x_{1},x_{2})^{T}
,
y=(y_{1},y_{2})^{T}
. Moreover

\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&y_{1}\\
x_{2}&y_{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}=x.

That means
\UU_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts transitively on
\mathbb{P}(A^{2})
.

Now compute the stabilizer of
(1,0)^{T}A
. Take
U:=\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&y_{1}\\
x_{2}&y_{2}\end{pmatrix}\in\UU_{2}(A,\sigma).
Since
U(1,0)=(a,0)
for some
a\in A
,
x_{2}=0
. Further

\Id_{2}=\sigma(U)^{T}U=\begin{pmatrix}\sigma(x_{1})&0\\
\sigma(y_{1})&\sigma(y_{2})\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&y_{1}\\
0&y_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\sigma(x_{1})x_{1}&\sigma(x_{1})y_{1}\\
\sigma(y_{1})x_{2}&\sigma(y_{1})y_{1}+\sigma(y_{2})y_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

Therefore,
\sigma(x_{1})x_{1}=1
, i.e.
x_{1}\in U_{(A,\sigma)}
. Further,
\sigma(x_{1})y_{1}=0
. Since
x_{1}\in U_{(A,\sigma)}
, it is invertible and so
y_{1}=0
. Finally,
\sigma(y_{1})y_{1}+\sigma(y_{2})y_{2}=\sigma(y_{2})y_{2}=1
. Therefore,
y_{2}\in U_{(A,\sigma)}
.
∎

Corollary 4.2
.

The space
\mathbb{P}(A^{2})
is homeomorphic to the quotient space:

\UU_{2}(A,\sigma)/U_{(A,\sigma)}\times U_{(A,\sigma)}

where the group
U_{(A,\sigma)}\times U_{(A,\sigma)}
is embedded into
\UU_{2}(A,\sigma)
diagonally.

In particular, if
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{P}(A^{2})
is compact and
\mathbb{P}(\Is(\omega))
is compact for any sesquilinear form
\omega\colon A^{2}\times A^{2}\to A
.

Corollary 4.3
.

For Hermitian algebras and complexifications of Hermitian algebras, we can describe the space
\mathbb{P}(\Is(\omega))
more precisely. Similarly to the Proposition
4.1
and Corollary
4.2
, one can prove the following two statements:

(1)

Let
(A,\sigma)
be Hermitian and
\omega
be the standard symplectic form on
A^{2}
. Then
\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts transitively on
\mathbb{P}(\Is(\omega))
with

\Stab_{\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}A=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}u&0\\
0&u\end{pmatrix}\,\middle|\,u\in U_{(A,\sigma)}\right\}\cong U_{(A,\sigma)}.

In particular,
\mathbb{P}(\Is(\omega))
is homeomorphic to the quotient space:

\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)/U_{(A,\sigma)}

where the group
U_{(A,\sigma)}
is embedded into
\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)
in the diagonal way.

(2)

Let
(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
be the complexification of a Hermitian algebra
(A,\sigma)
and
\omega
be the standard symplectic form on
A_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
. Then
\KSp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
acts transitively on
\mathbb{P}(\Is(\omega))
with

\Stab_{\KSp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}A=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}u&0\\
0&u\end{pmatrix}\,\middle|\,u\in U_{(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})}\right\}\cong U_{(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})}.

In particular,
\mathbb{P}(\Is(\omega))
is homeomorphic to the quotient space:

\KSp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})/U_{(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})}

where the group
U_{(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})}
is embedded into
\KSp^{c}_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
in the diagonal way.

Proposition 4.4
.

\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts transitively on
\mathbb{P}(\Is(\omega))
.

\Stab_{\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)}\left(\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}A\right):=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}x&xy\\
0&\sigma(x)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\,\middle|\,x\in A^{\times},y\in A^{\sigma}\right\}

\Stab_{\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)}\left(\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}A\right):=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}x&0\\
zx&\sigma(x)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\,\middle|\,x\in A^{\times},z\in A^{\sigma}\right\}

Proof.

\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts transitively on the space of isotropic lines since it acts transitively on
\Is(\omega)
.

We prove only the statement for the first stabilizer. The second one can be proved analogously.

Since

\begin{pmatrix}x&a\\
b&t\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}x\\
b\end{pmatrix},

x\in A^{\times}
and
b=0
. Furthermore,

\sigma\left(\begin{pmatrix}x&a\\
0&t\end{pmatrix}\right)^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}x&a\\
0&t\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\sigma(x)t\\
-\sigma(t)x&-\sigma(t)a+\sigma(a)t\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},

we obtain
t=\sigma(x)^{-1}
,
a=xy
for
y\in A^{\sigma}
.
∎

4.2.
Action of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
on pairs of isotropic lines

Let
(A,\sigma)
be a unital ring with an anti-involution.

Proposition 4.5
.

Two elements
u,v\in\Is(\omega)
are linearly independent if and only if, up to action of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
,
u=(1,0)^{T}
,
v=(a,b)^{T}
with
b\in A^{\times}
. Moreover, if
\omega(u,v)=1
, then
a\in A^{\sigma}
,
b=1
.

Proof.

\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts transitively on
\Is(\omega)
, therefore, up to
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
-action, we can assume that
u=(1,0)^{T}
. Since
u
and
v
are linearly independent,
b\in A^{\times}
. If
\omega(u,v)=1=b
, then
v=(a,1)^{T}
is isotropic, i.e.

\omega(v,v)=\sigma(a)-a=0

So
a\in A^{\sigma}
.
∎

Corollary 4.6
.

If
x,y\in\Is(\omega)
linearly independent, then
\omega(x,y)\in A^{\times}
.

Proposition 4.7
.

If
(x,y)
is a symplectic basis then there exists the unique
g\in\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
such that
g(1,0)^{T}=x
,
g(0,1)^{T}=y
. In particular,
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts transitively on symplectic bases.

Proof.

We can assume,
x=(1,0)^{T}
,
y=(a,1)^{T}
and
a\in A^{\sigma}
. Take
g:=\begin{pmatrix}1&a\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
, then
gx=x
,
gy=(0,1)^{T}
.
∎

Corollary 4.8
.

Let
xA
,
yA
be two transverse isotropic lines with
x,y\in\Is(\omega)
. Then there exist
M\in\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
and
y^{\prime}\in\Is(\omega)
such that
y^{\prime}A=yA
and
Mx=(1,0)^{T}
,
My^{\prime}=(0,1)^{T}
. In particular,
\omega(x,y^{\prime})=1
.

Proposition 4.9
.

\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts transitively on pairs of transverse isotropic lines.

\Stab_{\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)}\left(\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}A,\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}A\right):=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}x&0\\
0&\sigma(x)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\mid x\in A^{\times}\right\}\cong A^{\times}.

Proof.

By the Corollary
4.8
, every pair of transverse isotropic lines can be mapped to
((1,0)^{T}A,(0,1)^{T}A)
by an element of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
. So
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts transitively on pairs of transverse isotropic lines.

By the Proposition
4.4
,

\Stab_{\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)}\left(\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}A,\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}A\right)=

=\Stab_{\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)}\left(\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}A\right)\cap\Stab_{\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)}\left(\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}A\right)=

=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}x&0\\
0&\sigma(x)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\mid x\in A\right\}.\qed

4.3.
Action of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
on triples of isotropic lines

Let
(A,\sigma)
be a unital ring with an anti-involution. Let
(x_{1}A,x_{3}A,x_{2}A)
be a triple of pairwise transverse isotropic lines where all
x_{i}\in\Is(\omega)
. Because of transversality of
x_{1}A
and
x_{2}A
, we can assume
\omega(x_{1},x_{2})=1
. Up to action of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
, we can assume
x_{1}=(1,0)^{T}
,
x_{2}=(0,1)^{T}
. We can also normalize
x_{3}
so that
\omega(x_{1},x_{3})=1
. Then
x_{3}=(b,1)^{T}
,
b=\omega(x_{3},x_{2})\in(A^{\sigma})^{\times}
.

Proposition 4.10
.

Orbits of the action of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
on triples of pairwise transverse isotropic lines are in 1-1 correspondence with orbits of the following action of
A^{\times}
on
(A^{\sigma})^{\times}
:

\begin{array}[]{cccl}\psi\colon&A^{\times}\times(A^{\sigma})^{\times}&\mapsto&(A^{\sigma})^{\times}\\
&(a,b)&\mapsto&ab\sigma(a).\end{array}

Proof.

Let
(l_{1},l_{3},l_{2})
be a triple pairwise transverse of isotropic lines.
Up to
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
-action, we can assume
l_{i}=x_{i}A
for
x_{1}=(1,0)^{T}
,
x_{2}=(0,1)^{T}
,
x_{3}=(1,b)^{T}
with
b\in(A^{\sigma})^{\times}
. The stabilizer
\Stab_{\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)}((1,0)^{T}A,(0,1)^{T}A)\cong A^{\times}
acts on
x_{3}
in the following way:

\diag(a,\sigma(a)^{-1})x_{3}=(ab,\sigma(a)^{-1})^{T}=(ab\sigma(a),1)^{T}a^{-1}

i.e.
\diag(a,\sigma(a)^{-1})(b,1)^{T}A=(ab\sigma(a),1)^{T}A

So we see that in the orbit of
(b,1)^{T}A
are exactly all isotropic lines of the form
(b^{\prime},1)^{T}A
where
b^{\prime}
is from the orbit of
b
under
\psi
.
∎

Definition 4.11
.

Let
(A,\sigma)
is a semisimple Hermitian algebra over a real closed field such that
A^{\sigma}
is a simple Jordan algebra. The
Kashiwara-Maslov index
of a triple of pairwise transverse isotropic lines
(l_{1},l_{3},l_{2})
is a signature of an element
b\in A^{\sigma}
such that up to the action of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
,
l_{i}=x_{i}A
,
x_{1}=(1,0)^{T}
,
x_{2}=(0,1)^{T}
,
x_{3}=(b,1)^{T}
.

We call a triple
(l_{1},l_{3},l_{2})
of isotropic lines
positive
if its Kashiwara-Maslov index is
(n,0)
where
n
is the rank of
A^{\sigma}
. (or equivalently a corresponding element
b
is in
A^{\sigma}_{+}
.

By the Sylvester’s law of inertia
2.77
, the action
\psi
form
4.10
preserves the signature. Therefore, the Kashiwara-Maslov index is well defined for every triple of pairwise transverse isotropic lines. Moreover, because of
2.65
, the positivity of a triple is well defined for every semisimple Hermitian algebra (not only if
A^{\sigma}
is a simple Jordan algebra).

The following properties of the Kashiwara-Maslov index are well known (see
[
18
, Section 1.5]
,
[
8
, Section 5]
):

Proposition 4.12
.

(1)

The Kashiwara-Maslov index
\mu
is alternating and invariant for the diagonal action of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
on the space of transverse triples of isotropic lines;

(2)

\mu
takes values
\{-n,-n+2,\dots,n\}
where
n
is the rank of the Jordan algebra
A^{\sigma}
;

(3)

\mu
is a cocycle: for 4-tuple
(l_{1},l_{2},l_{3},l_{4})
of pairwise transverse isotropic lines, we have

\mu(l_{2},l_{3},l_{4})-\mu(l_{1},l_{3},l_{4})+\mu(l_{1},l_{2},l_{4})-\mu(l_{1},l_{2},l_{3})=0.

Proposition 4.13
.

If
(A,\sigma)
a semisimple Hermitian algebra over a real closed field, then
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts transitively on positive triples of isotropic lines.

The stabilizer of the positive triple

\left(\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}A,\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\end{pmatrix}A,\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}A\right)

in
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
coincides with the following subgroup:

\hat{U}:=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}u&0\\
0&u\end{pmatrix}\,\middle|\,u\in U_{(A,\sigma)}\right\}\cong U_{(A,\sigma)}.

The stabilizer of every positive triple of isotropic lines is conjugate in
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
to
\hat{U}
.

Proof.

This follows by a direct computation from
2.65
and
4.10
.
∎

Proposition 4.14
.

If
(A,\sigma)
a semisimple Hermitian algebra over a real closed field such that
A^{\sigma}
is a simple Jordan algebra of rank
n
, then
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts transitively on triples of isotropic lines with a fixed Kashiwara-Maslov index.

Let
p,q\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
with
p+q=n
. Let
(e_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
be a fixed Jordan frame in
A^{\sigma}
and
o_{p,q}:=\sum_{i=1}^{p}e_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{q}e_{p+i}
. The stabilizer of the triple

\left(\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}A,\begin{pmatrix}o_{p,q}\\
1\end{pmatrix}A,\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}A\right)

in
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
coincides with the following subgroup:

\hat{U}(o_{p,q}):=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}u&0\\
0&u\end{pmatrix}\,\middle|\,\sigma(u)o_{p,q}u=o_{p,q}\right\}.

Proof.

This follows by a direct computation from
2.77
and
4.10
.
∎

Finally, we consider the complexification
(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
of a Hermitian algebra
(A,\sigma)
. In this case, the action of
\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
on triples of isotropic
A_{\mathbb{C}}
-lines is transitive if the Jordan algebra
A^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}}
is simple.

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
be the complexification of a Hermitian algebra
(A,\sigma)
over a real closed field such that the Jordan algebra
A^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}}
is simple. The group
\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
acts transitively on triples of isotropic
A_{\mathbb{C}}
-lines.

The stabilizer of the triple

\left(\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}A,\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\end{pmatrix}A,\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}A\right)

in
\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
coincides with the following subgroup:

\hat{U}_{\mathbb{C}}:=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}u&0\\
0&u\end{pmatrix}\,\middle|\,u\in U_{(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})}\right\}\cong U_{(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})}

The stabilizer of every positive triple of isotropic lines is conjugate in
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
to
\hat{U}
.

Proof.

This follows from
2.91
and
4.10
.
∎

4.4.
Action of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
on quadruples of isotropic lines – the cross ratio

Let
(A,\sigma)
be a unital ring with an anti-involution. We consider the following subspace of
A
:

A_{0}:=\{bb^{\prime}\mid b,b^{\prime}\in(A^{\sigma})^{\times}\}.

A^{\times}
acts on
A_{0}
by conjugation because for
b,b^{\prime}\in(A^{\sigma})^{\times}
,
a\in A^{\times}
:

a(bb^{\prime})a^{-1}=(ab\sigma(a))(\sigma(a)^{-1}b^{\prime})a^{-1}\in A_{0}.

Remark 4.16
.

It is a well-known fact from linear algebra that for matrix algebras
A
over
\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{C}
or
\mathbb{H}
, we always have
A_{0}=A^{\times}
.

Proposition 4.17
.

Orbits of the action of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
on quadruples of pairwise transverse isotropic lines are in 1-1 correspondence with orbits of the following action of
A^{\times}
on
A_{0}
:

\begin{array}[]{cccl}\eta\colon&A^{\times}\times A_{0}&\mapsto&A_{0}\\
&(a,b)&\mapsto&aba^{-1}.\end{array}

Proof.

Let
(l_{1},l_{3},l_{2},l_{4})
be a quadruple pairwise transverse of isotropic lines. Then up to action of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
, we can assume
l_{1}=(1,0)^{T}A
,
l_{2}=(0,1)^{T}A
,
l_{3}=(b,1)^{T}A
,
l_{3}=(1,b^{\prime})^{T}A
with
b,b^{\prime}\in(A^{\sigma})^{\times}
. Consider the action of the stabilizer of
(l_{1},l_{2})
:

\diag(a,\sigma(a)^{-1})(b,1)^{T}A=(ab\sigma(a),1)A,

\diag(a,\sigma(a)^{-1})(1,b^{\prime})^{T}A=(1,\sigma(a)^{-1}b^{\prime}a^{-1})A.

We consider the map
(l_{1},l_{3},l_{2},l_{4})\mapsto bb^{\prime}\in A_{0}
. This map is well-defined, bijective and the action of the stabilizer of
(l_{1},l_{2})
(that is isomorphic to
A^{\times}
) induces the action of
A^{\times}
by conjugation on
A_{0}
. So we obtain that these two actions are isomorphic.
∎

Definition 4.18
.

The conjugacy class of
A_{0}
corresponding to the quadruple
(l_{1},l_{3},l_{2},l_{4})
of pairwise transverse isotropic lines is called the
cross ratio
of
(l_{1},l_{3},l_{2},l_{4})
.

4.5.
Examples of matrix algebras

In this section, we construct explicitly examples of spaces of isotropic lines for classical matrix algebras. We will use the following notation: for complex numbers, we write
\mathbb{C}\{I\}
to emphasize that the imaginary unit is denoted by
I
. Similarly, for quaternions, we write
\mathbb{H}\{I,J,K\}
to emphasize that the imaginary units are denoted by
I
,
J
,
K
. The multiplication rule is then
IJ=K
.

Example 4.19
.

Let
(A,\sigma)
be
(\Mat(n,\mathbb{R}),\sigma)
,
(\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}),\sigma)
,
(\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}),\bar{\sigma})
or
(\Mat(n,\mathbb{H}),\bar{\sigma})
where
\sigma
is the transposition,
\bar{\sigma}
the composition of transposition and complex/quaternionic conjugation.

Every regular element of
x\in A^{2}
can be seen as a
2n\times n
-matrix of maximal rank. Columns of this matrix are elements of
\mathbb{K}^{2n}
considered as a right
\mathbb{K}
-module where
\mathbb{K}
is
\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{C}
or
\mathbb{H}
. If we take the
\mathbb{K}
-span of this columns, we obtain
n
-dimensional submodule of
\mathbb{K}^{2n}
denoted by
\Span_{\mathbb{K}}(x)
. It is easy to see that the map:

\begin{array}[]{cccl}L\colon&\mathbb{P}(A^{2})&\to&\Gr(n,\mathbb{K}^{2n})\\
&xA&\mapsto&\Span_{\mathbb{K}}(x)\end{array}

where
\Gr(n,\mathbb{K}^{2n})
is the space of all
n
-dimensional submodules of
\mathbb{K}^{2n}
is a bijection.

We consider the following form (bilinear or sesquilinear depending on
\sigma
) on
\mathbb{K}^{2n}
:

\tilde{\omega}(u,v):=\sigma(u)\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}v

for
u,v\in\mathbb{K}^{2n}
. Then
x\in\Is(\omega)
if and only if
\Span_{\mathbb{K}}(x)
is isotropic with respect to
\tilde{\omega}
, that means for all
u,v\in\Span_{\mathbb{K}}(x)
,
\tilde{\omega}(u,v)=0
. So we obtain that
L
maps bijectively isotropic lines of
A^{2}
to isotropic
n
-dimensional submodules of
\mathbb{K}^{2n}
. Such submodules are called
Lagrangian
with respect to
\tilde{\omega}
. The space of all Lagrangian with respect to
\tilde{\omega}
submodules are denoted by
\Lag(\mathbb{K}^{2n},\tilde{\omega})
.

Example 4.20
.

Let
A=\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\otimes\mathbb{C}\{i\}
with the anti-involution
\bar{\sigma}\otimes\Id
. We use the map
\chi:A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
(see Appendix
B.1.1
) to identify
A
with
A^{\prime}:=\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\times\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})
.
The anti-involution
\sigma^{\prime}:=\chi\circ(\bar{\sigma}\otimes\Id)\circ\chi^{-1}
induced by
\bar{\sigma}\otimes\Id

on
\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\times\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})
acts in the following way:

(m_{1},m_{2})\mapsto(m^{T}_{2},m^{T}_{1}).

The map
\chi
can be extended componentwise to the map

\chi^{\prime}\colon\Mat_{2}(A)\to\Mat_{2}(A^{\prime}).

Proposition 4.21
.

\Sp_{2}(A,\bar{\sigma}\otimes\Id)
is isomorphic to
\GL(2n,\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

First, we note that

A^{\bar{\sigma}\otimes\Id}=\Sym(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})+\Skew(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})I.

It is enough, to identify
\spp_{2}(A,\bar{\sigma}\otimes\Id)
and
\Mat_{2}(A_{\mathbb{R}})=\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})
as Lie algebras. First, we take the map
\chi^{\prime}
restricted to
\spp_{2}(A,\bar{\sigma}\otimes\Id)
:

\begin{matrix}\chi^{\prime}\colon&\spp_{2}(A,\sigma)&\to&\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\times\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\\
&\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}+a_{2}I&b_{1}+b_{2}I\\
c_{1}+c_{2}I&-a_{1}^{T}+a_{2}^{T}I\end{pmatrix}&\mapsto&\left(\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}+a_{2}i&b_{1}+b_{2}i\\
c_{1}+c_{2}i&-a_{1}^{T}+a_{2}^{T}i\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}-a_{2}i&b_{1}-b_{2}i\\
c_{1}-c_{2}i&-a_{1}^{T}-a_{2}^{T}i\end{pmatrix}\right).\end{matrix}

where
a_{1},a_{2}\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})
,
b_{1},c_{1}\in\Sym(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})
,
b_{2},c_{2}\in\Skew(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})
. This is an injective homomorphism of
\mathbb{C}\{i\}
-Lie algebras as restriction of injective map. Finally, we take a projection to the first component:

\pi_{1}\colon\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\times\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\to\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C}\{i\}).

Easy computation shows that
\pi_{1}\circ\chi^{\prime}
is an isomorphism.
∎

The set
(A^{\prime})^{2}
can be identified with the space of pairs
(x_{1},x_{2})^{T}
such that
x_{1},x_{2}\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})
. We define the sesquilinear form:

\omega((x_{1},x_{2})^{T},(y_{1},y_{2})^{T})=\sigma^{\prime}(x_{1},x_{2})\begin{pmatrix}0&(\Id_{n},\Id_{n})\\
-(\Id_{n},\Id_{n})&0\end{pmatrix}(y_{2},y_{2})^{T}=

=(\sigma(x_{2})\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}y_{1},\sigma(x_{1})\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}y_{2}).

Therefore,

\Is(\omega)=\{(l_{1},l_{2})\mid l=x_{1}\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\}),\,l_{2}=x_{2}\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\}),\,x_{1},x_{2}\text{ regular},\,\omega(x_{1},x_{2})=0\}.

Since
\omega
is non-degenerate,
l_{2}
is uniquely determined by
l_{1}
. Therefore, we can identify:

\Is(\omega)\cong\{x\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\mid x\text{ regular}\}.

As in the previous example, we can identify lines in
\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})^{2}
with Lagrangian subspaces of
(\mathbb{C}^{2n},\tilde{\omega})
where:

\tilde{\omega}(u,v)=u^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}v.

So the space
\Is(\omega)
can be identified with

\Is(\omega)\cong\{(l_{1},l_{2})\in\Gr(n,\mathbb{C}^{2n})^{2}\mid\tilde{\omega}(u,v)=0\text{ for all }u\in l_{1},\,v\in l_{2}\}.

The form
\tilde{\omega}
is a non-degenerate. Therefore, for
l\in\Gr(n,\mathbb{C}^{2n})
there exists exactly one
\tilde{\omega}
-orthogonal complement
l^{\bot}\in\Gr(n,\mathbb{C}^{2n})
such that for all
u\in l
,
v\in l^{\bot}
,
\tilde{\omega}(u,v)=0
. So we can identify

\Is(\omega)\cong\Gr(n,\mathbb{C}^{2n})

and
\GL(2n,\mathbb{C})
acts on
\Gr(n,\mathbb{C}^{2n})
in the standard way.

Example 4.22
.

Let
A=\Mat(n,\mathbb{H}\{i,j,k\})\otimes\mathbb{C}\{I\}
with the anti-involution
\bar{\sigma}\otimes\Id
. We use the map
\psi:A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
(see Appendix
B.1.2
) to identify
A
with
A^{\prime}:=\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})
. The anti-involution
\sigma^{\prime}:=\psi\circ(\bar{\sigma}\otimes\Id)\circ\psi^{-1}
on
A^{\prime}
induced by
\bar{\sigma}\otimes\Id

on
\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})
acts in the following way:

m\mapsto-\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id\\
-\Id&0\end{pmatrix}m^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id\\
-\Id&0\end{pmatrix}.

We define the following
\sigma^{\prime}
-sesquilinear form on
(A^{\prime})^{2}
: for
x,y\in(A^{\prime})^{2}

\omega(x,y)=\sigma^{\prime}(x)^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{2n}\\
-\Id_{2n}&0\end{pmatrix}y.

Proposition 4.23
.

\Sp_{2}(A,\bar{\sigma}\otimes\Id)
is isomorphic to
\OO(4n,\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

M\in\Sp_{2}(A^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})\cong\Sp_{2}(A,\bar{\sigma}\otimes\Id)
if and only if

\sigma^{\prime}(M)^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{2n}\\
-\Id_{2n}&0\end{pmatrix}M=\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{2n}\\
-\Id_{2n}&0\end{pmatrix},

i.e.

-\begin{pmatrix}\begin{matrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{matrix}&0\\
0&\begin{matrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{matrix}\end{pmatrix}M^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\begin{matrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{matrix}&0\\
0&\begin{matrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{matrix}\end{pmatrix}\cdot

\cdot\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{2n}\\
-\Id_{2n}&0\end{pmatrix}M=\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{2n}\\
-\Id_{2n}&0\end{pmatrix}

This is equivalent to:

M^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\begin{matrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{matrix}\\
\begin{matrix}0&-\Id_{n}\\
\Id_{n}&0\end{matrix}&0\end{pmatrix}M=\begin{pmatrix}0&\begin{matrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{matrix}\\
\begin{matrix}0&-\Id_{n}\\
\Id_{n}&0\end{matrix}&0\end{pmatrix}.

So the group
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
is the group of symmetries of the symmetric bilinear form form

\begin{pmatrix}0&\begin{matrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{matrix}\\
\begin{matrix}0&-\Id_{n}\\
\Id_{n}&0\end{matrix}&0\end{pmatrix}

on
\mathbb{C}^{4n}
. But all symmetric bilinear forms on
\mathbb{C}^{4n}
are conjugated. Therefore,
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
is isomorphic to
\OO(4n,\mathbb{C})
.
∎

Note that
\Is(\omega)=\Is(\omega^{\prime})
for

\omega^{\prime}(x,y):=x^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\begin{matrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{matrix}\\
\begin{matrix}0&-\Id_{n}\\
\Id_{n}&0\end{matrix}&0\end{pmatrix}y.

As before, we can identify lines in
(A^{\prime})^{2}
with the space
\Gr(2n,\mathbb{C}^{4n})
of
2n
-dimensional subspaces of
\mathbb{C}^{4n}
using the map
L
(see Example
4.19
). Under this map, the space
\Is(\omega)
goes to the space of all maximal
\tilde{\omega}
-isotropic subspaces where

\tilde{\omega}(u,v)=u^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\begin{matrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{matrix}\\
\begin{matrix}0&-\Id_{n}\\
\Id_{n}&0\end{matrix}&0\end{pmatrix}v

for
x,y\in\mathbb{C}^{4n}
. The group
\OO(\tilde{\omega})\cong\OO(4n,\mathbb{C})
acts on the space of all maximal
\tilde{\omega}
-isotropic subspaces in the standard way.

5.
Models for the symmetric space of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
over Hermitian algebras

The goal of this Chapter is to construct different models of the symmetric space for
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
for a real Hermitian algebra
(A,\sigma)
. In the case when
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
is a classical Hermitian Lie group of tube type, we recover many aspects of their well-known structure theory. We refer the reader to
[
21
,
16
]
for standard works on Hermitian Lie groups and their symmetric spaces.

In this Chapter, we discuss only groups
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
for semisimple Hermitian algebras
(A,\sigma)
over the field
\mathbb{R}
. In this case, the corresponding symmetric spaces become naturally Riemannian manifolds. Actually, this construction works also for every real closed field. Although in general the topology a real closed field does not allow to define a structure of a Riemannian manifold on the corresponding symmetric spaces, we still have some “Riemannian structure” on them. For example, a
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
-invariant
\mathbb{K}
-valued Riemannian metric is well-defined. Moreover, as we will see, all the models we are going to describe in this Chapter are semi-algebraic sets that are semi-algebraically isomorphic to each other. This allows us to define a natural semi-algebraic structure on symmetric spaces for
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
for every semisimple Hermitian algebra
(A,\sigma)
over any real closed field
\mathbb{K}
.

5.1.
Complex structures model

The first model we construct is the complex structures model.

Definition 5.1
.

A
complex structure
on a right
A
-module
V
is an
A
-linear map
J\colon V\to V
such that
J^{2}=-\Id
.

Let
V=A^{2}
and
\omega
be the standard symplectic form on
A^{2}
. For every complex structure
J
on
A^{2}
, we define the following
\sigma
-sesquilinear form

\begin{matrix}h_{J}\colon&A^{2}\times A^{2}&\to&A\\
&(x,y)&\mapsto&\omega(J(x),y)\end{matrix}

We remind the definition of a
\sigma
-inner product:

Definition 5.2
.

A
\sigma
-sesquilinear form
h
on
(A^{2},\omega)
is called
\sigma
-inner product
if
h
is
\sigma
-symmetric and for all regular
v\in A^{2}
,
h(v,v)\in A^{\sigma}_{+}
.

We define the
complex structures model
by setting

\mathfrak{C}:=\left\{J\text{ complex structure on $A^{2}$}\mid h_{J}\text{ is an $\sigma$-inner product}\right\}.

We show now that
\mathfrak{C}
is a model of the symmetric space of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
J\in\mathfrak{C}
and
w\in\Is(\omega)
, then
J(w)\in\Is(\omega)
.

Proof.

For
w\in\Is(\omega)
,

\omega(J(w),J(w))=h_{J}(w,J(w))=\sigma(h_{J}(J(w),w))=\sigma(\omega(w,w))=0,

therefore,
J(w)\in\Is(\omega)
.
∎

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
J
be a complex structure on
A^{2}
. Then
J\in\mathfrak{C}
if and only if there exists
w\in\Is(\omega)
such that
(J(w),w)
is a symplectic basis of
A^{2}
.

Proof.

1. Let
J\in\mathfrak{C}
and
w^{\prime}\in\Is(\omega)
. Since
h_{J}(w^{\prime},w^{\prime})=b\in A^{\sigma}_{+}
, we can take
w:=w^{\prime}b^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, then
h_{J}(w,w)=1
. Then:

\omega(J(w),J(w))=h_{J}(w,J(w))=\sigma(h_{J}(J(w),w))=\sigma(\omega(w,w))=0,

\omega(J(w),w)=h_{J}(w,w)=1.

Therefore,
(J(w),w)
is a symplectic basis of
A^{2}
.

2. Let
w\in A^{2}
such that
(J(w),w)
is a symplectic basis of
A^{2}
. Then,

h_{J}(w,w)=\omega(J(w),w)=1

h_{J}(J(w),J(w))=\omega(J^{2}(w),J(w))=-\omega(w,J(w))=1,

h_{J}(J(w),w)=\omega(J^{2}(w),w)=-\omega(w,w)=0.

Therefore,
(J(w),w)
is an orthonormal basis for
h_{J}
and in this basis
h_{J}
is the standard
\sigma
-inner product, so
h_{J}
is an
\sigma
-inner product.
∎

Definition 5.5
.

The
standard complex
structure on
A^{2}
is the map

\begin{matrix}J_{0}\colon&A^{2}&\to&A^{2}\\
&(x,y)&\mapsto&(y,-x)\end{matrix}

Theorem 5.6
.

\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts on
\mathfrak{C}
by conjugation. This action is transitive. The stabilizer of the standard complex structure is
\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

In particular,
\mathfrak{C}\cong\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)/\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)
is a model of the symmetric space of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

Proof.

1. First, we prove that
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts on
\mathfrak{C}
by conjugation. Let
J\in\mathfrak{C}
,
g\in\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
. Consider
J^{\prime}:=g^{-1}Jg
.
(J^{\prime})^{2}=g^{-1}J^{2}g=-\Id
so
J^{\prime}
is a complex structure on
A^{2}
. For
x\in\Is(\omega)
,
g(x)\in\Is(\omega)
and we obtain

h_{J^{\prime}}(x,x)=\omega(J^{\prime}(x),x)=\omega(g^{-1}Jg(x),x)=

=\omega(Jg(x),g(x))=h_{J}(g(x),g(x))\in A^{\sigma}_{+}.

Therefore,
h_{J^{\prime}}
is a
\sigma
-inner product on
A^{2}
, i.e.
J^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}
.

2. Second, we prove that this action is transitive. Let
J\in\mathfrak{C}
, take a symplectic basis
(J(w),w)
from the Proposition
5.4
. Since
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts transitively on symplectic bases, there exists
g\in\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
which maps the standard symplectic basis to
(J(w),w)
. That means,
g
maps the standard complex structure
J_{0}
to
J
. So the action is transitive.

3. Finally, compute the stabilizer of
J_{0}
.
g\in\Stab_{\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)}(J_{0})
if and only if

g\in\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
and
g\in\OO(h_{J_{0}})=\UU_{2}(A,\sigma)
, i.e.

g\in\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)\cap\UU_{2}(A,\sigma)=\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma).\qed

5.2.
Upper half-space model

Now we describe the upper half-space model that generalizes the well-known upper half-plane model for
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
.

We denote as before by
A_{\mathbb{C}}
the complexification of
A
, i.e.
A_{\mathbb{C}}:=A\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
. We extend
\sigma
to
A_{\mathbb{C}}
complex linearly, i.e.
\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}(x+yi):=\sigma(x)+\sigma(y)i
.

Every element of
z\in A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}
can be uniquely written as
z=x+yi
where
x,y\in A^{\sigma}
. We denote by
\Ree(z):=x
,
\Imm(z):=y
. We also have a complex conjugation on
A_{\mathbb{C}}
given by
\bar{z}=x-yi
.

Definition 5.7
.

The
upper half-space
is

\mathfrak{U}:=\{z\in A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}\mid\Imm(z)\in A^{\sigma}_{+}\}

Proposition 5.8
.

The group
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts transitively on
\mathfrak{U}
via
Möbius transformations

z\mapsto M.z=(az+b)(cz+d)^{-1}\text{, where }M=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}.

The stabilizer of
1i
is
\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

In particular,
\mathfrak{U}
is a model of the symmetric space of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

Proof.

First, we show that the action is well defined.
Since
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
is generated by matrices

\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&\sigma(a)^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1&b\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}

where
a\in A^{\times}
,
b\in A^{\sigma}
, we proof that
M.z\in\mathfrak{U}
for all
z\in\mathfrak{U}
on these generators.

If
M:=\begin{pmatrix}1&b\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
with
b\in A^{\sigma}
, then
M.z=z+b\in A^{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\Imm(M.z)=\Imm(z)\in A^{\sigma}_{+}
.

If
M:=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}
,
then
M.z=-z^{-1}\in A^{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}
. If
z=x+iy
, then

z^{-1}=y^{-1}x(y+xy^{-1}x)^{-1}-i(y+xy^{-1}x)^{-1},

i.e.
\Imm(M.z)=(y+xy^{-1}x)^{-1}
. For
y\in A^{\sigma}_{+}
, also
y^{-1}\in A^{\sigma}_{+}
.

Let
y^{-1}=\sigma(p)p
for some
p\in A^{\times}
, then

y+xy^{-1}x=y+\sigma(px)px\in A^{\sigma}_{+}.

Therefore,
\Imm(M.z)=(y+xy^{-1}x)^{-1}\in A^{\sigma}_{+}.

If
M:=\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&\sigma(a)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

for
a\in A^{\times}
, then
M.z=az\sigma(a)\in A^{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}
because
A^{\sigma}
is closed by action of
A^{\times}
.
\Imm(M.z)=a\Imm(z)\sigma(a)\in A^{\sigma}_{+}
because
A^{\sigma}_{+}
is closed by action of
A^{\times}
.

Now, we check the transitivity.
Let
z=x+yi\in\mathfrak{U}
then
y=u^{2}
for some
u\in(A^{\sigma})^{\times}
. Then

\pi\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&x\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}u&0\\
0&u^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\right)=\pi\left(\begin{pmatrix}u&xu^{-1}\\
0&u^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\right)=x+yi=z

Finally, let us find the stabilizer of
1i
.

M=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}

stabilizes
1i
if and only if

1i=M.1i=(ai+b)(ci+d)^{-1}=(ai+b)(-c+di)^{-1}i.

So,
a=d
and
c=-b
, i.e.
M\in\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.
∎

5.3.
Symmetric space of
\OO(h)
for an indefinite form
h

In order to describe other models of the symmetric space of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
, we consider the following
\sigma
-sesquilinear
\sigma
-symmetric forms on
A^{2}
:

Definition 5.9
.

A
\sigma
-sesquilinear
\sigma
-symmetric form
h
on
A^{2}
such that there exist a basis
(e_{1},e_{2})
of
A^{2}
such that
h(e_{1},e_{1})=-1
,
h(e_{2},e_{2})=1
,
h(e_{1},e_{2})=0
is called
indefinite
.

The
standard indefinite form

h_{st}
is the
\sigma
-sesquilinear
\sigma
-symmetric form on
A^{2}
given by the matrix
\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
in the standard basis
((1,0)^{T},(0,1)^{T})
of
A^{2}
.

We define the group of symmetries of
h
by :

\OO(h):=\{g\in\Aut(A^{2})\mid h(gx,gy)=h(x,y)\text{ for all }x,y\in A^{2}\},

and set

\mathcal{X}_{\OO(h)}:=\{xA\mid x\in A^{2}\text{ such that }h(x,x)\in A^{\sigma}_{+}\},\;\;\mathcal{X}:=\mathcal{X}_{\OO(h_{st})}.

Remark 5.10
.

The space
\mathcal{X}_{\OO(h)}
is well defined because if
xA=yA
, i.e. there exists
a\in A^{\times}
such that
y=xa
, then

h(y,y)=\sigma(a)h(x,x)a=\sigma(a)\sigma(p)pa=\sigma(pa)pa\in A^{\sigma}_{+}

where
p\in A^{\times}
,
\sigma(p)p=h(x,x)\in A^{\sigma}_{+}
.

Remark 5.11
.

Since
\Aut(A^{2})
acts transitively on bases of
A^{2}
, all
\OO(h)
are isomorphic for indefinite
h
. Therefore, all
\mathcal{X}_{\OO(h)}
are also isomorphic.

Proposition 5.12
.

\OO(h_{st})
acts transitively on
\mathcal{X}
with stabilizer of
(0,1)^{T}A
equal to
U_{(A,\sigma)}\times U_{(A,\sigma)}
diagonally embedded into
\OO(h_{st})
.

Proof.

Since
h_{st}((0,1)^{T},(0,1)^{T})=1\in A^{\sigma}_{+}
, the line
(0,1)^{T}A\in\mathcal{X}
. Let
vA\in\mathcal{X}
for some
v\in A^{2}
. Since
h_{st}(v,v)\in A^{\sigma}_{+}
, there exists
p\in A
such that
h_{st}(v,v)=\sigma(p)p
. Let
v^{\prime}:=vp^{-1}
, then
h(v^{\prime},v^{\prime})=1
and
v^{\prime}A=vA
.

Consider the vector

v^{\prime}:=(x,\sigma(v_{2})^{-1}\sigma(v_{1})x)^{T}

where
v=(v_{1},v_{2})^{T}
,
x=(1+v_{1}\sigma(v_{1}))^{\frac{1}{2}}
. Then an easy calculation shows that
h(v^{\prime},v^{\prime})=-1
and
h(v,v^{\prime})=0
. So we can take the following matrix
M:=(v^{\prime},v)\in\OO(h_{st})
. Since
M(0,1)^{T}=v
, we obtain
M(0,1)^{T}A=vA
, i.e.
\OO(h_{st})
acts transitively on
\mathcal{X}
.

Now, we compute the stabilizer of
(0,1)^{T}A
. Let

M:=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\OO(h_{st})

stabilize
(0,1)^{T}A
, then
M(0,1)^{T}=(b,d)
, i.e.
b=0
. Moreover

\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\sigma(a)&\sigma(c)\\
0&\sigma(d)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-\sigma(a)&\sigma(c)\\
0&\sigma(d)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}=

=\begin{pmatrix}-\sigma(a)a+\sigma(c)c&\sigma(c)d\\
0&\sigma(d)d\end{pmatrix}.

Therefore,
\sigma(d)d=1
, i.e.
d
is invertible. So we obtain
c=0
and
\sigma(a)a=1
, i.e.
a,d\in U_{(A,\sigma)}
.
∎

Proposition 5.13
.

The group
U_{(A,\sigma)}\times U_{(A,\sigma)}
is diagonally embedded into
\OO(h_{st})
as a maximal compact subgroup of
\OO(h_{st})
.

In particular,
\mathcal{X}
is a model of the symmetric space of
\OO(h_{st})
.

Proof.

First, note that the Lie algebra of
\OO(h_{st})
is:

\oo(h_{st})=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
\sigma(b)&d\end{pmatrix}\,\middle|\,\sigma(a)=-a\in A,\sigma(d)=-d\in A,b\in A\right\}.

Assume,
K
is compact subgroup of
\OO(h_{st})
that contains
U_{(A,\sigma)}\times U_{(A,\sigma)}
as a proper subgroup. Then
\Lie(K)
contains an element

\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
\sigma(b)&d\end{pmatrix}
with
b\neq 0
. Therefore,

x:=\begin{pmatrix}0&b\\
\sigma(b)&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
\sigma(b)&d\end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&d\end{pmatrix}\in\Lie(K)

and

tx=\begin{pmatrix}0&tb\\
t\sigma(b)&0\end{pmatrix}\in\Lie(K)

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Take a polar decomposition of
b=uy
where
u\in U_{(A,\sigma)}
,
y\in A^{\sigma}
. We take the spectral decompositions of
y
:

y=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}c_{i}
where
(c_{i})
is a complete system of orthogonal idempotents,
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
.

Further,

x^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}b\sigma(b)&0\\
0&\sigma(b)b\end{pmatrix}.

Therefore,

b\sigma(b)=u\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}^{2}c_{i}u^{-1},\;\sigma(b)b=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}^{2}c_{i}

and

\exp(tx)=\begin{pmatrix}u\sum_{i=1}^{k}\cosh(t\lambda_{i})c_{i}u^{-1}&u\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sinh(t\lambda_{i})c_{i}\\
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sinh(t\lambda_{i})c_{i}u^{-1}&\sum_{i=1}^{k}\cosh(t\lambda_{i})c_{i}\end{pmatrix}\in K.

For
t
going to infinity,
\exp(xt)
does not converge even up to taking subsequence unless all
\lambda_{i}=0
. But this means that
b=0
, so we obtain
K=U_{(A,\sigma)}\times U_{(A,\sigma)}
. This contradicts the assumption that
U_{(A,\sigma)}\times U_{(A,\sigma)}
is a proper subgroup of
K
.
∎

Proposition 5.14
.

The map

\begin{array}[]{cccl}\Phi\colon&\mathcal{X}&\to&\mathring{D}(A,\sigma):=\{c\in A\mid 1-\sigma(c)c\in A^{\sigma}_{+}\}\\
&(a,b)^{T}A&\mapsto&ab^{-1}\end{array}

is a homeomorphism.

Proof.

Let
xA\in\mathcal{X}
then
x=(a,b)^{T}
with
-\sigma(a)a+\sigma(b)b\in A^{\sigma}_{+}
, i.e. there exists
p\in A^{\times}
such that

-\sigma(a)a+\sigma(b)b=\sigma(p)p.

Therefore,

\sigma(b)b=\sigma(p)p+\sigma(a)a\in A^{\sigma}_{+},

i.e.
b\in A^{\times}
. So for
c=ab^{-1}
,
xA=(c,1)^{T}A
. Moreover, for every line
xA\in\mathcal{X}
, the element
c\in A
such that
xA=(c,1)^{T}A
is well defined and
1-\sigma(c)c\in A^{\sigma}_{+}
.

For every
c\in\mathring{D}(A,\sigma)
, the line
(c,1)^{T}A\in\mathcal{X}
because

h_{st}((c,1)^{T},(c,1)^{T})=1-\sigma(c)c\in A^{\sigma}_{+}.

Therefore,
\Phi
is a homeomorphism.
∎

Corollary 5.15
.

\OO(h_{st})
acts on
\mathring{D}(A,\sigma)
via Möbius transformations

z\mapsto M.z=(az+b)(cz+d)^{-1}\text{, where }M=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}.

This transformation is called
Möbius transformation
.

Remark 5.16
.

Since
(A,\sigma)
is Hermitian, by Proposition
2.99
the domain
\mathring{D}(A,\sigma)
is precompact.

5.4.
Projective model

Now we use
\mathcal{X}
to construct the projective model of the symmetric space of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
. Let
A_{\mathbb{C}}=A\otimes\mathbb{C}
and denote by
\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}:A_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow A_{\mathbb{C}}
the
\mathbb{C}
-linear extension of
\sigma
, i.e.

\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}(x+iy)=\sigma(x)+i\sigma(y)

for every
x,y\in A
and by
\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}
the
\mathbb{C}
-antilinear extension of
\sigma
, i.e.

\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}(x+iy)=\sigma(x)-i\sigma(y)

for every
x,y\in A
.

As we have seen in the Corollary
2.80
,
(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
is Hermitian. We extend
\omega
in the following way:

\omega_{\mathbb{C}}(x,y):=\sigma(x)^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}y.

The following
\bar{\sigma}
-sesquilinear form is an indefinite form on
A_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
:

h(x,y):=\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}(x)^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&i\\
-i&0\end{pmatrix}y=i\omega_{\mathbb{C}}(\bar{x},y).

Indeed,

h(y,x)=\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}(y)^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&i\\
-i&0\end{pmatrix}x=\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}\left(\sigma_{1}(x)^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&i\\
-i&0\end{pmatrix}y\right)=\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}(h(x,y)).

Then in the basis
e_{1}:=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^{T}
,
e_{2}:=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},-\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^{T}
, the form
h
is represented by the matrix
\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
, i.e.
h
is a
\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}
-sesquilinear indefinite form on
A_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
.

Note,
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts on
A^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}
preserving
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
and
h
.

Definition 5.17
.

The space

\mathfrak{P}:=\{vA_{\mathbb{C}}\mid v\in\Is(\omega_{\mathbb{C}}),\;h(v,v)\in(A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\bar{\sigma}})_{+}\}=\Is(\omega_{\mathbb{C}})\cap\mathcal{X}_{\OO(h)}

is called the
projective model
.

To justify this Definition, we prove the following Proposition:

Proposition 5.18
.

The group
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts transitively on
\mathfrak{P}
with the stabilizer of
(i,1)^{T}A_{\mathbb{C}}
equal to
\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

In particular,
\mathfrak{P}
is a model of the symmetric space of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

Proof.

Let
v\in\Is(\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
such that
h(v,v)\in(A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\bar{\sigma}})_{+}
, so
vA_{\mathbb{C}}\in\mathfrak{P}
. We can renormalize
v
so that
h(v,v)=2
. We write
v=u+wi
, then
(w,u)
is a symplectic basis of
A^{2}
. Indeed,

2=h(v,v)=i\omega_{\mathbb{C}}(u-iw,u+iw)=i(\omega(u,u)+\omega(w,w)+i(\omega(u,w)-\omega(w,u)))=

=\omega(w,u)-\omega(u,w)+i(\omega(u,u)+\omega(w,w));

0=\omega_{\mathbb{C}}(v,v)=\omega(u,u)-\omega(v,v)+i(\omega(u,v)+\omega(w,u)).

Therefore,
\omega(u,u)=\omega(w,w)=0
and
\omega(w,u)=1
, i.e.
(w,u)
is a symplectic basis of
A^{2}
.

Since
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts transitively on symplectic bases of
A^{2}
,
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts transitively on
\mathfrak{P}
.

Now compute the stabilizer of
l_{0}:=(i,1)^{T}A_{\mathbb{C}}
. Let
M=(M_{ij})\in\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.
M(l_{0})=l_{0}
if and only if

M_{11}i+M_{12}=(M_{21}i+M_{22})i,

i.e.
M_{11}=M_{22}
,
M_{21}=-M_{12}
. This holds if and only if
M\in\UU_{2}(A,\sigma)\cap\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)=\KSp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.
∎

Definition 5.19
.

The space
\mathfrak{P}
is called the
the
