projective model
of the symmetric space of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

Finally, we describe the
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
-equivariant homeomorphism between the complex structure model
\mathfrak{C}
and the projective model
\mathfrak{P}
. For this, we extend every complex structure
J
on
A^{2}
to a complex structure
J_{\mathbb{C}}
on
A^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}
in the
\mathbb{C}
-linear way.

Proposition 5.20
.

For every complex structure
J\in\mathfrak{C}
, there exist regular
x,y\in A^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}
such that
J_{\mathbb{C}}(x)=xi
,
J_{\mathbb{C}}(y)=-yi
. Elements
x,y
are uniquely defined up to multiplication by elements of
A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
.

Proof.

Since
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts transitively on
\mathfrak{C}
, it is enough to prove the proposition for the standard complex structure
J_{0}
.

Since
J_{0}(a,b)^{T}=(b,-a)^{T},
we obtain
(b,-a)^{T}=(a,b)^{T}i
if and only if
b=ai
, i.e.

x=(a,ai)^{T}=(1,i)^{T}a,

where
a\in A_{\mathbb{C}}
arbitrary element. For
a\in A^{\times}
, x is regular.
Analogously,
y=(i,1)^{T}a
where
a\in A_{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
arbitrary element.
∎

For a complex structure
J\in\mathfrak{C}
, we denote by
l_{J}
the
A_{\mathbb{C}}
-line
yA_{\mathbb{C}}
such that
J_{\mathbb{C}}(y)=-yi
.

Corollary 5.21
.

The map

\begin{array}[]{cccl}F\colon&\mathfrak{C}&\to&\mathfrak{P}\\
&J&\mapsto&l_{J}\end{array}

defines is an
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
-equivariant homeomorphism.

5.5.
Precompact model

Lastly, we define the precompact model of the symmetric space of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

For this we consider the following
\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
-transformation that maps the indefinite form
h
introduced in the previous section to the standard indefinite form
h_{st}
:

T:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&i\\
i&1\end{pmatrix},

i.e.
\bar{\sigma}(T)^{T}[h]T=\diag(-1,1)=[h_{st}]
. Since
T\in\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
, it stabilizes the set
\Is(\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
.

Definition 5.22
.

The space

\mathfrak{B}:=\mathring{D}(A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}):=\{c\in A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}}\mid 1-\bar{c}c\in(A^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}})_{+}\}

is called the
precompact model
.

To justify this Definition, we prove the following Proposition:

Proposition 5.23
.

The map

\begin{array}[]{cccl}\Phi\colon&T^{-1}\mathfrak{P}&\to&\mathring{D}(A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})\\
&(a,b)^{T}A_{\mathbb{C}}&\mapsto&ab^{-1}\end{array}

is a homeomorphism. The set
\mathring{D}(A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
is precompact on
A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

In particular,
\mathring{D}(A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
is a model of the symmetric space of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

Proof.

Let
v=(v_{1},v_{2})^{T}\in\Is(\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
such that
vA_{\mathbb{C}}\in\mathfrak{P}
and
v=u+wi
where
(w,u)
is a symplectic basis of
(A^{2},\omega)
. Then:

2=h(v,v)=h_{st}(T^{-1}v,T^{-1}v)=-\bar{\sigma}(x_{1})x_{1}+\bar{\sigma}(x_{2})x_{2}\in A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{+}

where
T^{-1}v=:(x_{1},x_{2})^{T}
. Therefore,
\bar{\sigma}(x_{2})x_{2}=\bar{\sigma}(x_{1})x_{1}+2\in A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{+}
because
A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{+}
is a proper convex cone. This means that
x_{2}
is invertible, i.e.
x_{2}\in A^{\times}
,
(c,1)^{T}:=(x_{1}x_{2}^{-1},1)^{T}\in\Is(\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
and
(c,1)^{T}A_{\mathbb{C}}=(T^{-1}v)A_{\mathbb{C}}
.

(c,1)^{T}\in\Is(\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
if and only if
c\in A^{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}
, and
h((c,1)^{T},(c,1)^{T})=1-\bar{c}c\in A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{+}
. Therefore,
\Phi(T^{-1}v)\in\mathring{D}(A^{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma})
.

The map
\Phi
is injective because
T
is injective and, if
x_{1}x_{2}^{-1}=\Phi(x_{1},x_{2})^{T}=\Phi(y_{1},y_{2})^{T}=y_{1}y_{2}^{-1}
, then
(y_{1},y_{2})^{T}=(x_{1},x_{2})^{T}x_{2}^{-1}y_{2}
, i.e.
(x_{1},x_{2})^{T}A_{\mathbb{C}}=(y_{1},y_{2})^{T}A_{\mathbb{C}}
.

The map
\Phi
is surjective because for every
c\in\mathring{D}(A^{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma})
,
(c,1)^{T}A_{\mathbb{C}}=(T^{-1}v)A_{\mathbb{C}}
for
v:=T(c,1)^{T}\sqrt{2}(1-\bar{c}c)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. Then
v\in\Is(\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
and
h(v,v)=2
. Therefore,
v=u+wi
for
(w,u)
a symplectic basis of
(A^{2},\omega)
. Therefore,
vA_{\mathbb{C}}\in T^{-1}\mathfrak{P}
.

The set
\mathring{D}(A^{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma})
is precompact in
A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}}
because it is a subset of the following domain:

D(A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}):=\{a\in A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}}\mid 1-\bar{a}a\in(A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{\mathbb{C}})_{\geq 0}\}\subseteq A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}}

that is compact by Proposition
2.99
.
∎

Remark 5.24
.

The group
T^{-1}\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)T<\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\sigma_{\mathbb{C}})
acts on
\mathring{D}(A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
by Möbius transformations.

5.6.
Connection between models

In this section, we consider
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
-equivariant homeomorphisms between the projective model, the upper half-space model and precompact model of the symmetric space for
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

It is easy to check that the following map:

\begin{matrix}F\colon&\mathfrak{P}&\to&\mathfrak{U}\\
&(x_{1},x_{2})^{T}A_{\mathbb{C}}&\mapsto&x_{1}x_{2}^{-1}\end{matrix}

is an
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
-equivariant homeomorphism. As we have seen in the Proposition
5.23
, the map

\Phi\circ T^{-1}\colon\mathfrak{P}\to\mathring{D}(A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}):=\{c\in A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}}\mid 1-\bar{c}c\in(A^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}})_{+}\}.

defines another
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
-equivariant homeomorphism.

The maps
F
and
\Phi\circ T^{-1}
can be seen as different coordinate charts for the projective model
\mathfrak{P}
of the symmetric space for
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

5.7.
Compactification and Shilov boundary

In this section, we construct a natural compactification of the symmetric space of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

As we have seen, the precompact model
\mathring{D}(A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
is a precompact domain in
A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}}
, so
taking the topological closure of
\mathring{D}(A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
in
A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}}
, we obtain the compactification

D(A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}):=\{c\in A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}}\mid 1-\bar{c}c\in(A^{\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}})_{\geq 0}\}

of
\mathring{D}(A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
.

Definition 5.25
.

We call

\check{S}(A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}):=\{c\in A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}}\mid 1-\bar{c}c=0\}

the
Shilov boundary
of the precompact model
\mathring{D}(A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
.

Note, that

\check{S}(A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})=U_{(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})}\cap A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}}.

So the Shilpov boundary is a compact subspace of
D(A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
.

Remark 5.26
.

The map
\Phi^{-1}
extends to the boundary of
D(A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
and remains continuous and bijective. Since the boundary is compact, it is a homeomorphism. Therefore, we can see the boundary also in the projective model. In particular, we can see the Shilov boundary there.

The next Proposition describes the Shilov boundary in the projective model.

Proposition 5.27
.

The preimage of the Shilov boundary
\check{S}(A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
in
\mathbb{P}(\Is(\omega_{\mathbb{C}}))
under the map
\Phi\circ T^{-1}
gives a compact subset of the boundary of the projective model. It consists of all lines of the form
xA_{\mathbb{C}}
such that
x\in\Is(\omega)
regular.

Proof.

Note that the line
l\in\Is(\omega)
is of the form
xA_{\mathbb{C}}
for some
x\in\Is(\omega)
if and only if
\bar{l}=l
.

Assume
c\in\check{S}(A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
, i.e.
\bar{c}^{-1}=c
. Then

(\Phi\circ T^{-1})\overline{T\circ\Phi^{-1}(c)}=\Phi\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&i\\
i&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\bar{c}\\
1\end{pmatrix}\right)=\Phi\left(\begin{pmatrix}1\\
\bar{c}i\end{pmatrix}\right)=\bar{c}^{-1}=c

i.e. for
l=(c,1)^{T}A_{\mathbb{C}}
,
\bar{l}=l
.

If we take a line
xA_{\mathbb{C}}
for some
x=(x_{1},x_{2})^{T}\in\Is(\omega)
, then

c:=(\Phi\circ T^{-1})(xA_{\mathbb{C}})=(x_{1}-ix_{2})(-ix_{1}+x_{2})^{-1}.

Since
x\in\Is(\omega)
,
c\in A^{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}}

\bar{c}c=(x_{1}+ix_{2})(ix_{1}+x_{2})^{-1}(x_{1}-ix_{2})(-ix_{1}+x_{2})^{-1}=

=i(x_{1}+ix_{2})(x_{1}-ix_{2})^{-1}(x_{1}-ix_{2})(-ix_{1}+x_{2})^{-1}=

=i(x_{1}+ix_{2})(-ix_{1}+x_{2})^{-1}=(x_{1}+ix_{2})(x_{1}+ix_{2})^{-1}=1.

Therefore,
(\Phi\circ T^{-1})(xA)\in\check{S}(A^{\sigma_{\mathbb{C}}}_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma}_{\mathbb{C}})
.
∎

Corollary 5.28
.

The space
\mathbb{P}(\Is(\omega))
of isotropic lines of
(A^{2},\omega)
embedded into
\mathbb{P}(\Is(\omega_{\mathbb{C}}))
as:

xA\mapsto xA_{\mathbb{C}}

is a Shilov boundary in the projective model. This is a closed (even compact) orbit of the action of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
on the boundary of the projective model.

6.
Models for the symmetric space of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
over complexified algebras

The goal of this Chapter is to construct different models of the symmetric space for
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
where
A
is the complexification of a real Hermitian algebra.

To simplify the notation in this and the next chapters, we denote by
(A_{\mathbb{R}},\sigma_{\mathbb{R}})
a real Hermitian algebra. By
A=A_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
, we denote the complexification of
A_{\mathbb{R}}
. The complex linear extension of
\sigma_{\mathbb{R}}
is denoted by
\sigma
, the complex antilinear extension of
\sigma_{\mathbb{R}}
is denoted by
\bar{\sigma}
.

Similar to the approach for real Hermitian algebras, where we considered their complexification, we consider here quaternionifications of
A
(see Section
2.9
).

As we noticed in the previous chapter, this construction works for every real closed field. All the models we are going to describe are semi-algebraic sets, and they are semi-algebraically isomorphic to each other. This allows us to define a natural semi-algebraic structure on symmetric spaces of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
for a complexification of every semisimple Hermitian algebra
(A,\sigma)
over any real closed field
\mathbb{K}
.

6.1.
Quaternionic structures model

Let
(A_{\mathbb{R}},\sigma_{\mathbb{R}})
be a Hermitian algebra with anti-involution. We consider the complexification
A:=A_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
. As we have seen in Corollary
2.80
,
(A,\bar{\sigma})
is a Hermitian algebra.

Definition 6.1
.

A
quaternionic structure
on an right
A
-module
V
is an additive map
J\colon V\to V
such that
J^{2}=-\Id
and
J(xa)=J(x)\bar{a}
for all
x\in V
,
a\in A
.

Let
V=A^{2}
and
\omega
be the standard symplectic form on
A^{2}
. For every quaternionic structure
J
on
A^{2}
, we can define the form:

\begin{matrix}h_{J}\colon&A^{2}\times A^{2}&\to&A\\
&(x,y)&\mapsto&\omega(J(x),y)\end{matrix}

that is
\bar{\sigma}
-sesquilinear. Indeed, for
a_{1},a_{2}\in A

h_{J}(xa_{1},ya_{2})=\omega(J(xa_{1}),ya_{2})=\omega(J(x)\bar{a}_{1},ya_{2})=\bar{\sigma}(a_{1})h_{J}(x,y)a_{2}.

Definition 6.2
.

The space:

\mathfrak{C}:=\{J\text{ quaternionic structure on $A^{2}$}\mid h_{J}\text{\text{ is a $\bar{\sigma}$-inner product}\lx@text@rbrace}

is called
quaternionic structures model
.

We show that
\mathfrak{C}
is a model of the symmetric space of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

Definition 6.3
.

The
standard quaternionic structure
on
A^{2}
is the map

\begin{matrix}J_{0}\colon&A^{2}&\to&A^{2}\\
&(x,y)&\mapsto&(\bar{y},-\bar{x})\end{matrix}

Remark 6.4
.

h_{J_{0}}
is the standard
\bar{\sigma}
-inner product on
A^{2}
.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
J
be a quaternionic structure on
A^{2}
.
J\in\mathfrak{C}
if and only if there exists a regular isotropic
w\in A^{2}
such that
(J(w),w)
is a symplectic basis.

Proof.

1. Let
J\in\mathfrak{C}
and
w\in A^{2}
some regular isotropic element. Since
h_{J}(w,w)\in A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{+}
, we can normalize
w
so that
h_{J}(w,w)=1
. Then:

\omega(J(w),J(w))=h_{J}(w,J(w))=\bar{\sigma}(h_{J}(J(w),w))=\bar{\sigma}(\omega(w,w))=0,

\omega(J(w),w)=h_{J}(w,w)=1.

Therefore,
(J(w),w)
is a
\sigma
-symplectic basis.

2. Let
w\in A^{2}
and
(J(w),w)
is a
\sigma
-symplectic basis. Then,

h_{J}(w,w)=\omega(J(w),w)=1

h_{J}(J(w),J(w))=\omega(J^{2}(w),J(w))=\omega(J(w),w)=1,

h_{J}(J(w),w)=\omega(J^{2}(w),w)=-\omega(w,w)=0.

Therefore,
(w,J(w))
is an orthonormal basis for
h_{J}
, and in this basis,
h_{J}
is the standard
\sigma
-inner product, so
h_{J}
is an
\bar{\sigma}
-inner product.
∎

Corollary 6.6
.

For every
J\in\mathfrak{C}
, for every
v\in\Is(\omega)
,
J(v)\in\Is(\omega)
.

Theorem 6.7
.

\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts on
\mathfrak{C}
in the following way:

(g,J)\mapsto g^{-1}\circ J\circ g.

This action is transitive. The stabilizer of the standard quaternionic structure is
\KSp^{c}_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

In particular,
\mathfrak{C}
is a model of the symmetric space of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

Proof.

1. First, we prove that
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts on
\mathfrak{C}
by conjugation. Let
J\in\mathfrak{C}
,
g\in\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
. Consider
J^{\prime}:=g^{-1}\circ J\circ g
. Then

(J^{\prime})^{2}=g^{-1}\circ J\circ g\circ g^{-1}\circ J\circ g=-\Id.

So
J^{\prime}
is a quaternionic structure on
A^{2}
. For a regular
x\in A^{2}
,

h_{J^{\prime}}(x,x)=\omega(J^{\prime}(x),x)=\omega(g^{-1}Jg(x),x)=\omega(Jg(x),g(x))=

=h_{J}(g(x),g(x))\in A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{+}.

Therefore,
h_{J^{\prime}}
is an inner product on
A^{2}
, i.e.
J^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}
.

2. Second, we prove that the action is transitive. Let
J\in\mathfrak{C}
, take a symplectic basis
(J(w),w)
from the Proposition
6.5
. Since
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts transitively on symplectic bases, there exists
g\in\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
which maps the standard symplectic basis to
(J(w),w)
. That means,
g
maps the standard complex structure
J_{0}
to
J
. So the action is transitive.

3. Finally, compute the stabilizer of
J_{0}
.
g\in\Stab_{\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)}(J_{0})
if and only if

g\in\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
and
g\in\OO(h_{J_{0}})=\UU_{2}(A_{\mathbb{C}},\bar{\sigma})
, i.e.

g\in\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)\cap\UU_{2}(A,\bar{\sigma})=\KSp^{c}_{2}(A,\sigma).\qed

Remark 6.8
.

Since any quaternionic structure is a
\mathbb{C}
-antilinear map, if we write the action of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
in the matrix form, we need to add the complex conjugation: i.e. let
[J]
be the matrix for the quaternionic structure
J
, then

[g^{-1}\circ J\circ g]=g^{-1}[J]\bar{g}.

6.2.
Upper half-space model for
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)

Let
A_{\mathbb{R}}
be an Hermitian
\mathbb{R}
-algebra with an anti-involution
\sigma_{\mathbb{R}}
. We assume
A:=A_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\{I\}
to be the complexification of
A_{\mathbb{R}}
. We denote here the imaginary unit by
I
because the algebra
A
sometimes is already a complex algebra where we just forget about its complex structure, so it may contain
i
as an element. In order to be more precise, we do not use the letter
i
in our construction.

We denote by
\sigma
the complex linear extension of
\sigma_{\mathbb{R}}
. We denote by
\bar{\sigma}
the complex antilinear extension of
\sigma_{\mathbb{R}}
.

We denote by
A_{\mathbb{H}}
the quaternionification of
A_{\mathbb{R}}
, i.e.
A_{\mathbb{H}}:=A_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{H}\{I,J,K\}
. By our convention form the previous Section
2.9
, we have
A\subset A_{\mathbb{H}}
.

We extend
\sigma
to
A_{\mathbb{H}}
quaternionic linearly, i.e.

\sigma_{0}:=\sigma(x)+J\sigma(y)=\sigma(x)+\sigma(\bar{y})J=\sigma(x)+\bar{\sigma}(y)J.

So
A^{\sigma_{0}}_{\mathbb{H}}=\Fix_{A_{\mathbb{H}}}(\sigma_{0})=A^{\sigma}\oplus A^{\bar{\sigma}}J
is well defined.

Every element of
z\in A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}}
can be uniquely written as
z=x+yJ
where
x\in A^{\sigma}
,
y\in A^{\bar{\sigma}}
. We denote by
\Ree(z):=x
,
\Imm(z):=y
. We also have a quaternionic conjugation on
A_{\mathbb{H}}
given by
\bar{z}=\bar{x}-J\bar{y}=\bar{x}-yJ
.

Definition 6.9
.

The
upper half-space
is

\mathfrak{U}:=\{z\in A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}}\mid\Imm(z)\in A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{+}\}

Proposition 6.10
.

The group
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts on
\mathfrak{U}
via
Möbius transformation

z\mapsto M.z=(az+b)(cz+d)^{-1}\text{, where }M=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}

transitively with the stabilizer of
1J
equal to
\KSp^{c}_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

In particular,
\mathfrak{U}
is a model for the symmetric space for
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

Proof.

First, we show that the action is well defined.
Since
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
is generated by matrices

\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&\sigma(a)^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1&b\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}

where
a\in A^{\times}
,
b\in A^{\sigma}
, we proof
M.z\in\mathfrak{U}
on these generators.

If
M:=\begin{pmatrix}1&b\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
with
b\in A^{\sigma}
, then
M.z=z+b\in A^{\sigma_{0}}_{\mathbb{H}}
and
\Imm(M.z)=\Imm(z)\in A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{+}
.

If
M:=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}
,
then
M.z=-z^{-1}\in A^{\sigma_{0}}_{\mathbb{H}}
. Let
z=x+yJ
, then it is easy to check that
z^{-1}=\bar{y}^{-1}\bar{x}\bar{b}-bJ,
where

b=\Imm(M.z)=(\bar{x}y^{-1}x+y)^{-1}
.

We need to check that
b\in A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{+}
. Since
y\in A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{+}
, we have
y^{-1}\in A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{+}
. Moreover,
\bar{x}y^{-1}x\in A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{\geq 0}
for any
x\in A^{\sigma}
. Because
A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{+}
is a convex cone,
\bar{x}y^{-1}x+y\in A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{+}
. Therefore,
b=(\bar{x}y^{-1}x+y)^{-1}\in A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{+}

If
M:=\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&\sigma(a)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

for
a\in A^{\times}
, then
M.z=az\sigma(a)\in A^{\sigma_{0}}_{\mathbb{H}}
. Further,
\Imm(M.z)=a\Imm(z)\sigma(a)\in A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{+}
because
A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{+}
is closed under action by congruence of
A^{\times}
.

Now, we check the transitivity.
Let
z=x+yJ\in\mathcal{S}
then
y=u^{2}
for some
u\in(A^{\bar{\sigma}})^{\times}
. Then

\pi\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&x\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}u&0\\
0&\sigma(u)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\right)=\pi\left(\begin{pmatrix}u&x\sigma(u)^{-1}\\
0&\sigma(u)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\right)=x+uJ\sigma(u)=

=x+u\bar{\sigma}(u)J=x+yJ=z

Finally, let us find the stabilizer of
1J
.
An element
M=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}
stabilizes
1J
if and only if

1J=M.1J=(aJ+b)(cJ+d)^{-1}=(aJ+b)(-\bar{c}+\bar{d}J)^{-1}J.

So,
a=\bar{d}
and
c=-\bar{b}
, i.e.
M\in\KSp_{2}^{c}(A,\sigma)
.
∎

6.3.
Projective model for
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)

Now we define the projective model of the symmetric space of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
. For this, we consider the following quaternionic extension of
A
:

A_{\mathbb{H}}:=\mathbb{H}[A,A_{\mathbb{R}},i,j]=A_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{H}\{i,j,k\}.

This space can be embedded into
\Mat_{2}(A)
as a subalgebra in the following way:

\begin{matrix}A_{\mathbb{H}}&\hookrightarrow&\Mat_{2}(A)\\
a_{1}+a_{2}j&\mapsto&\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}&a_{2}\\
-\bar{a}_{2}&\bar{a}_{1}\end{pmatrix}.\end{matrix}

The anti-involution
\bar{\sigma}^{T}
on
\Mat_{2}(A)
restricts to the following anti-involution on
A_{\mathbb{H}}
:

\sigma_{1}(a_{1}+a_{2}j):=\bar{\sigma}(a_{1})-\sigma(a_{2})j,

where
a_{1},a_{2}\in A
. Because
(A,\bar{\sigma})
is Hermitian, by the Proposition
2.78
,
(\Mat_{2}(A),\bar{\sigma}^{T})
is Hermitian and, therefore,
(A_{\mathbb{H}},\sigma_{1})
is Hermitian as well.

We denote:

A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{1}}:=\Fix_{A_{\mathbb{H}}}(\sigma_{1}),\;(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{1}})_{+}:=\theta_{\mathbb{H}}(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\times})

where

\begin{matrix}\theta_{\mathbb{H}}\colon&A_{\mathbb{H}}&\to&A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{1}}\\
&a&\mapsto&\sigma_{1}(a)a.\end{matrix}

We also consider the following anti-involution on
A_{\mathbb{H}}
:

\sigma_{0}(a_{1}+a_{2}j):=\sigma(a_{1})+\bar{\sigma}(a_{2})j,

where
a_{1},a_{2}\in A
and extend
\omega
in the following way:

\omega_{\mathbb{H}}(x,y):=\sigma_{0}(x)^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}y.

The following
\sigma_{1}
-sesquilinear form is an indefinite form on
A_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}
:

h(x,y):=\sigma_{1}(x)^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&j\\
-j&0\end{pmatrix}y.

Indeed,

h(y,x)=\sigma_{1}(y)^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&j\\
-j&0\end{pmatrix}x=\sigma_{1}\left(\sigma_{1}(x)^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&j\\
-j&0\end{pmatrix}y\right)=\sigma_{1}(h(x,y)).

Then in the basis
e_{1}:=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},\frac{j}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^{T}
,
e_{2}:=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},-\frac{j}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^{T}
, the form
h
is represented by the matrix
\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
, i.e.
h
is a
\sigma_{1}
-sesquilinear indefinite form on
A_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}
.

Proposition 6.11
.

\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts on
A_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}
preserving
h
. So we can see
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
as a subgroup of
\OO(h)
.

Proof.

Let
x,y\in A_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}
,
M\in\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
, then

h(Mx,My)=\sigma_{1}(Mx)^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&j\\
-j&0\end{pmatrix}My=\sigma_{1}(x)^{T}\bar{\sigma}(M)^{T}j\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}My=

=\sigma_{1}(x)^{T}j\sigma(M)^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}My=\sigma_{1}(x)^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&j\\
-j&0\end{pmatrix}y=h(x,y).

So
M
preserves
h
.
∎

Every quaternionic structure
J
on
A^{2}
can be extended additively to a quaternionic structure
J_{\mathbb{H}}
on
A^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}
in the following linear way:

J_{\mathbb{H}}(x(a+bj)):=J(x)(\bar{a}+\bar{b}j).

where
x\in A^{2}
,
a,b\in A
.

Proposition 6.12
.

For every quaternionic structure
J\in\mathfrak{C}
, there exist regular
x,y\in A^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}
such that
J_{\mathbb{H}}(x)=xj
,
J_{\mathbb{H}}(y)=-yj
. The elements
x,y
are uniquely defined up to multiplication by elements of
A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\times}
.

Proof.

Since
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts transitively on
\mathfrak{C}
, it is enough to prove the proposition for the standard quaternionic structure
J_{0}
.

Since

J_{0}(a_{1}+a_{2}j,b_{1}+b_{2}j)^{T}=(\bar{b}_{1}+\bar{b}_{2}j,-\bar{a}_{1}-\bar{a}_{2}j)^{T},

we obtain

(\bar{b}_{1}+\bar{b}_{2}j,-\bar{a}_{1}-\bar{a}_{2}j)^{T}=(a_{1}+a_{2}j,b_{1}+b_{2}j)^{T}j=(-a_{2}+a_{1}j,-b_{2}+b_{1}j)^{T}

if and only if
\bar{a}_{1}=b_{2}
,
\bar{a}_{2}=-b_{1}
, i.e.

x=(a_{1}+a_{2}j,-\bar{a}_{2}+\bar{a}_{1}j)^{T}=(a_{1}+a_{2}j,j(a_{1}+a_{2}j))^{T}=(1,j^{T})a,

where
a=a_{1}+a_{2}j\in A_{\mathbb{H}}
arbitrary element. The element
x
is regular if and only if
a\in A^{\times}_{\mathbb{H}}
.
Analogously,
y=(j,1)^{T}a
where
a=a_{1}+a_{2}j\in A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\times}
arbitrary element.
∎

For a quaternionic structure
J\in\mathfrak{C}
, we denote by
l_{J}
the
A_{\mathbb{H}}
-line
yA_{\mathbb{H}}
such that
J_{\mathbb{C}}(y)=-yj
.

We consider the spaces of isotropic elements and isotropic lines of
(A^{2}_{\mathbb{H}},\omega_{\mathbb{H}})
:

\Is(\omega_{\mathbb{H}}):=\{x\mid x\in A^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\text{ regular, }\omega_{\mathbb{H}}(x,x)=0\},

\mathbb{P}(\Is(\omega_{\mathbb{H}})):=\{xA\mid x\in\Is(\omega_{\mathbb{H}})\}.

We also consider the symmetric space of
\OO(h)
:

\mathcal{X}_{\OO(h)}:=\{xA\mid h(x,x)\in(A^{\sigma_{1}}_{\mathbb{H}})_{+}\}.

Definition 6.13
.

We call the space

\mathfrak{P}:=\mathcal{X}_{\OO(h)}\cap\mathbb{P}(\Is(\omega_{\mathbb{H}}))

the
projective model
.

To justify this Definition, we prove the following Proposition:

Proposition 6.14
.

The map

\begin{array}[]{cccl}F\colon&\mathfrak{C}&\to&\mathfrak{P}\\
&J&\mapsto&l_{J}\end{array}

defines is a homeomorphism that is equivariant under the action of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

In particular,
\mathfrak{P}
is a model of the symmetric space of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

Proof.

1. Show that
l_{J}\in\mathcal{X}_{\OO(h)}
. Since
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
acts transitively on
\mathfrak{C}
, it is enough to check it the standard quaternionic structure
J_{0}
:

h((j,1)^{T},(j,1)^{T})=\sigma_{1}(j,1)\begin{pmatrix}0&j\\
-j&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}j\\
1\end{pmatrix}=(-j,1)\begin{pmatrix}j\\
1\end{pmatrix}=2\in(A^{\sigma_{1}}_{\mathbb{H}})_{+}.

2. Show that
l_{J}\in\mathbb{P}(\Is(\omega))
. It is enough to prove it for
J_{0}
:

\omega((j,1)^{T},(j,1)^{T})=\sigma_{0}(j,1)\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}j\\
1\end{pmatrix}=(-1,j)\begin{pmatrix}j\\
1\end{pmatrix}=0.

3. Show that
F
is surjective. Let
v=u+wj\in A_{\mathbb{H}}
such that
vA_{\mathbb{H}}\in\mathfrak{P}
. Since
h(v,v)\in(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{1}})_{+}
, we can renormalize
v
so that
h(v,v)=2
. Since
v\in\Is(\omega)
,

0=\omega_{\mathbb{H}}(v,v)=\omega(u,u)+j\omega(w,w)j+\omega(u,w)j+j\omega(w,u)=

=\omega(u,u)-\overline{\omega(w,w)}+\left(\omega(u,w)+\overline{\omega(w,u)}\right)j

So we have:

\omega(u,u)=\overline{\omega(w,w)}

\omega(u,w)=-\overline{\omega(w,u)}.

Moreover,

2=h(v,v)=h(u+wj,u+wj)=h(u,u)-jh(w,w)j+h(u,w)j-jh(w,u).

Notice, for
u,w\in A^{2}
,
h(u,w)=\omega(\bar{u},\bar{w})j=j\omega(u,w)
. Therefore,

h(v,v)=\omega(\bar{u},\bar{u})j+\omega(w,w)j-\omega(\bar{u},\bar{w})+\omega(w,u).

=2\omega(w,u)+2\omega(w,w)j

So we have:

\omega(w,u)=1

\omega(u,u)=\omega(w,w)=0

It means that
(w,u)
is a symplectic basis of
(A^{2},\omega)
. We can define the following quaternionic structure:
J(u)=w
,
J(w)=-u
. By the Proposition
6.5
,
J\in\mathfrak{C}
. Since

J_{\mathbb{H}}(v)=J_{\mathbb{H}}(u+wj)=w-uj=-(u+wj)j=-vj,

we obtain
F(J)=vA
, i.e.
F
is surjective.

4. The map
F
is injective because if
l_{J}=l_{J^{\prime}}=yA
for
J,J^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
and some
y=y_{1}+y_{2}j\in A^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}
. Then
J(y_{1})=J^{\prime}(y_{1})=-y_{2}
,
J(y_{2})=J^{\prime}(y_{2})=y_{1}
and
(y_{1},y_{2})
is a basis of
A^{2}
, i.e.
J=J^{\prime}
.

5. Now, show the equivariance of
F
. Let
M\in\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
,
J\in\mathfrak{C}
and
u,w\in A^{2}
such that
w:=J(u)
,
J(w)=-u
. Then
MJM^{-1}(Mu)=Mw
,
MJM^{-1}(Mw)=-Mu
. That means that for
v=u+wj
,

F(MJM^{-1})=(Mv)A_{\mathbb{H}}=M(vA_{\mathbb{H}})=MF(J),

i.e.
F
is equivariant with respect to the
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
-action.
∎

6.4.
Precompact model for
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)

Now we define the precompact model of the symmetric space of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
. As we have seen in the Proposition
5.14
, the space
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{X}_{\OO(h_{st})}
for the standard
\sigma_{1}
-indefinite form on
A^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}
can be seen as a precompact domain

\mathring{D}(A_{\mathbb{H}},\sigma_{1})=\{c\in A_{\mathbb{H}}\mid 1-\sigma_{1}(c)c\in(A^{\sigma_{1}}_{\mathbb{H}})_{+}\}.

To see the symmetric space for
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
as a subset of this domain, we need an
A_{\mathbb{H}}
-linear transformation that maps
h
to the standard indefinite form. We can take the following matrix:

T:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&j\\
j&1\end{pmatrix}.

Then
\sigma_{1}(T)^{T}[h]T=\diag(-1,1)=[h_{st}]
and
T^{-1}\mathfrak{P}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
. Notice,
T\in\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{H}},\sigma)
, therefore it stabilizes the set of isotropic elements of
(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{2},\omega
).

Definition 6.15
.

The space

\mathfrak{B}:=\mathring{D}(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}},\sigma_{1}):=\mathring{D}(A_{\mathbb{H}},\sigma_{1})\cap A^{\sigma_{0}}_{\mathbb{H}}=\{c\in A^{\sigma_{0}}_{\mathbb{H}}\mid 1-\sigma_{1}(c)c\in(A^{\sigma_{1}}_{\mathbb{H}})_{+}\}

is called the
precompact model
.

To justify this Definition, we prove the following Proposition.

Proposition 6.16
.

The image of
T^{-1}\mathfrak{P}
under the homeomorphism
\Phi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathring{D}(A_{\mathbb{H}},\sigma_{1})
is

\mathring{D}(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}},\sigma_{1})
which is precompact in
A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}}
.

In particular,
\mathring{D}(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}},\sigma_{1})
is a model of the symmetric space of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

Proof.

To characterize the image of the symmetric space for
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
inside
\mathring{D}(A_{\mathbb{H}},\sigma_{1})
, we remind that
(x_{1},x_{2})^{T}\in\Is(\omega)
if and only if
\sigma_{0}(x_{1})x_{2}\in A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}}
. Therefore,
(c,1)^{T}
is isotropic if and only if
\sigma_{0}(c)\in A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}}
, i.e.
c\in A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}}
.

\Phi(T^{-1}\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime})=\{c\in A^{\sigma_{0}}_{\mathbb{H}}\mid 1-\sigma_{1}(c)c\in(A^{\sigma_{1}}_{\mathbb{H}})_{+}\}\subseteq A^{\sigma_{0}}_{\mathbb{H}}.

The domain
\mathring{D}(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}},\sigma_{1})
is precompact in
A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}}
because it is a subset of the following domain:

D(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}},\sigma_{1})=\{c\in A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}}\mid 1-\sigma_{1}(c)c\in(A^{\sigma_{1}}_{\mathbb{H}})_{\geq 0}\}\subseteq A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}}.

that is compact by Proposition
2.99
.
∎

Remark 6.17
.

The group
T^{-1}\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)T
acts on
\mathring{D}(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}},\sigma_{1})
by Möbius transformations.

6.5.
Connection between models

Consider a Hermitian algebra
(A_{\mathbb{R}},\sigma_{\mathbb{R}})
and its complexification
A=A_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
. In this section, we consider
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
-equivariant homeomorphisms between the projective model, the upper half-space model and precompact model of the symmetric space for
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

It is easy to check that the map:

\begin{matrix}F\colon&\mathfrak{P}&\to&\mathfrak{U}\\
&(x_{1},x_{2})A_{\mathbb{H}}&\mapsto&x_{1}x_{2}^{-1}\end{matrix}

is an
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
-equivariant homeomorphism.

As we have seen in the Proposition
6.16
, the map

\Phi\circ T^{-1}\colon\mathfrak{P}\to\mathring{D}(A^{\sigma_{0}}_{\mathbb{H}},\sigma_{1})=\{c\in A^{\sigma_{0}}_{\mathbb{H}}\mid 1-\sigma_{1}(c)c\in(A^{\sigma_{1}}_{\mathbb{H}})_{+}\}.

defines another
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
-equivariant homeomorphism.

The maps
F
and
\Phi\circ T^{-1}
can be seen as different coordinate charts for the projective model
\mathfrak{P}
of the symmetric space for
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

6.6.
Compactification and Shilov boundary

In this section, we construct a natural compactification of the symmetric space of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
.

Let
(A,\sigma)
be the complexification of a Hermitian algebra as before. The space

\mathring{D}(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}},\sigma_{1})=\{c\in A^{\sigma_{0}}_{\mathbb{H}}\mid 1-\sigma_{1}(c)c\in(A^{\sigma_{1}}_{\mathbb{H}})_{+}\}

is precompact. We take the topological closure of
\mathring{D}(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}},\sigma_{1})
in
A^{\sigma_{0}}_{\mathbb{H}}
:

D(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}},\sigma_{1}):=\{c\in A^{\sigma_{0}}_{\mathbb{H}}\mid 1-\sigma_{1}(c)c\in(A^{\sigma_{1}}_{\mathbb{H}})_{\geq 0}\}.

Definition 6.18
.

We call

\check{S}(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}},\sigma_{1}):=\{c\in A^{\sigma_{0}}_{\mathbb{H}}\mid 1-\sigma_{1}(c)c=0\}

Shilov boundary
of the precompact model
\mathring{D}(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}},\sigma_{1})
.

Note, that
\check{S}(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}},\sigma_{1})
is compact as a closed subspace of a compact space
D(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}},\sigma_{1})
.

Remark 6.19
.

The map
\Phi^{-1}
extends to the boundary of
D(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}},\sigma_{1})
and remains continuous and bijective. Since the boundary is compact, it is a homeomorphism. Therefore, we can see the boundary also in the projective model. In particular, we can see the Shilov boundary there.

The next Proposition describes the Shilov boundary in the projective model.

Proposition 6.20
.

The preimage of the Shilov boundary
\check{S}(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}},\sigma_{1})
in
\Is(\omega_{\mathbb{H}})
the map
\Phi\circ T^{-1}
gives a compact subset of the boundary of the projective model. It consists of all lines of the form
xA_{\mathbb{H}}
such that
x\in\Is(\omega)
.

Proof.

Note that the line
l\in\Is(\omega_{\mathbb{H}})
is of the form
xA_{\mathbb{H}}
for some
x\in\Is(\omega)
if and only if
\eta(l)=l
where
\eta\colon A_{\mathbb{H}}\to A_{\mathbb{H}}
the following involution

\eta(c_{1}+c_{2}j):=c_{1}-c_{2}j

for
c_{1},c_{2}\in A_{\mathbb{C}\{i\}}
. Notice,
\eta
is an involution on
A_{\mathbb{H}}
and

\sigma_{1}(\sigma_{0}(c_{1}+c_{2}j))=\bar{c}_{1}-\bar{c}_{2}j=-j\eta(c_{1}+c_{2}j)j.

Assume
c\in\check{S}(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}},\sigma_{1})
, i.e.
\sigma_{1}(c)^{-1}=c
,
\sigma_{0}(c)=c
. Then

(\Phi\circ T^{-1})\eta(T\circ\Phi^{-1}(c))=\Phi\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&j\\
j&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\eta(c)\\
1\end{pmatrix}\right)=\Phi\left(\begin{pmatrix}j\\
j\eta(c)\end{pmatrix}\right)=

=-j\eta(c)^{-1}j=\sigma_{1}(\sigma_{0}(c))^{-1}=\sigma_{1}(c)^{-1}=c

i.e. for
l=(c,1)^{T}A_{\mathbb{H}}
,
\eta(l)=l
.

If we take a line
xA_{\mathbb{H}}
for some
x=(x_{1},x_{2})^{T}\in\Is(\omega)
, then

c:=(\Phi\circ T^{-1})(xA)=(x_{1}-jx_{2})(-jx_{1}+x_{2})^{-1}.

Since
x\in\Is(\omega)\subset\Is(\omega_{\mathbb{H}})
,
c\in A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}}
. Further

\sigma_{1}(c)c=\sigma_{1}(\sigma_{0}(c))c=(\bar{x}_{1}+j\bar{x}_{2})(j\bar{x}_{1}+\bar{x}_{2})^{-1}(x_{1}-jx_{2})(-jx_{1}+x_{2})^{-1}=

=(\bar{x}_{1}+j\bar{x}_{2})(j\bar{x}_{1}+\bar{x}_{2})^{-1}(j\bar{x}_{1}+\bar{x}_{2})(-j)(-jx_{1}+x_{2})^{-1}=

=(\bar{x}_{1}+j\bar{x}_{2})(-j)(-jx_{1}+x_{2})^{-1}=(-jx_{1}+x_{2})(-jx_{1}+x_{2})^{-1}=1.

Therefore,
(\Phi\circ T^{-1})(xA)\in\check{S}(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}},\sigma_{1})
.
∎

Corollary 6.21
.

The space
\mathbb{P}(\Is(\omega))
of isotropic lines of
(A^{2},\omega)
embedded into
\mathbb{P}(\Is(\omega_{\mathbb{H}}))
as:

xA\mapsto xA_{\mathbb{H}}

is a Shilov boundary in the projective model. This is a closed (even compact) orbit of the action of
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
on the boundary of the projective model.

7.
Realizations of classical symmetric spaces

In this section we apply the general construction of the different models of the symmetric space associated to
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
to give explicit models for the symmetric spaces of the classical Lie groups that can be realized as
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
. This applies in particular to
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C})
,
\GL(n,\mathbb{C})
and
\OO(4n,\mathbb{C})
.

We construct explicit examples of models of symmetric space for classical Hermitian Lie groups of tube type. We will always denote by
A_{\mathbb{R}}
a real Hermitian algebra, the complexified algebra will be denoted by
A:=A_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
. The quaternionification of
A_{\mathbb{R}}
will be denoted by
A_{\mathbb{H}}
.

For the algebras
\Mat(n,\mathbb{R})
and
\Mat(n,\mathbb{C})
, we denote by
\sigma
the transposition. For
\Mat(n,\mathbb{C})
, we denote by
\bar{\sigma}
the composition of transposition and complex conjugation. For
\Mat(n,\mathbb{H}\{i,j,k\})
, we denote by
\sigma_{0}
the anti-involution acting in the following way:

\sigma_{0}(a+bj):=a^{T}+\bar{b}^{T}j,

and by
\sigma_{1}
the anti-involution acting in the following way:

\sigma_{1}(a+bj):=\bar{a}^{T}-b^{T}j

for
a,b\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})
. In particular, we use the same notation in the case
n=1
, i.e.
\bar{\sigma}
is the complex conjugation on
\mathbb{C}
.

To denote different models of the symmetric space for a group
G
that can be seen as
\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
for some reel or complex
A
and anti-involution
\sigma
, we use the following letters:
\mathfrak{U}(G)
for the upper half-space model,
\mathfrak{P}(G)
for the projective model,
\mathfrak{B}(G)
for the precompact model and
\mathfrak{C}(G)
for the complex/quaternionic structure model.

7.1.
Algebra
(\Mat(n,\mathbb{R}),\sigma)
and its complexification

Let
A_{\mathbb{R}}:=\Mat(n,\mathbb{R})
be the real algebra with the anti-involution
\sigma
given by transposition. Then

A=\Mat(n,\mathbb{R})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}=\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}),

\Sp_{2}(\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}),\sigma)=\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C}),\;\Sp_{2}(\Mat(n,\mathbb{R}),\sigma)=\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R}),

A_{\mathbb{H}}=\Mat(n,\mathbb{H}),\;A^{\sigma}=\Sym(n,\mathbb{C}),\;A^{\bar{\sigma}}_{+}=\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{C}),

\sigma_{0}(M_{1}+M_{2}j)=\sigma(M_{1})+\bar{\sigma}(M_{2})j=M_{1}^{T}+\bar{M}_{2}^{T}j.

\sigma_{1}(M_{1}+M_{2}j)=\bar{\sigma}(M_{1})-\sigma(M_{2})j=\bar{M}_{1}^{T}-M_{2}^{T}j.

where
M_{1},M_{2}\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{C})
.

A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}}=\{M_{1}+M_{2}j\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{H})\mid M_{1}\in\Sym(n,\mathbb{C}),\;M_{2}\in\Herm(n,\mathbb{C})\}.

Example 7.1
.

The
upper half-space model
of the symmetric space of

\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C})
is:

\mathfrak{U}(\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C}))=\{M_{1}+M_{2}J\mid M_{1}\in\Sym(n,\mathbb{C}),\;M_{2}\in\Herm^{+}(n)\}\subset

\subset\Mat(n,\mathbb{H}).

The Siegel upper half space of
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})
is the real locus of this space:

\mathfrak{U}(\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R}))=\{M_{1}+M_{2}J\mid M_{1}\in\Sym(n,\mathbb{R}),\;M_{2}\in\Sym^{+}(n,\mathbb{R})\}\subset

\subset\mathfrak{U}(\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C})).

Example 7.2
.

The
precompact model
of the symmetric space of
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C})
is :

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}(\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C}))=

\displaystyle=\{M_{1}+M_{2}j\in A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{0}}\mid\Id_{n}-(\bar{M}_{1}-\bar{M}_{2}j)(M_{1}+M_{2}j)\in\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{H})\}

The symmetric space for
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})
can be seen as the intersection of
\mathfrak{B}(\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C}))
with
\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{j\})
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}(\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R}))=

\displaystyle=\{M_{1}+M_{2}j\in\Sym(n,\mathbb{C}\{j\})\mid\Id_{n}-(M_{1}-M_{2}j)(M_{1}+M_{2}j)\in\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{C}\{j\})\}=

\displaystyle=\{M\in\Sym(n,\mathbb{C}\{j\})\mid\Id_{n}-\bar{M}M\in\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{C}\{j\})\}\subset\mathfrak{B}(\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C})).

Example 7.3
.

We consider
x,y\in A^{2}

\omega(x,y)=\sigma_{0}(x)^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}y,

h(x,y)=\sigma_{1}(x)^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}j\\
-\Id_{n}j&0\end{pmatrix}y.

Then the
projective model
of
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C})
is:

\mathfrak{P}(\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C}))=\{xA_{\mathbb{H}}\mid x\in A^{2}_{\mathbb{H}},\;\omega(x,x)=0,\;h(x,x)\in\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{H})\}.

The Shilov boundary corresponds in this model to the space:

\check{S}(\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C}))\cong\{xA_{\mathbb{H}}\mid x\in A^{2}_{\mathbb{H}},\;\omega(x,x)=h(x,x)=0\}\cong

\cong\{xA\mid x\in A^{2},\;\omega(x,x)=0\}.

The projective model for
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})
can be seen as:

\mathfrak{P}(\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R}))=\{xA_{\mathbb{C}\{j\}}\mid x\in A^{2}_{\mathbb{C}\{j\}},\;\omega(x,x)=0,\;h(x,x)\in\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{C}\{j\})\}

where
\mathbb{C}\{j\}\subset\mathbb{H}
and
A_{\mathbb{C}\{j\}}=A_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\{j\}\cong A
.
\mathfrak{P}(\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R}))
can be embedded into
\mathfrak{P}(\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C}))
using the following injective map: for
x\in\mathbb{C}\{j\}
,
xA_{\mathbb{C}\{j\}}\mapsto xA_{\mathbb{H}}
.
The Shilov boundary corresponds in this model to the space:

\check{S}(\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R}))\cong\{xA_{\mathbb{C}\{j\}}\mid x\in A^{2}_{\mathbb{R}},\;\omega(x,x)=h(x,x)=0\}\cong

\cong\{xA_{\mathbb{R}}\mid x\in A_{\mathbb{R}}^{2},\;\omega(x,x)=0\}.

We can also construct the projective model in terms of Lagrangians of
\mathbb{H}^{2n}
. Consider
\mathbb{H}^{2n}
as a right module over
\mathbb{H}
. We can identify a line
xA_{\mathbb{H}}
for a regular
x\in A_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}
with a
n
-dimensional submodule of
\mathbb{H}^{2n}
in the following way:

L(xA):=\Span_{\mathbb{H}}(xe_{1},\dots,xe_{n})\subset\mathbb{H}^{2n}

where
e_{i}
is the
i
-th basis vector (considered as a column) of the standard basis of
\mathbb{H}^{n}
. In fact, the map
L
is well-defined (does not depend on the choice of a regular
x\in xA
) and, moreover, it is a bijection.

We define two forms on
\mathbb{H}^{2n}
: for
u,v\in\mathbb{H}^{2n}
,

\tilde{\omega}(u,v):=\sigma_{0}(u)^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}v,

\tilde{h}(u,v):=\sigma_{1}(u)^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&j\Id_{n}\\
-j\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}v.

If we take
x\in\Is(\omega)
, then
L(xA)\in\Lag(\mathbb{H}^{2n},\tilde{\omega})
. Using the map
L
, we obtain the following projective model for
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C})\cong\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
:

\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}(\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C}))=\{l\in\Lag(\mathbb{H}^{2n},\tilde{\omega})\mid\forall v\in l\setminus\{0\},\;\tilde{h}(v,v)>0\}.

The Shilov boundary corresponds in this model to the space:

\check{S}(\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C}))\cong\{l\in\Lag(\mathbb{H}^{2n},\tilde{\omega})\mid\forall v\in l\setminus\{0\},\;\tilde{h}(v,v)=0\}\cong\Lag(\mathbb{C}^{2n},\tilde{\omega}).

The projective model for the symmetric space of
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})\cong\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{R}},\sigma_{\mathbb{R}})
is:

\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}(\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R}))=\{l\in\Lag(\mathbb{C}\{j\}^{2n},\tilde{\omega})\mid\forall v\in l\setminus\{0\},\;\tilde{h}(v,v)>0\}.

It can be embedded to the projective model of
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C})
by the map:

\begin{matrix}\Lag(\mathbb{C}\{j\}^{2n},\tilde{\omega})&\to&\Lag(\mathbb{H}^{2n},\tilde{\omega})\\
l&\mapsto&\Span_{\mathbb{H}}(l)\end{matrix}.

The Shilov boundary corresponds in this model to the space:

\check{S}(\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R}))\cong\{l\in\Lag(\mathbb{C}\{j\}^{2n},\tilde{\omega})\mid\forall v\in l\setminus\{0\},\;\tilde{h}(v,v)=0\}\cong\Lag(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\tilde{\omega}).

Example 7.4
.

Now we construct the quaternionic structures model and the complex structures model.
The quaternionic structure on
A
can be seen as a
2n\times 2n
-matrix
J
acting on
A^{2}
as
J(x)=J\bar{x}
for
x\in A^{2}
. Since
J(J(x))=J\overline{J\bar{x}}=-x
,
J\bar{J}=-\Id_{n}
.

The corresponding
\bar{\sigma}
-sesquilinear form is then

h_{J}(x,y)=\omega(J(x),y)=\bar{x}^{T}J^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}y.

So we obtain the quaternionic structure model for
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C}):

\mathfrak{C}(\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C})):=\left\{J\in\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})\mid J^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}\in\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{C}),\,J\bar{J}=-\Id\right\}.

The space of complex structures on
A_{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
can be seen as a subspace of
\mathfrak{C}(\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C}))
because every complex structure can be extended in the unique way to the quaternionic structure on
A^{2}
in the following way: for a complex structure
J
we define

J_{\mathbb{C}}(x+yi):=J(x)-J(y)i

where
x,y\in A_{\mathbb{R}}
. So we obtain the inclusion of the complex structure model for
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})
into the quaternionic model for
\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C})
as subspace of quaternionic structure fixing
A_{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\subset A^{2}
:

\mathfrak{C}(\Sp(2n,\mathbb{R})):=\left\{J\in\Mat(2n,\mathbb{R})\mid J^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}\in\Sym^{+}(n,\mathbb{R}),\,J^{2}=-\Id_{2n}\right\}=

=\{J\in\mathfrak{C}(\Sp(2n,\mathbb{C}))\mid J\in\Mat(2n,\mathbb{R})\}

7.2.
Algebra
(\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}),\bar{\sigma})
and its complexification

In this section, we consider the algebra
A_{\mathbb{R}}:=\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})
with the anti-involution
\bar{\sigma}
given by transposition and complex conjugation. Then

A=\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\{i\},

\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{R}},\bar{\sigma})=\UU(n,n),

\Sp_{2}(A,\bar{\sigma}\otimes\Id)=\GL(2n,\mathbb{C}).

A_{\mathbb{H}}=\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{H}\{i,j,k\}.

Example 7.5
.

First, we construct the upper half-space model. In the Section
B.1.1
, we studied the following
\mathbb{C}\{I\}
-algebras isomorphism:

\begin{matrix}\chi\colon&\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\{I\}&\to&\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\times\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\\
&a+bi&\mapsto&(a+bI,a-bI)\end{matrix}

where
a,b\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})
.

We have seen,

\chi(A_{\mathbb{R}})=\chi(\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\}))=\{(m,\bar{m})\mid m\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\},

\chi(A^{\bar{\sigma}\otimes\Id})=\{(m,m^{T})\mid m\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\}\cong\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}).

\chi(A^{\bar{\sigma}\otimes\bar{\sigma}})=\Herm(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\times\Herm(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\}),

\chi(A^{\bar{\sigma}\otimes\bar{\sigma}}_{+})=\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\times\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\}).

So we have the following model for the symmetric space for
\GL(2n,\mathbb{C})
:

\mathfrak{U}(\GL(2n,\mathbb{C}))=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}m_{11}\\
m^{T}_{11}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}m_{12}\\
m_{22}\end{pmatrix}J\left|\;\begin{matrix}m_{11}\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\}),\\
m_{12},m_{22}\in\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\end{matrix}\right.\right\}\subset

\subset\mathbb{H}[\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\times\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\}),\chi(\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})),(I,I),J].

Since
A_{\mathbb{R}}^{\sigma}=A_{\mathbb{R}}\cap A^{\bar{\sigma}\otimes\Id}=A_{\mathbb{R}}\cap A^{\bar{\sigma}\otimes\bar{\sigma}}=\Herm(n)
, we obtain the symmetric space for
\UU(n,n)
is:

\mathfrak{U}(\UU(n,n))\cong\left\{\begin{pmatrix}m_{1}\\
\bar{m}_{1}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}m_{2}\\
\bar{m}_{2}\end{pmatrix}j\,\middle|\,\begin{matrix}m_{1}\in\Herm(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\}),\\
m_{2}\in\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\end{matrix}\right\}\subset\mathfrak{U}(\GL(2n,\mathbb{C})).

To see
\mathfrak{U}(\UU(n,n))
as a subset of
\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\times\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})
, we have to identify
J
and
(I,I)=\chi(1\otimes I)
, so we get

\mathfrak{U}(\UU(n,n))=

=\left\{(m_{1}+m_{2}I,\bar{m}_{1}+\bar{m}_{2}I)\mid m_{1}\in\Herm(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\}),m_{2}\in\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\right\}\subset

\subset\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\times\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\}).

In a pair
(m_{1}+m_{2}I,\bar{m}_{1}+\bar{m}_{2}I)
for
m_{1}\in\Herm(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\}),m_{2}\in\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})
, the second component is completely determined by the first one. It is easy to see, because
m_{2}I
is skew-Hermitian and
m_{1}+m_{2}I
corresponds to the decomposition of an element from
\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})
in Hermitian and skew-Hermitian part. Therefore,
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
are well-defined by
m_{1}+m_{2}I
.
Therefore, we can identify

\mathfrak{U}(\UU(n,n))\cong\{m_{1}+m_{2}I\mid m_{1}\in\Herm(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\}),m_{2}\in\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\}.

Example 7.6
.

Now we construct the precompact model.
We use the map
\psi
from the Section
B.1.2
to identify
A_{\mathbb{H}}
with
\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})
.

\begin{matrix}\psi\colon&\Mat(n,\mathbb{H}\{i,j,k\})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\{I\}&\to&\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\\
&(q_{1}+q_{2}j)+(p_{1}+p_{2}j)I&\mapsto&\begin{pmatrix}q_{1}+p_{1}i&q_{2}+p_{2}i\\
-\bar{q}_{2}-\bar{p}_{2}i&\bar{q}_{1}+\bar{p}_{1}i\end{pmatrix}.\end{matrix}

where
q_{1},q_{2},p_{1},p_{2}\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})
.

The anti-involution
\bar{\sigma}\otimes\sigma_{0}
on
\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{H}\{i,j,k\}
induces the following anti-involution

\psi\circ(\bar{\sigma}\otimes\sigma_{0})\circ\psi^{-1}

on
\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})
:
m\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id\\
\Id&0\end{pmatrix}\bar{m}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id\\
\Id&0\end{pmatrix}
. Therefore,

\psi(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\bar{\sigma}\otimes\sigma_{0}})=\left\{m\in\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\,\middle|\,m=\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id\\
\Id&0\end{pmatrix}\bar{m}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id\\
\Id&0\end{pmatrix}\right\}.

Similarly, the anti-involution
\bar{\sigma}\otimes\sigma_{1}
on
\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{H}\{i,j,k\}
induces the following anti-involution

\psi\circ(\bar{\sigma}\otimes\sigma_{1})\circ\psi^{-1}

on
\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})
:
M\mapsto\bar{M}^{T}

and so
\psi(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\bar{\sigma}\otimes\sigma_{1}})=\Herm(2n,\mathbb{C})
. So we obtain the following precompact model for the symmetric space of
\GL(2n,\mathbb{C})
:

\mathfrak{B}(\GL(2n,\mathbb{C}))=\{M\in\psi(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\bar{\sigma}\otimes\sigma_{0}})\mid\Id_{2n}-\bar{M}^{T}M\in\Herm^{+}(2n,\mathbb{C})\}.

To see the precompact for
\UU(n,n)
as a subspace of
\mathfrak{B}(\GL(2n,\mathbb{C}))
, we have to intersect of with
\psi(\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\{j\})
. We remind from the Section
B.2.2

\psi(\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\{j\})=

=\left\{m\in\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\,\middle|\,m=-\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id\\
-\Id&0\end{pmatrix}m\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id\\
-\Id&0\end{pmatrix}\right\}.

Since

\psi(\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\{j\})\cap\psi(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\bar{\sigma}\otimes\sigma_{0}})=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
-b&a\end{pmatrix}\,\middle|\,a,b\in\Herm(n,\mathbb{C})\right\},

we obtain:

\mathfrak{B}(\UU(n,n))\cong\left\{\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
-b&a\end{pmatrix}\,\middle|\,\begin{pmatrix}\Id_{n}-a^{2}-b^{2}&ba-ab\\
ab-ba&\Id_{n}-a^{2}-b^{2}\end{pmatrix}\in\Herm^{+}(2n,\mathbb{C})\right\}\subset

\subset\mathfrak{B}(\GL(2n,\mathbb{C})).

Under the map
\chi
from the Section
B.1.1
,
A
can be identified with
\Mat(n,\mathbb{C})\times\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}),
so we obtain the following precompact model for
\UU(n,n)
:

\mathfrak{B}(\UU(n,n))=

=\{(M,M^{T})\mid M\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}),\;\Id_{n}-\bar{M}^{T}M\in\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{C}),\Id_{n}-\bar{M}M^{T}\in\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{C})\}.

The second component if the pair
(M,M^{T})
is determined by the first one. Moreover, if
\Id_{n}-\bar{M}^{T}M\in\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{C})
then
\Id_{n}-\bar{M}M^{T}\in\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{C})
. Therefore, we can identify:

\mathfrak{B}(\UU(n,n))=\{M\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{C})\mid\Id_{n}-\bar{M}^{T}M\in\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{C})\}.

Remark 7.7
.

The description for the precompact model of the symmetric space of
\UU(n,n)
seen as
\Sp_{2}(\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}),\bar{\sigma})
agree with the description for the projective model of the symmetric space of
\UU(n,n)
seen as
\OO(h_{st})
for
h_{st}
the standard indefinite form (see Section
5.3
).

Example 7.8
.

We now construct the projective model. Under
\psi
, the anti-involution
\bar{\sigma}\otimes\sigma_{0}
on
\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{H}\{i,j,k\}
induces the following anti-involution

\sigma^{\prime}:=\psi\circ(\bar{\sigma}\otimes\sigma_{0})\circ\psi^{-1}

on
\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})
:
M\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}\bar{M}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}
. Therefore,

\psi(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\bar{\sigma}\otimes\sigma_{0}})=\left\{M\in\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})\,\middle|\,\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}\bar{M}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}\right\}.

Similarly, the anti-involution
\bar{\sigma}\otimes\sigma_{1}
on
\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{H}\{i,j,k\}
induces the following anti-involution

\sigma^{\prime\prime}:=\psi\circ(\bar{\sigma}\otimes\sigma_{1})\circ\psi^{-1}

on
\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})
:
M\mapsto\bar{M}^{T}

and so
\psi(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\bar{\sigma}\otimes\sigma_{1}})=\Herm(2n,\mathbb{C})
.

Further, for
x,y\in(A^{\prime})^{2}

\omega(x,y)=\sigma^{\prime}(x)^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{2n}\\
-\Id_{2n}&0\end{pmatrix}y=

=\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}\bar{x}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\begin{matrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
\Id_{n}&0\end{matrix}&0\\
0&\begin{matrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
\Id_{n}&0\end{matrix}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\Id_{n}&0\\
0&0&0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0&0&0\\
0&-\Id_{n}&0&0\end{pmatrix}y=

=\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}\bar{x}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0&\Id_{n}\\
0&0&\Id_{n}&0\\
0&-\Id_{n}&0&0\\
-\Id_{n}&0&0&0\end{pmatrix}y

h(x,y)=\sigma^{\prime\prime}(x)^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{2n}i\\
-\Id_{2n}i&0\end{pmatrix}y=\bar{x}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{2n}i\\
-\Id_{2n}i&0\end{pmatrix}y

Note,
x\in\Is(\omega)
if and only if
x\in\Is(\omega^{\prime})
where

\omega^{\prime}(x,y)=\bar{x}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0&\Id_{n}\\
0&0&\Id_{n}&0\\
0&-\Id_{n}&0&0\\
-\Id_{n}&0&0&0\end{pmatrix}y

We obtain the projective model for
\GL(2n,\mathbb{C})
:

\mathfrak{P}(\GL(2n,\mathbb{C}))=\{xA^{\prime}\mid x\in(A^{\prime})^{2},\;\omega^{\prime}(x,x)=0,\;h(x,x)\in\Herm^{+}(2n,\mathbb{C})\}.

The Shilov boundary corresponds in this model to the space:

\check{S}(\GL(2n,\mathbb{C}))\cong\{xA^{\prime}\mid x\in(A^{\prime})^{2},\;\omega^{\prime}(x,x)=h(x,x)=0\}.

The projective model for
\UU(n,n)
can be seen as a subspace of
\mathfrak{P}(\GL(2n,\mathbb{C}))
in the following way. As we have seen in the Section
B.2.1
,

\psi(A_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\{j\})=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}q&p\\
-p&q\end{pmatrix}\,\middle|\,p,q\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\right\}=

=\left\{m\in\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\,\middle|\,m=-\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}m\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}\right\}.

Therefore, if we define

\delta(x):=-\begin{pmatrix}\begin{matrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{matrix}&0\\
0&\begin{matrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{matrix}\end{pmatrix}x\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}

for
x\in(A^{\prime})^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}.
We obtain

\mathfrak{P}(\UU(n,n))\cong\{xA^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{P}(\GL(2n,\mathbb{C}))\mid x\in(A^{\prime})^{2}_{\mathbb{H}},\;\delta(x)=x\}

We can also see the projective model for
\UU(n,n)
in another way. We consider the isomorphism
\chi
from the Section
B.1.1
identifying
\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\{i\}
with
\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\times\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})=:A^{\prime\prime}
. Then the induced by
\bar{\sigma}\otimes\Id
anti-involution

\chi\circ(\bar{\sigma}\otimes\Id)\circ\chi^{-1}

on
\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\times\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})
acts in the following way:

(m_{1},m_{2})\mapsto(m^{T}_{2},m^{T}_{1}).

The induced by
\bar{\sigma}\otimes\bar{\sigma}
involution

\chi\circ(\bar{\sigma}\otimes\bar{\sigma})\circ\chi^{-1}

on
\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\times\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})
acts in the following way:

(m_{1},m_{2})\mapsto(\bar{m}_{1}^{T},\bar{m}_{2}^{T}).

Note,

(A^{\prime\prime})^{2}=\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})^{2}\times\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})^{2}.

We take
x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})^{2}
, then we can define

\omega((x_{1},x_{2}),(y_{1},y_{2})):=

=\chi\circ(\bar{\sigma}\otimes\Id)\circ\chi^{-1}(x_{1},x_{2})\begin{pmatrix}0&(\Id_{n},\Id_{n})\\
-(\Id_{n},\Id_{n})&0\end{pmatrix}(y_{1},y_{2})=

=\left(x_{2}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}y_{1},x_{1}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}y_{2}\right),

h((x_{1},x_{2}),(y_{1},y_{2})):=\left(\bar{x}_{1}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}i\\
-\Id_{n}i&0\end{pmatrix}y_{1},\bar{x}_{2}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}i\\
-\Id_{n}i&0\end{pmatrix}y_{2}\right).

We obtain the projective model for
\UU(n,n)
:

\mathfrak{P}(\UU(n,n))=\left\{(x_{1},x_{2})A^{\prime\prime}\left|\begin{array}[]{l}x_{1},x_{2}\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})^{2},\;\hat{\omega}(x_{1},x_{2})=0,\\
\hat{h}(x_{1},x_{1}),\hat{h}(x_{2},x_{2})\in\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{C})\end{array}\right.\right\}

where
\hat{\omega}(x_{1},x_{2}):=x_{1}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}y_{2}
,
\hat{h}(x,y):=\bar{x}_{1}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}i\\
-\Id_{n}i&0\end{pmatrix}y_{1}
.
Since
\hat{\omega}
is non-degenerate, the line
x_{2}\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\}
is uniquely defined by
x_{1}
.

Let us check that for the pair
(x_{1},x_{2})
such that
\hat{\omega}(x_{1},x_{2})=0
,
\hat{h}(x_{1},x_{1})\in\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{C})
, we have always
\hat{h}(x_{2},x_{2})\in\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{C})
. As we have seen in the Section
5.6
, we can always choose
x_{1}=(m_{1},1)^{T}
,
x_{2}=(m_{2},1)^{T}
. Then

\hat{\omega}(x_{1},x_{2})=m_{1}^{T}-m_{2}=0,

\hat{h}(x_{1},x_{1})=i(\bar{m}_{1}^{T}-m_{1})\in\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{C}).

These two conditions imply

\hat{h}(x_{2},x_{2})=i(\bar{m}_{2}^{T}-m_{2})=i(\bar{m}_{1}-m_{1}^{T})=i(\bar{m}_{1}^{T}-m_{1})^{T}\in\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{C}).

Therefore, we can write the following identification:

\mathfrak{P}(\UU(n,n))\cong\left\{x\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\mid\hat{h}(x,x)\in\Herm^{+}(n,\mathbb{C})\right\}.

The Shilov boundary corresponds in this model to the space:

\check{S}(\UU(n,n))\cong\left\{x\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\mid\hat{h}(x,x)=0\right\}.

To construct the projective model in terms of Lagrangians, similarly to the Example
7.3
, we can identify the space of
A^{\prime}
-lines of
(A^{\prime})^{2}
with the space
\Gr(2n,\mathbb{C}^{4n})
of
2n
-dimensional subspaces of
\mathbb{C}^{4n}
by the rule:

L(xA^{\prime}):=\Span_{\mathbb{H}}(xe_{1},\dots,xe_{2n})

where
e_{i}
is the
i
-th basis vector (considered as a column) of the standard basis of
\mathbb{C}^{2n}
.

We define two forms on
\mathbb{C}^{4n}
: for
u,v\in\mathbb{C}^{4n}
,

\tilde{\omega}(u,v):=\bar{u}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0&\Id_{n}\\
0&0&\Id_{n}&0\\
0&-\Id_{n}&0&0\\
-\Id_{n}&0&0&0\end{pmatrix}v,

\tilde{h}(u,v):=\bar{u}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&i\Id_{2n}\\
-i\Id_{2n}&0\end{pmatrix}v.

The projective model for the symmetric space of
\GL(4n,\mathbb{C})\cong\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma)
can be seen as the following space:

\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}(\GL(4n,\mathbb{C}))=\{l\in\Lag(\mathbb{C}^{4n},\tilde{\omega})\mid\forall v\in l\setminus\{0\},\;\tilde{h}(v,v)>0\}.

where
\Lag(\mathbb{C}^{4n},\tilde{\omega})
is the space of all maximal isotropic subspaces of
\mathbb{C}^{4n}
with respect to
\tilde{\omega}
.
The Shilov boundary corresponds in this model to the space:

\check{S}(\GL(4n,\mathbb{C}))=\{l\in\Lag(\mathbb{C}^{4n},\tilde{\omega})\mid\forall v\in l\setminus\{0\},\;\tilde{h}(v,v)=0\}.

We can see the the projective model for the symmetric space of
\UU(n,n)\cong\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{R}},\sigma_{\mathbb{R}})
as a subspace of
\mathfrak{P}(\GL(4n,\mathbb{C}))
:

\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}(\UU(n,n))\cong\{l\in\mathfrak{P}(\GL(4n,\mathbb{C}))\mid\delta^{\prime}(l)=l\}

where

\begin{matrix}\delta^{\prime}\colon&\mathbb{C}^{4n}&\to&\mathbb{C}^{4n}\\
&v&\mapsto&\begin{pmatrix}\begin{matrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{matrix}&0\\
0&\begin{matrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{matrix}\end{pmatrix}v.\end{matrix}

We can also see another projective model for the symmetric space of
\UU(n,n)\cong\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{R}},\bar{\sigma})
if we identify again
A=\Mat(n,\mathbb{C})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
with
\Mat(n,\mathbb{C})\times\Mat(n,\mathbb{C})=:A^{\prime}
by the map
\chi
form the Section
B.1.1
.

\begin{matrix}\chi\colon&\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\{i\}&\to&\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\times\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\\
&a+bI&\mapsto&(a+bi,a-bi)\end{matrix}

As before, we can identify every line
xA^{\prime}\subset(A^{\prime})^{2}
with pair of
n
-dimensional subspaces of
\mathbb{C}^{2n}
. We define two forms on
\mathbb{C}^{2n}
: for
u,v\in\mathbb{C}^{2n}

\tilde{\omega}(u,v):=u^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}v,

\tilde{h}(u,v):=\bar{u}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}i\\
-\Id_{n}i&0\end{pmatrix}v.

The pair
(l_{1},l_{2})
of
n
-dimensional subspaces of
\mathbb{C}^{2n}
is called
\omega
-orthogonal if for all
v\in l_{1}
,
u\in l_{2}
,
\tilde{\omega}(v,u)=0
. So we can see the projective model of the symmetric space for
\UU(n,n)
:

\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}(\UU(n,n))=\{(l_{1},l_{2})\text{ $\tilde{\omega}$-orthogonal pair}\mid\forall u\in l_{1}\cup l_{2}\setminus\{0\},\;\tilde{h}(u,u)>0\}.

Since
\omega
is non-degenerate, the space
l_{2}
is completely determined by
l_{1}
. And as we have seen for
\mathfrak{P}(\UU(n,n))
, if for all
u\in l_{1}\setminus\{0\}
,
\tilde{h}(u,u)>0
, then for all
u\in l_{2}\setminus\{0\}
,
\tilde{h}(u,u)>0
. Therefore, we can identify

\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}(\UU(n,n))\cong\{l\in\Gr(n,\mathbb{C}^{2n})\mid\forall u\in l\setminus\{0\},\;\tilde{h}(u,u)>0\}.

The Shilov boundary corresponds in this model to the space:

\check{S}(\UU(n,n))\cong\{l\in\Gr(n,\mathbb{C}^{2n})\mid\forall u\in l\setminus\{0\},\;\tilde{h}(u,u)=0\}.

Remark 7.9
.

The description for the projective model of the symmetric space of
\UU(n,n)
seen as
\Sp_{2}(\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}),\bar{\sigma})
agree with the description for the projective model of the symmetric space of
\UU(n,n)
seen as
\OO(h_{st})
for
h_{st}
the standard indefinite form (see Section
5.3
).

Example 7.10
.

Now we define the quaternionic structures model and the complex structures model.
We use the map
\chi
from the Section
B.1.1
,

\begin{matrix}\chi\colon&\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\{i\}&\to&\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\times\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\\
&a+bI&\mapsto&(a+bi,a-bi)\end{matrix}

to identify
A
with
A^{\prime}:=\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\times\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})
. The involution
\Id\otimes\bar{\sigma}
is mapped under
\chi
to the involution

(m_{1},m_{2})\mapsto(\bar{m}_{2},\bar{m}_{1}).

on
\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\times\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})
.

If we take a quaternionic structure
J
on
A^{2}
then we define

J^{\prime}:=\chi\circ J\circ\chi^{-1}.

If we see
J^{\prime}
as a pair
(J_{1},J_{2})
of
2n\times 2n
complex matrices then
J_{1}\bar{J}_{2}=-\Id_{2n}
because for
(m_{1},m_{2})\in(A^{\prime})^{2}\cong\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})^{2}\times\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})^{2}
,

J^{\prime}(m_{1},m_{2})=(J_{1},J_{2})(\bar{m}_{2},\bar{m}_{1})=(J_{1}\bar{m}_{2},J_{2}\bar{m}_{1}),

-(m_{1},m_{2})=(J^{\prime})^{2}(m_{1},m_{2})=(J_{1}\overline{J_{2}\bar{m}_{1}},J_{2}\overline{J_{1}\bar{m}_{2}}).

The induced by
\bar{\sigma}\otimes\Id
anti-involution

\chi\circ(\bar{\sigma}\otimes\Id)\circ\chi^{-1}

on
\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\times\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})
acts in the following way:

(m_{1},m_{2})\mapsto(m^{T}_{2},m^{T}_{1}).

We take the standard symplectic structure on
(A^{\prime})^{2}
: for
x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})

\omega((x_{1},x_{2}),(y_{1},y_{2}))=\chi\circ(\bar{\sigma}\otimes\Id)\circ\chi^{-1}(x_{1},x_{2})\begin{pmatrix}0&(\Id_{n},\Id_{n})\\
-(\Id_{n},\Id_{n})&0\end{pmatrix}(y_{1},y_{2})=

=\left(x_{2}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}y_{1},x_{2}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}y_{1}\right).

For a quaternionic structure on
(A^{\prime})^{2}
seen as pair of matrices
(J_{1},J_{2})
, we define

h_{(J_{1},J_{2})}((x_{1},x_{2}),(y_{1},y_{2})):=

=\left((J_{1}\bar{x}_{1})^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}y_{1},(J_{2}\bar{x}_{2})^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}y_{2}\right)=

=\left(\bar{x}_{1}^{T}J_{1}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}y_{1},\bar{x}_{2}^{T}J_{2}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}y_{2}\right).

The quaternionic structure model for
\GL(2n,\mathbb{C})
is then:

\mathfrak{C}(\GL(2n,\mathbb{C})):=\left\{(J_{1},J_{2})\left|\begin{array}[]{l}J_{1},J_{2}\in\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C}),\,J_{1}\bar{J}_{2}=-\Id_{2n},\\
J_{1}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix},\,J_{2}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}\in\Herm^{+}(2n,\mathbb{C})\end{array}\right.\right\}.

Since
J_{1}\bar{J}_{2}=-\Id_{2n}
, by given
J_{1}
such that
J_{1}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}\in\Herm^{+}(2n,\mathbb{C})
, we can calculate
J_{2}=-\bar{J}_{1}^{-1}
. Then

J_{2}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}=-\bar{J}_{1}^{-T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}=\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}\bar{J}_{1}^{T}\right)^{-1}\in\Herm^{+}(2n,\mathbb{C})

if and only if

\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}\bar{J}_{1}^{T}\in\Herm^{+}(2n,\mathbb{C})

if and only if

\bar{J}_{1}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}\in\Herm^{+}(2n,\mathbb{C}).

Therefore, we can identify

\mathfrak{C}(\GL(2n,\mathbb{C}))\cong\left\{J\in\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})\,\middle|\,J^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}\in\Herm^{+}(2n,\mathbb{C})\right\}.

In this presentation of the symmetric space,
\GL(2n,\mathbb{C})
acts on it in the following way:

(g,J)\mapsto-g^{-1}J\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}\bar{g}^{-T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}

for
g\in\GL(2n,\mathbb{C})
.

Since

\chi(A_{\mathbb{R}})=\{(m,\bar{m})\mid m\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})\},

the quaternionic structure model for
\UU(n,n)\cong\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{R}},\bar{\sigma})
can be seen as a subset of
\mathfrak{C}(\GL(2n,\mathbb{C}))
stabilizing
\chi(A_{\mathbb{R}})
.
(J_{1},J_{2})\in\mathfrak{C}(\GL(2n,\mathbb{C}))
stabilizes
\chi(A_{\mathbb{R}})^{2}
if and only if for all
m\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{i\})^{2}
,

(J_{1},J_{2})(m,\bar{m})=(J_{1}(m),J_{2}(\bar{m}))=(m^{\prime},\bar{m}^{\prime}),

for some
m^{\prime}\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{C})^{2}
, i.e.
J_{1}=J_{2}
. Therefore,

\mathfrak{C}(\UU(n,n))\cong\{(J,J)\in\mathfrak{C}(\GL(2n,\mathbb{C}))\}.

We can also see
\mathfrak{C}(\UU(n,n))
directly as the space complex structures on
A_{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
:

\mathfrak{C}(\UU(n,n))=\left\{J\in\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})\,\middle|\,\bar{J}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}\in\Herm^{+}(2n,\mathbb{C}),J\bar{J}=-\Id_{2n}\right\}.

7.3.
Algebra
\Mat(n,\mathbb{H})
and its complexification

In this section, we consider the algebra
A_{\mathbb{R}}:=\Mat(n,\mathbb{H}\{I,J,K\})
with the anti-involution
\sigma_{1}
given by transposition and quaternionic conjugation. Then

A=\Mat(n,\mathbb{H}\{I,J,K\})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\{i\},

\Sp_{2}(A_{\mathbb{R}},\sigma_{1})=\SO^{*}(4n),

\Sp_{2}(A,\sigma_{1}\otimes\Id)=\OO(4n,\mathbb{C}),

A_{\mathbb{H}}=\Mat(n,\mathbb{H}\{I,J,K\})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{H}\{i,j,k\}.

Example 7.11
.

First, we construct the upper half-space model. In the Section
B.1.2
, we studied the following
\mathbb{C}\{i\}
-
\mathbb{C}\{I\}
-algebras isomorphism:

\begin{matrix}\psi\colon&\Mat(n,\mathbb{H}\{I,J,K\})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\{i\}&\to&\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\\
&(q_{1}+q_{2}J)+(p_{1}+p_{2}J)i&\mapsto&\begin{pmatrix}q_{1}+p_{1}I&q_{2}+p_{2}I\\
-\bar{q}_{2}-\bar{p}_{2}I&\bar{q}_{1}+\bar{p}_{1}I\end{pmatrix}.\end{matrix}

where
q_{1},q_{2},p_{1},p_{2}\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})
.

We remind,

\psi(\Id_{n}\otimes i)=\Id_{2n}I
and

\psi(A_{\mathbb{R}})=\psi(\Mat(n,\mathbb{H}\{I,J,K\}))=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}q_{1}&q_{2}\\
-\bar{q}_{2}&\bar{q}_{1}\end{pmatrix}\,\middle|\,q_{1},q_{2}\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{C})\right\}.

Under
\psi
, the anti-involution
\sigma_{1}\otimes\Id
on
\Mat(n,\mathbb{H}\{I,J,K\})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\{i\}
indices the following anti-involution

\sigma^{\prime}:=\psi\circ(\sigma_{1}\otimes\Id)\circ\psi^{-1}

on
\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})=\Mat_{2}(\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\}))
:

\sigma^{\prime}(m)=-\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}m^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}

for
m\in\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})
. So we have:

\psi(A^{\sigma_{1}\otimes\Id})=\left\{m\in\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\,\middle|\,m=-\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}m^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}\right\}=

=\spp(2n,\mathbb{C}\{I\}).

The anti-involution
\sigma_{1}\otimes\bar{\sigma}
on
\Mat(n,\mathbb{H}\{I,J,K\})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\{i\}
indices the following anti-involution

\tilde{\sigma}=\psi\circ(\sigma_{1}\otimes\bar{\sigma})\circ\psi^{-1}

on
\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})
:

\tilde{\sigma}(m)=\bar{m}^{T}.

So, as expected,
(\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C}\{I\}),\tilde{\sigma})
is a Hermitian algebra and

\psi(A_{+}^{\sigma_{1}\otimes\bar{\sigma}})=\Herm^{+}(2n).

Since
\psi(1\otimes i)=\Id_{2n}I
, we have to do quaternionification with respect to
\Id I
. So the symmetric space is:

\mathfrak{U}(\OO(4n,\mathbb{C}))=\{M_{1}+M_{2}j\mid M_{1}\in\spp(2n,\mathbb{C}),\;M_{2}\in\Herm^{+}(2n)\}\subset

\subset\mathbb{H}[\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C}\{I\}),\psi(\Mat(n,\mathbb{H}\{I,J,K\})),\Id_{2n}I,j].

Since
A_{\mathbb{R}}^{\sigma}=A^{\bar{\sigma}\otimes\Id}\cap A^{\bar{\sigma}\otimes\bar{\sigma}}
, the real locus of this space is the symmetric space of
\SO^{*}(4n)
:

\mathfrak{U}(\SO^{*}(4n))\cong

=\{M_{1}+M_{2}j\mid M_{1}\in\spp(2n,\mathbb{C})\cap\Herm(2n),\;M_{2}\in\spp(2n,\mathbb{C})\cap\Herm^{+}(2n)\}\subset

\subset\mathfrak{U}(\OO(4n,\mathbb{C})).

After identification
j
and
\Id_{2n}I
, we obtain it as a subset of
\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})
:

\mathfrak{U}(\SO^{*}(4n))=

=\{M_{1}+M_{2}I\mid M_{1}\in\spp(2n,\mathbb{C})\cap\Herm(2n),\;M_{2}\in\spp(2n,\mathbb{C})\cap\Herm^{+}(2n)\}\subset

\subset\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C}\{I\}).

Example 7.12
.

Now we construct the precompact model. We use the map

\phi\colon\Mat(n,\mathbb{H}\{I,J,K\})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{H}\{i,j,k\}\to\Mat(4n,\mathbb{R})

from the Section
B.1.3
to identify
A_{\mathbb{H}}
with
\Mat(4n,\mathbb{R})

defined on generators of
A_{\mathbb{H}}
as follows:

\phi(a\otimes i)=\begin{pmatrix}0&a&0&0\\
-a&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&-a\\
0&0&a&0\end{pmatrix},\;\phi(a\otimes j)=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&a&0\\
0&0&0&a\\
-a&0&0&0\\
0&-a&0&0\end{pmatrix},

\phi(a\otimes k)=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0&a\\
0&0&-a&0\\
0&a&0&0\\
-a&0&0&0\end{pmatrix},\;\phi(aI\otimes 1)=\begin{pmatrix}0&-a&0&0\\
a&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&-a\\
0&0&a&0\end{pmatrix},

\phi(aJ\otimes 1)=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&-a&0\\
0&0&0&a\\
a&0&0&0\\
0&-a&0&0\end{pmatrix},\;\phi(aK\otimes 1)=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0&-a\\
0&0&-a&0\\
0&a&0&0\\
a&0&0&0\end{pmatrix}

where
a\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{R})
.

As we have seen, the anti-involution
\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{0}
corresponds under
\phi
to the following anti-involution on
\Mat(4n,\mathbb{R})
:
M\mapsto-\Xi M^{T}\Xi
where

\Xi:=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0&\Id_{n}\\
0&0&-\Id_{n}&0\\
0&\Id_{n}&0&0\\
-\Id_{n}&0&0&0\end{pmatrix}.

The anti-involution
\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{1}
corresponds under
\phi
to the transposition on
\Mat(4n,\mathbb{R})
. So we obtain the following precompact model of the symmetric space of
\OO(4n,\mathbb{C})
:

\mathfrak{B}(\OO(4n,\mathbb{C}))=\{M\in\phi(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{0}})\mid 1-M^{T}M\in\Sym^{+}(4n,\mathbb{R})\}

where

\phi(A_{\mathbb{H}}^{\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{0}})=\{M\in\Mat(4n,\mathbb{R})\mid M=-\Xi M^{T}\Xi\}\cong\spp(4n,\mathbb{R}).

To see the precompact model
\mathfrak{B}(\SO^{*}(4n))
for the symmetric space of
\SO^{*}(4n)
as a subspace of
\mathfrak{B}(\OO(4n,\mathbb{C}))
, we have to intersect
\mathfrak{B}(\OO(4n,\mathbb{C}))
with
\phi(\Mat(n,\mathbb{H}\{I,J,K\})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\{j\})
. We remind from the Section
B.2.2
:

\phi(\Mat(n,\mathbb{H}\{I,J,K\})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\{j\})=\left\{m\in\Mat(4n,\mathbb{R})\mid M=-\phi(\Id_{n}\otimes j)M\phi(\Id_{n}\otimes j)\right\}.

Therefore, we obtain:

\mathfrak{B}(\SO^{*}(4n))\cong\left\{M\in\mathfrak{B}(\OO(4n,\mathbb{C}))\mid M=-\phi(\Id_{n}\otimes j)M\phi(\Id_{n}\otimes j)\right\}.

Under the map
\psi
from the Section
B.1.2
, we can identify
A
with
\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})
.
The anti-involution
\sigma_{1}\otimes\Id
corresponds to the following anti-involution on
\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})
:

m\mapsto-\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id\\
-\Id&0\end{pmatrix}m^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id\\
-\Id&0\end{pmatrix}.

Therefore,
\psi(A^{\sigma_{1}\otimes\Id})=\spp(2n,\mathbb{C})
.

The anti-involution
\sigma_{1}\otimes\bar{\sigma}
corresponds to the following anti-involution on
\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})
:
M\mapsto\bar{M}^{T}
. Therefore, we obtain the precompact model for
\SO^{*}(4n)
:

\mathfrak{B}(\SO^{*}(4n))=\{M\in\spp(2n,\mathbb{C})\mid 1-\bar{M}^{T}M\in\Herm^{+}(2n,\mathbb{C})\}.

Example 7.13
.

Now we construct the projective model. As we have seen, the map
\phi
defines an
\mathbb{R}
-algebra isomorphism:

\phi\colon A_{\mathbb{H}}\to\Mat(4n,\mathbb{R})=:A^{\prime}.

Moreover, the anti-involution
\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{0}
corresponds under
\psi
to the following anti-involution
\sigma^{\prime}_{0}
on
\Mat(4n,\mathbb{R})
:

\sigma^{\prime}_{0}(M)=-\Xi M^{T}\Xi
where

\Xi:=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0&\Id_{n}\\
0&0&-\Id_{n}&0\\
0&\Id_{n}&0&0\\
-\Id_{n}&0&0&0\end{pmatrix}.

The anti-involution
\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{1}
corresponds under
\phi
to the transposition on
\Mat(4n,\mathbb{R})
.

Further, for
x,y\in(A^{\prime})^{2}

\omega(x,y)=\sigma^{\prime}_{0}(x)^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{4n}\\
-\Id_{4n}&0\end{pmatrix}y=

=-\Xi x^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\Xi&0\\
0&\Xi\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\Id_{2n}&0\\
0&0&0&\Id_{2n}\\
-\Id_{2n}&0&0&0\\
0&-\Id_{2n}&0&0\end{pmatrix}y=

=-\Xi x^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0&0&0&0&0&\Id_{n}\\
0&0&0&0&0&0&-\Id_{n}&0\\
0&0&0&0&0&\Id_{n}&0&0\\
0&0&0&0&-\Id_{n}&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&-\Id_{n}&0&0&0&0\\
0&0&\Id_{n}&0&0&0&0&0\\
0&-\Id_{n}&0&0&0&0&0&0\\
\Id_{n}&0&0&0&0&0&0&0\end{pmatrix}y

h(x,y)=x^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0&0&0&0&\Id_{n}&0\\
0&0&0&0&0&0&0&\Id_{n}\\
0&0&0&0&-\Id_{n}&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&0&0&-\Id_{n}&0&0\\
0&0&-\Id_{n}&0&0&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&-\Id_{n}&0&0&0&0\\
\Id_{n}&0&0&0&0&0&0&0\\
0&\Id_{n}&0&0&0&0&0&0\end{pmatrix}y.

By definition of
h
, we use that

\phi(\Id_{n}\otimes j)=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\Id_{n}&0\\
0&0&0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0&0&0\\
0&-\Id_{n}&0&0\end{pmatrix}.

Note,
x\in\Is(\omega)
if and only if
x\in\Is(\omega^{\prime})
where

\omega^{\prime}(x,y)=x^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0&0&0&0&0&\Id_{n}\\
0&0&0&0&0&0&-\Id_{n}&0\\
0&0&0&0&0&\Id_{n}&0&0\\
0&0&0&0&-\Id_{n}&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&-\Id_{n}&0&0&0&0\\
0&0&\Id_{n}&0&0&0&0&0\\
0&-\Id_{n}&0&0&0&0&0&0\\
\Id_{n}&0&0&0&0&0&0&0\end{pmatrix}y.

So we obtain the projective model for the symmetric space of
\OO(4n,\mathbb{C})
:

\mathfrak{P}(\OO(4n,\mathbb{C}))=\{xA^{\prime}\mid x\in(A^{\prime})^{2},\;\omega^{\prime}(x,x)=0,\;h(x,x)\in\Sym^{+}(4n,\mathbb{R})\}.

We can see the Shilov boundary in this model as the space:

\check{S}(\OO(4n,\mathbb{C}))\cong\{xA^{\prime}\mid x\in(A^{\prime})^{2},\;\omega^{\prime}(x,x)=h(x,x)=0\}.

The projective model for
\SO^{*}(4n)
can be seen as a subspace of
\mathfrak{P}(\OO(4n,\mathbb{C}))
in the following way. As we have seen in the Section
B.2.2
,

\psi(A_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\{j\})=\left\{m\in\Mat(4n,\mathbb{R})\mid m=-\phi(1\otimes j)m\phi(1\otimes j)\right\}.

Therefore,

\mathfrak{P}(\SO^{*}(4n))\cong\{xA^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{P}(\GL(2n,\mathbb{C}))\mid x=-\phi(1\otimes j)x\phi(1\otimes j)\}.

To see another projective model for the symmetric space of
\SO^{*}(4n)
, we remind that
A=\Mat(n,\mathbb{H})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
is to
\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})=:A^{\prime\prime}
isomorphic under the map
\psi
from the Section
B.1.2
. The anti-involution
\sigma_{1}\otimes\Id
corresponds under this map to the anti-involution
\sigma^{\prime}
on
\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})
given by:

\sigma^{\prime}(m)=-\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}m^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}.

The anti-involution
\sigma_{1}\otimes\bar{\sigma}
corresponds under
\psi
to the complex conjugation composed with transposition on
\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})
.

We define for
x,y\in(A^{\prime\prime})^{2}
,

\omega(x,y):=x^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\begin{matrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{matrix}&0\\
0&\begin{matrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{matrix}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{2n}\\
-\Id_{2n}&0\end{pmatrix}y=

=x^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0&\Id_{n}\\
0&0&-\Id_{n}&0\\
0&-\Id_{n}&0&0\\
\Id_{n}&0&0&0\end{pmatrix}y,

\tilde{h}(x,y):=\bar{x}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{2n}i\\
-\Id_{2n}i&0\end{pmatrix}y.

Then the projective model of the symmetric space for
\SO^{*}(4n)
can be seen as:

\mathfrak{P}(\SO^{*}(4n))=\{xA^{\prime\prime}\mid x\in(A^{\prime\prime})^{2},\tilde{h}(x,x)\in\Herm^{+}(2n,\mathbb{C})\}.

We can see the Shilov boundary in this model as the space:

\check{S}(\SO^{*}(4n))\cong\{xA^{\prime\prime}\mid x\in(A^{\prime\prime})^{2},\tilde{h}(x,x)=0\}.

Now we construct the projective model in terms of isotropic subspaces. As before, we identify using the map
L
the space of
A^{\prime}
-lines and the space
\Gr(4n,\mathbb{R}^{8n})
of
4n
-dimensional subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{8n}
:

L(xA^{\prime}):=\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(xe_{1},\dots,xe_{4n})

where
e_{i}
is the
i
-th standard basis vector of
\mathbb{R}^{4n}
. We define two forms on
\mathbb{R}^{8n}
: for
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{8n}
,

\tilde{\omega}(u,v):=u^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0&0&0&0&0&\Id_{n}\\
0&0&0&0&0&0&-\Id_{n}&0\\
0&0&0&0&0&\Id_{n}&0&0\\
0&0&0&0&-\Id_{n}&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&-\Id_{n}&0&0&0&0\\
0&0&\Id_{n}&0&0&0&0&0\\
0&-\Id_{n}&0&0&0&0&0&0\\
\Id_{n}&0&0&0&0&0&0&0\end{pmatrix}v,

\tilde{h}(u,v):=u^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0&0&0&0&\Id_{n}&0\\
0&0&0&0&0&0&0&\Id_{n}\\
0&0&0&0&-\Id_{n}&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&0&0&-\Id_{n}&0&0\\
0&0&-\Id_{n}&0&0&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&-\Id_{n}&0&0&0&0\\
\Id_{n}&0&0&0&0&0&0&0\\
0&\Id_{n}&0&0&0&0&0&0\end{pmatrix}v.

The space of
\tilde{\omega}
-isotropic vectors of
\mathbb{R}^{8n}
is denoted by
\Is(\tilde{\omega})
. Then the projective model of the symmetric space for
\OO(4n,\mathbb{C})
can be seen as:

\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}(\OO(4n,\mathbb{C}))=\{l\in\Lag(\mathbb{R}^{8n},\tilde{\omega})\mid\forall x\in l\setminus\{0\},\tilde{h}(x,x)>0\}

where
\Lag(\mathbb{R}^{8n},\tilde{\omega})
is the space of all maximal
\tilde{\omega}
-isotropic subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{8n}
.

We can see the Shilov boundary in this model as the space:

\check{S}(\OO(4n,\mathbb{C}))\cong\{l\in\Lag(\mathbb{R}^{8n},\tilde{\omega})\mid\forall x\in l\setminus\{0\},\tilde{h}(x,x)=0\}.

The projective model for the symmetric space of
\SO^{*}(4n)
can be seen as a subspace of
\mathfrak{P}(\OO(4n,\mathbb{C}))
:

\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}(\SO^{*}(4n))=\{l\in\mathfrak{P}(\OO(4n,\mathbb{C}))\mid\delta(l)=l\},

where

\begin{matrix}\delta\colon&\mathbb{R}^{8n}&\to&\mathbb{R}^{8n}\\
&v&\mapsto&\begin{pmatrix}\phi(\Id_{n}\otimes j)&0\\
0&\phi(\Id_{n}\otimes j)\end{pmatrix}v.\end{matrix}

To see another projective model for the symmetric space of
\SO^{*}(4n)
, we remind that
A=\Mat(n,\mathbb{H})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
is to
\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})=:A^{\prime\prime}
isomorphic under the map
\psi
from the Section
B.1.2
. The anti-involution
\sigma_{1}\otimes\Id
corresponds under this map to the anti-involution
\sigma^{\prime}
on
\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})
given by:

\sigma^{\prime}(m)=-\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}m^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}.

The anti-involution
\sigma_{1}\otimes\bar{\sigma}
corresponds under
\psi
to the complex conjugation composed with transposition on
\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})
.

To construct the projective model in terms of Lagrangians, as before, we identify using the map
L
the space of
A^{\prime\prime}
-lines and the space
\Gr(2n,\mathbb{C}^{4n})
of
2n
-dimensional subspaces of
\mathbb{C}^{4n}
:

L(xA^{\prime\prime}):=\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(xe_{1},\dots,xe_{2n})

where
e_{i}
is the
i
-th standard basis vector of
\mathbb{C}^{2n}
. We define two forms on
\mathbb{R}^{8n}
: for
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{8n}
,

\tilde{\omega}(u,v):=u^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\begin{matrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{matrix}&0\\
0&\begin{matrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{matrix}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{2n}\\
-\Id_{2n}&0\end{pmatrix}v=

=u^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0&\Id_{n}\\
0&0&-\Id_{n}&0\\
0&-\Id_{n}&0&0\\
\Id_{n}&0&0&0\end{pmatrix}v,

\tilde{h}(u,v):=\bar{u}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}i\\
-\Id_{n}i&0\end{pmatrix}v.

Then the projective model of the symmetric space for
\SO^{*}(4n)
can be seen as:

\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}(\SO^{*}(4n))=\{l\in\Lag(\mathbb{C}^{4n},\tilde{\omega})\mid\forall x\in l\setminus\{0\},\tilde{h}(x,x)>0\}

where
\Lag(\mathbb{C}^{4n},\tilde{\omega})
is the space of all maximal
\tilde{\omega}
-isotropic subspaces of
\mathbb{C}^{4n}
.

We can see the Shilov boundary in this model as the space:

\check{S}(\SO^{*}(4n))\cong\{l\in\Lag(\mathbb{C}^{4n},\tilde{\omega})\mid\forall x\in l\setminus\{0\},\tilde{h}(x,x)=0\}.

Example 7.14
.

Now we construct the quaternionic structures model and the complex structures model.

We use the map
\psi
from the Section
B.1.2
, to identify
A
with
A^{\prime}:=\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})
.

\begin{matrix}\psi\colon&\Mat(n,\mathbb{H}\{I,J,K\})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\{i\}&\to&\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})\\
&(q_{1}+q_{2}J)+(p_{1}+p_{2}J)i&\mapsto&\begin{pmatrix}q_{1}+p_{1}I&q_{2}+p_{2}I\\
-\bar{q}_{2}-\bar{p}_{2}I&\bar{q}_{1}+\bar{p}_{1}I\end{pmatrix}.\end{matrix}

where
q_{1},q_{2},p_{1},p_{2}\in\Mat(n,\mathbb{C}\{I\})
.

The induced by
\Id\otimes\bar{\sigma}
involution

\sigma^{\prime}:=\psi\circ(\Id\otimes\bar{\sigma})\circ\psi^{-1}

on
\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})
acts in the following way:

m\mapsto-\Omega\bar{m}\Omega

where
\Omega=\begin{pmatrix}0&\Id_{n}\\
-\Id_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}\in\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})
. We also denote by
\Omega_{0}:=\diag(\Omega,\Omega)\in\Mat(4n,\mathbb{C})
.

If we take a quaternionic structure
Q
on
A^{2}
then we define

Q^{\prime}:=\psi\circ Q\circ\psi^{-1}.

We can see
Q^{\prime}
as a complex
4n\times 4n
-matrix acting on
(A^{\prime})^{2}
in the following way: for
x\in(A^{\prime})^{2}
,

Q^{\prime}(x):=Q^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}(x)=-Q^{\prime}\Omega_{0}\bar{x}\Omega_{0}.

Q^{\prime}
is a quaternionic structure, therefore,

-x=(Q^{\prime})^{2}(x)=Q^{\prime}\Omega_{0}\overline{Q^{\prime}\Omega_{0}\bar{x}\Omega_{0}}\Omega_{0}=-Q^{\prime}\Omega_{0}\bar{Q}^{\prime}\Omega_{0}x.

So we obtain,
Q^{\prime}
is a quaternionic structure on
A
if and only if

Q^{\prime}\Omega_{0}\bar{Q}^{\prime}\Omega_{0}=\Id_{4n}.

The induced by
\sigma_{1}\otimes\Id
anti-involution

\psi\circ(\sigma_{1}\otimes\Id)\circ\psi^{-1}

on
\Mat(2n,\mathbb{C})
acts in the following way:

m\mapsto-\Omega m^{T}\Omega.

So we define the standard symplectic form
\omega
on
(A^{\prime})^{2}
with respect to this anti-involution: for
